# RECORD FINDINGS REGISTER

**Purpose:**  
This document captures the outputs produced by applying `HOW-TO-READ-COMPLETE-RECORD.md` to `complete-record.md`.

It is not itself a clinical opinion, legal opinion, complaint, or advocacy document. Its purpose is to separate:

- facts within the record;
- facts about the record;
- interpretive consequences;
- unresolved propositions;
- decision points; and
- questions requiring external clinical or other expert judgment.

The register should remain auditable. Each finding should be traceable back to the underlying record and should distinguish contemporaneous evidence from later reconstruction.

---

## 1. How to Use This Register

For each material issue:

1. Identify the **case fact**: what happened, was reported, observed, prescribed, tested, referred, or documented.
2. Identify any **record fact**: a fact about timing, framing, authorship, referral wording, repetition, inheritance, observation conditions, retrospective reconstruction, or evidential development.
3. State the **interpretive consequence**: what the record fact changes about what the case fact can safely prove.
4. Record any **counterevidence or competing evidence**.
5. State what remains **unresolved**.
6. Identify any **decision point** at which the new evidence could have altered assessment, investigation, referral, treatment, or communication.
7. Where appropriate, formulate an **expert question** rather than reaching an expert conclusion.

Do not convert absence of documentation into proof that something did not occur.

Do not treat a later explanation as proof that the same rationale existed contemporaneously.

Do not treat treatment response alone as proof of mechanism.

Do not collapse structural pathology, neurological motor phenomena, pain generation, pain modulation, and functional consequences into a single proposition.

---

## 2. Status Vocabulary

Use one of the following labels where useful:

- **Established documentary fact** — directly supported by the record.
- **Supported proposition** — supported by multiple or converging entries but still interpretive.
- **Documentary tension** — two or more record elements cannot safely be reduced to one simple account.
- **Retrospective reconstruction** — a later account attributes meaning, rationale, knowledge, or continuity to an earlier period.
- **Inherited framing** — a later entry repeats or adopts an earlier classification without clearly adding new evidence.
- **Observation-window limitation** — the observation may be accurate but may not represent the patient's state across the day, medication cycle, or symptom cycle.
- **Non-monotonic treatment response** — response varies over time and cannot safely be summarized as simply effective or ineffective.
- **Unresolved proposition** — the record leaves the issue open.
- **Expert question** — the record can identify the question but cannot answer it without external expertise.


### Pass 13 evidential-persistence status labels

For the evidential-persistence / knowledge-carry-forward audit, use the following documentary classifications:

- **evidential continuity preserved** — the earlier finding remains visibly active in later reasoning, management, or explicit historical comparison;
- **partial carry-forward** — some part of the earlier finding remains visible, but its full evidential content or scope is not expressly retained;
- **local updating without global re-synthesis** — the finding changes immediate/local management but is not visibly integrated into the broader longitudinal causal or treatment model;
- **loss of evidential continuity** — a clinically meaningful earlier finding is no longer visibly active when the same or closely related proposition later recurs; this is a documentary classification only and does not prove forgetting, ignorance, or error;
- **later retrospective reconstruction** — later documentation retrospectively restores or attributes continuity that is not equivalently visible in the intervening contemporaneous record;
- **unresolved** — the surviving record does not permit a reliable continuity classification.

---

# PART I — CORE FINDINGS REGISTER

## 3. Pass 1 — Bare Chronological Reading

**Pass scope:** This is the first pass only. It records the sequence of material events, symptoms, observations, investigations, referrals, treatment changes, treatment responses, and later explanations. It deliberately does **not** yet perform the prospective, retrospective, document-architecture, management-plan, referral-pathway, causal-proposition, evidential-development, perspective, uncertainty-and-closure, treatment-response, or complaint-response passes. Those fields are therefore kept narrow or explicitly deferred.

### Finding [F-001]: Initial documented gait and bilateral foot presentation

**Date / period:**  
2017-04-10

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Chronological

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
None applied in Pass 1

#### A. Case fact

The GP record documents abnormal gait and bilateral foot pain. The patient linked the gait to an older left-leg sciatica problem and wondered whether it had become habitual. Examination documented marked mallet toes, onychogryphosis, and callus/pressure areas. Podiatry referral and screening blood tests were planned.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, Document 01, 2017-04-10, CLIN-000018–CLIN-000028.

#### B. Record fact

This is the earliest surviving 2017 entry in the compiled record that jointly documents gait abnormality, foot pain, and structural foot findings.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, Document 01, CLIN-000018–CLIN-000028.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Pass 1 establishes only that gait abnormality, bilateral foot pain, and structural foot pathology were documented together at the outset. It does not establish the cause of the gait or pain.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The same entry records the patient's own earlier sciatica/habitual explanation; later neurological evidence had not yet entered the record.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Deferred to later passes.

#### F. Decision point

Deferred to later passes.

#### G. Expert question

Deferred to later passes.

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact

#### I. Notes

The GP export contains duplicate reproductions of many entries; source duplication should not be counted as independent corroboration.

### Finding [F-002]: Progressive movement-initiation, tone, dexterity, and work impairment prompted neurological assessment

**Date / period:**  
2017-06-08 to 2017-06-29

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Chronological

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
None applied in Pass 1

#### A. Case fact

The GP documented difficulty initiating movement, including using the slowing of a bus to help initiate standing/walking; later marked increased tone in both legs and brisk upper-limb reflexes were recorded. Occupational Health separately raised concern about the gait. By 29 June the patient reported worsening dexterity and inability to perform his job, and urgent Medical Assessment Unit review was arranged while a Neurology referral was already pending.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, Document 01, CLIN-000064–CLIN-000111; CLIN-000190–CLIN-000235. Document 02, CLIN-000847–CLIN-000884.

#### B. Record fact

The chronology shows escalation from a foot/gait presentation to documented motor-initiation, tone, reflex, dexterity, occupational, and daily-function concerns before specialist assessment.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, Document 01 and Document 02, cited above.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Pass 1 establishes a worsening neurological-function sequence and the timing of referral/escalation; it does not determine diagnosis or causation.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The patient had earlier attributed some symptoms to anxiety, sciatica, or foot problems; these remained part of the history when the neurological assessment was initiated.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Deferred to later passes.

#### F. Decision point

Deferred to later passes.

#### G. Expert question

Deferred to later passes.

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact

#### I. Notes

### Finding [F-003]: July 2017 specialist assessment established a Parkinsonian syndrome while foot causation remained open

**Date / period:**  
2017-07-03 to 2017-07-13

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Chronological

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
None applied in Pass 1

#### A. Case fact

Hospital Neurology documented marked symmetrical increased tone, gross bradykinesia, a strikingly abnormal gait with a dystonic-looking foot posture, pes planus, hammertoes, and minimal tremor. The impression was young-onset Parkinsonism/Parkinson's disease with multiple possible forms under consideration. The same assessment stated that the leg symptoms including corns, calluses, and burning might be separate or might reflect a secondary peripheral neuropathy and recorded the relationship as not clear. MRI/CT and broad investigations were performed; the 13 July DaTscan was markedly abnormal bilaterally.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, Document 04 / 2017 Neurology material, CLIN-001085–CLIN-001145; CLIN-001153–CLIN-001180; CLIN-001363–CLIN-001407; CLIN-001629–CLIN-001638.

#### B. Record fact

By July 2017 the record contains both a syndrome-level neurological formulation and an explicit unresolved statement about how the foot/corn/callus/burning symptoms related to that syndrome.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-001128–CLIN-001149.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Pass 1 establishes coexistence of Parkinsonian findings, structural foot findings, and contemporaneous causal uncertainty. It does not resolve the foot-pain mechanism.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Structural foot pathology was directly observed; neurological posture and rigidity were also directly observed. Neither strand eliminates the other on this pass.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Deferred to later passes.

#### F. Decision point

Deferred to later passes.

#### G. Expert question

Deferred to later passes.

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact

#### I. Notes

The discharge summary also uses a categorical primary-diagnosis field of “Parkinsons Disease”; the longer contemporaneous narrative contains a broader differential. The significance of that document structure is reserved for a later pass.

### Finding [F-004]: Initial treatment strategy used rasagiline/procyclidine and deferred levodopa/dopamine agonists

**Date / period:**  
July–August 2017

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Chronological

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
None applied in Pass 1

#### A. Case fact

Neurology documented a plan to use symptomatic alternatives including rasagiline and a slow procyclidine titration while avoiding levodopa or dopamine agonists for as long as possible because of concern about adverse effects in a very young patient. The record expressly states that levodopa might later work very well and that it might eventually be regretted that it had not been started earlier. GP records show procyclidine titration commencing in August.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-001166–CLIN-001223; CLIN-001561–CLIN-001612; Document 01 CLIN-000611–CLIN-000620.

The first recorded specialist management strategy explicitly separates immediate symptomatic treatment from deferred levodopa/dopamine-agonist treatment.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-001166–CLIN-001223; CLIN-001601–CLIN-001612.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Pass 1 establishes the timing and stated existence of the deferral strategy, not whether the strategy was clinically optimal.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The same document anticipates that levodopa could produce substantial benefit and records that later commencement might be regretted.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Deferred to later passes.

Deferred to later passes.

#### G. Expert question

Deferred to later passes.

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Chronological

**Cross-cutting control(s):**

#### A. Case fact

Prof Daniel Healy recorded that the patient “probably has Park II,” suggested testing it first, and documented a dramatic improvement in motor symptoms with low-dose procyclidine four times daily and rasagiline 1 mg daily. He also noted that PARK II patients may manage for long periods with relatively low doses of dopamine replacement but can become dyskinetic if over-used.

**Source location(s):**

#### B. Record fact

The earliest surviving specialist second-opinion letter in the compiled record both raises PARK2/PRKN specifically and records a strong treatment response on the then-current non-levodopa regimen.

`complete-record.md`, CLIN-001741–CLIN-001760.

### Finding [F-006]: Procyclidine withdrawal/reintroduction produced contemporaneous changes in gait, tension, feet, and vision

**Date / period:**  
September–October 2017; later described clinically in April 2018

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Chronological

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
None applied in Pass 1

#### A. Case fact

Contemporaneous October 2017 patient emails describe marked physical improvement on Kemadrin/procyclidine, including restoration of walking, writing, posture, leg looseness, and dexterity. During a planned withdrawal because of visual effects, the patient wrote that tension was building, his feet were restless, and his walk was falling apart while eyesight returned toward normal. A 2018 Neurology note later records that during the two-week withdrawal the first thing noticed to change rapidly was the return “on the foot, of the outside left corn,” with the patient becoming more shifty/unrelaxed off procyclidine.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, Documents 07–08, CLIN-001770–CLIN-001801; CLIN-001822–CLIN-001839. Document 10, CLIN-001914–CLIN-001925.

#### B. Record fact

The chronology contains a medication withdrawal and reintroduction episode with symptom changes recorded during the episode, plus a later clinical description of the same period.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-001782–CLIN-001801; CLIN-001914–CLIN-001925.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Pass 1 establishes temporal association between procyclidine state and changes in motor/foot symptoms, alongside visual adverse effects. Mechanism is not established on this pass.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The same period includes persistent structural corn/callus pathology and visual toxicity limiting procyclidine exposure.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Deferred to later passes.

#### F. Decision point

Deferred to later passes.

#### G. Expert question

Deferred to later passes.

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact

#### I. Notes

The claim that focal outside-left-foot pain was the *first* symptom to return also appears as later patient recollection; the stronger contemporaneous email evidence directly records restless feet and gait deterioration.

**Date / period:**  
2017-09-14 to 2019-03-25

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Chronological

**Cross-cutting control(s):**

#### A. Case fact

Podiatry documented recurrent left fifth-MTPJ callus/corn/HD, clawed toes, nail disease, pronating foot type, tight gastroc/soleus, and other local findings. Treatment included debridement/enucleation, toe props, offloading, insoles, and repeated routine care. The patient reported that the gait had previously been very bad but was no longer so at the first September 2017 visit.

The structural/mechanical foot findings recur across multiple podiatry visits over roughly eighteen months and are treated locally.

`complete-record.md`, CLIN-002872–CLIN-002969; CLIN-002984–CLIN-002988.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Pass 1 establishes a sustained structural/mechanical foot track in parallel with neurological treatment. It does not establish whether that track fully explains pain or gait phenomena.

Neupro (rotigotine) was introduced after amantadine had not helped. ### Finding [F-012]: Levodopa was introduced in 2021 as procyclidine was being removed

**Date / period:**  
September–October 2021

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Chronological

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
None applied in Pass 1

#### A. Case fact

The patient contacted the Parkinson's nurse feeling undertreated and was started on Sinemet 62.5 mg twice daily, described as the first levodopa treatment. Because of raised intraocular pressures/visual concerns, the nurse suggested removing Kemadrin/procyclidine and replacing it with Sinemet. Sinemet was then increased to four times daily. Neurology expressed concern about the rapid increase and noted early awareness of chorea while also explaining the aim to keep levodopa dosing minimal in a young patient.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, Document 13 / Document 18, CLIN-002330–CLIN-002359; CLIN-002813 onward for duplicate supplied excerpt.

The first documented levodopa introduction occurred in the context of both perceived undertreatment and removal of procyclidine for ocular reasons.

`complete-record.md`, CLIN-002335–CLIN-002353.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Pass 1 establishes the sequence and contemporaneous stated reasons; the relative contribution of each reason is for later analysis.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

### Finding [F-014]: Severe bilateral burning foot/lower-leg pain led to rapid pregabalin escalation and urgent Neurology re-referral

**Date / period:**  
Early 2023 / March 2023

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Chronological

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
None applied in Pass 1

#### A. Case fact

The GP urgently re-referred the patient for severe bilateral burning pain in the feet and lower legs, at times described as extreme and associated with a death wish. Pregabalin had been started at 25 mg twice daily and escalated through multiple prescribers to very high total daily dosing; a 28 March nurse email records 600 mg/day with debilitating pain and no quality of life. The patient at that time believed the pain was not Parkinson-related and reported stopping Parkinson's medications for ten days without noticing a Parkinsonian difference.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, Document 15, CLIN-002623–CLIN-002688.

#### B. Record fact

The severe pain crisis and pregabalin escalation were documented before the later 2023 neurology assessment that described substantial benefit from pregabalin.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-002637–CLIN-002683.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Pass 1 preserves the sequence: severe pain persisted despite high-dose pregabalin at one point, even though later entries describe substantial benefit.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The referral also recalls an earlier episode of pain settling with better Parkinson's control; the patient, however, then believed the current foot pain was separate from Parkinson's.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Deferred to later passes.

#### F. Decision point

Deferred to later passes.

#### G. Expert question

Deferred to later passes.

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact

### Finding [F-015]: Later 2023 records linked evening gait/pain fluctuations with levodopa state while maintaining pregabalin

**Date / period:**  
2023

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Chronological

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
None applied in Pass 1

#### A. Case fact

Later in 2023 the patient reported that pregabalin had largely removed the foot pain, although left-sided discomfort and evening walking difficulty remained. He described severe evening impairment not represented by a better video taken while Stalevo/levodopa was on board. Neurology considered the evening pattern typical of OFF Parkinson's symptoms, recorded that Stalevo facilitated darts and improved the pain experience, and considered increasing evening levodopa but was concerned about chorea/dyskinesia. Pregabalin was left at 600 mg/day.

A separate nurse contact records severe neuropathic foot pain with reduced mobility/shuffling, worse in the morning and later in the day if Sinemet had not been taken; after discussion with the consultant, Stalevo was started in the morning on the view that leg pain and shuffling appeared OFF-related.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, Document 15, CLIN-002424–CLIN-002495; CLIN-002573–CLIN-002583.

#### B. Record fact

By later 2023 the record contains explicit clinician language linking at least some pain/shuffling and evening motor difficulty to OFF state, while also continuing a neuropathic-pain treatment strategy.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-002456–CLIN-002492; CLIN-002574–CLIN-002581.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Pass 1 establishes that both explanatory/treatment tracks were active in the record at the same time.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Pregabalin was described as highly beneficial for pain; structural corn and clawing phenomena also remained documented; levodopa escalation carried a dyskinesia concern.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Deferred to later passes.

#### F. Decision point

Deferred to later passes.

#### G. Expert question

Deferred to later passes.

#### H. Status

Documentary tension

#### I. Notes

No attempt is made here to decide whether pain was neuropathic, structural, dystonic, OFF-related, amplified, or mixed.

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
None applied in Pass 1

#### A. Case fact

On pregabalin 150 mg four times daily, the record states that foot pain was removed on the right and much better overall, with some left-sided pain remaining. Toe curling during activities such as pumping bicycle tyres persisted despite pregabalin, and evening function remained limited. Neurology suggested moving the evening Sinemet to before food with fluid; the note described the therapeutic problem as balancing evening benefit against dyskinesia risk.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, Document 16, CLIN-002695–CLIN-002719.

#### B. Record fact

The 2024 chronology separates pain improvement on pregabalin from persistent toe-curling/motor phenomena and introduces an adjustment to levodopa timing rather than simply increasing dose.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-002698–CLIN-002711.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Pass 1 establishes partial symptom dissociation and a treatment-timing change; mechanism and significance are reserved for later passes.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The patient felt the overall medication was “spot on,” and no dyskinesia was seen that day, although mild dyskinesia had been observed at other times.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Deferred to later passes.

#### F. Decision point

Deferred to later passes.

#### G. Expert question

Deferred to later passes.

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact

#### I. Notes

Later passes should disaggregate dose, timing, formulation, and duration of coverage.

### Finding [F-017]: 2025 severe morning foot pain was documented after pregabalin self-reduction, with relief after Parkinson medication onset

**Date / period:**  
2025 Neurology clinic

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Chronological

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
None applied in Pass 1

#### A. Case fact

By 2025 pregabalin had been reduced from 600 mg/day to 50 mg three times daily. The patient described severe morning foot pain, especially on the left, with the first roughly 150 steps described as torture. The note records that once Parkinson medication reached his system the foot sensations improved. The clinician considered both loss of pregabalin benefit and Parkinson-related pain modulation, noted dyskinesia risk with more Sinemet, and discussed the patient's concern about lack of overnight medication coverage.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, Document 17, CLIN-002767–CLIN-002800.

#### B. Record fact

The 2025 entry simultaneously documents a large pregabalin reduction, severe morning pain, and a recurring temporal relationship between Parkinson medication onset and pain relief.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-002767–CLIN-002800.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Pass 1 establishes the sequence and coexistence of those variables; it does not allocate causation among them.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The clinician recorded that maximum-dose pregabalin had not eliminated pain and also expected increased Sinemet to give only partial relief with increased dyskinesia risk.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Deferred to later passes.

#### F. Decision point

Deferred to later passes.

#### G. Expert question

Deferred to later passes.

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact

#### I. Notes

The same clinic note states that morning Neurology appointments were not ideal because morning was the patient's best time; that observation-window issue is reserved for its dedicated pass.

### Finding [F-018]: Sinemet controlled release was introduced to alter overnight/early-morning coverage

2025 Neurology clinic

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Chronological

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
None applied in Pass 1

#### A. Case fact

Neurology discussed an overnight gap between the last evening Sinemet and waking, considered waking early to take medication, and then planned Sinemet controlled release close to bedtime, starting at half of a “Half Sinemet CR” tablet and increasing if tolerated. The clinician warned that the trial might not work and could cause dyskinesias or dreaming.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, Document 17, CLIN-002792–CLIN-002800.

#### B. Record fact

This is the first explicit entry in the chronological record identified in Pass 1 that changes levodopa **formulation/coverage** to address the overnight/early-morning period rather than merely discussing dose.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-002792–CLIN-002800.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Pass 1 establishes the timing and design of the controlled-release trial; outcome and counterfactual significance require later passes.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The clinician simultaneously considered increasing pregabalin and a chronic-pain referral, and did not promise that Sinemet CR would work.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Deferred to later passes.

#### F. Decision point

Deferred to later passes.

#### G. Expert question

Deferred to later passes.

Established documentary fact

### Finding [F-019]: March 2025 complaint response reported profound relief with Sinemet CR and gave a retrospective account of earlier management

**Date / period:**  
2025-03-25

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Chronological

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
None applied in Pass 1

#### A. Case fact

In the complaint response, the consultant stated that Sinemet CR had provided profound current relief, that the team had for some time understood Sinemet to help the patient's experience of pain, and that reluctance to increase Sinemet quickly reflected dyskinesia and other risks. The response also stated that pregabalin had been introduced for neuropathic pain “in part to try to spare further Sinemet increase,” and described the history as a cautious strategy in a young patient.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, Document 21, CLIN-003239–CLIN-003269.

These statements are later explanations written in response to a complaint and refer back to earlier treatment rationale and knowledge.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-003239–CLIN-003269.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Pass 1 records that the later explanation exists and what it says. Whether its historical claims are supported by contemporaneous entries is intentionally deferred to the retrospective/complaint-response passes.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The contemporaneous 2023 referral record identifies pregabalin as GP-originated treatment for severe burning pain; the relationship between that initiation rationale and the later “levodopa-sparing” description is not adjudicated in Pass 1.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Deferred to later passes.

#### F. Decision point

Deferred to later passes.

Deferred to later passes.

## 3A. Pass 2 — Prospective Reading

**Pass scope:** This pass reads the record from the standpoint of the people involved **at the time of each decision**. It asks what was then known, what was suspected, what remained unresolved, what genuinely new evidence had entered, and what later evidence was not yet available. Later genetic confirmation, later treatment success, and later complaint explanations are not back-projected into earlier decisions.

### Finding [F-021]: April 2017 presentation did not yet establish a neurological diagnosis

**Date / period:**  
2017-04-10

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Prospective

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Contemporaneous vs later-attributed rationale

#### A. Case fact

At the April GP presentation, the information then available was abnormal gait, bilateral foot pain, the patient's own sciatica/habit explanation, and directly observed mallet toes, onychogryphosis and callus/pressure areas. The immediate plan was Podiatry referral, screening blood tests and retrieval of old notes.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, Document 01, CLIN-000018–CLIN-000028.

#### B. Record fact

At this point the later neurological syndrome, abnormal DaTscan, PARK2 suspicion, medication responses and genetic result did not yet exist in the contemporaneous record.

**Source location(s):**  
Chronological relation between CLIN-000018–CLIN-000028 and the later 2017–2026 entries.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The April entry can support that structural foot pathology and gait abnormality were present together, but a prospective reading does not permit the later Parkinsonian diagnosis to be treated as already known or as the explanation for the foot presentation at that time.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The gait abnormality was already unusual enough to be noted, and later neurological concern emerged quickly. That later development does not change what was actually known on 10 April.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether the gait abnormality arose from structural/mechanical foot problems, a neurological process, the patient's earlier sciatica account, more than one process, or another explanation.

#### F. Decision point

The contemporaneous response was local foot assessment plus basic investigation and retrieval of prior history. On the April evidence alone, the record does not show that a neurological diagnosis had yet been established.

#### G. Expert question

None required for the documentary proposition. Any question about whether neurological referral should already have occurred on 10 April would require external clinical judgment.

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact / Unresolved proposition

#### I. Notes

Later diagnosis must not be back-projected into this entry.

---

### Finding [F-022]: June 2017 neurological concern became independently corroborated before diagnosis

**Date / period:**  
2017-06-08 to 2017-06-12

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Prospective

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Contemporaneous vs later-attributed rationale

#### A. Case fact

By 8 June the GP had observed difficulty with fluid initiation of movement and was considering Neurology review of gait, feet and “muscle tension,” initially wanting old notes first. On 12 June an Occupational Health doctor independently contacted the GP about the gait; after discussion, the GP decided to refer to Neurology before the old notes arrived.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, Document 01, CLIN-000064–CLIN-000111.

#### B. Record fact

The contemporaneous sequence records a change in threshold for referral: initial consideration pending old records, followed by referral once another doctor independently echoed the concern.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-000068–CLIN-000074; CLIN-000102–CLIN-000105.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Prospectively, the decision to refer arose from observed motor-initiation/gait features and convergent clinical concern, not from a diagnosis that had already been made.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

At this stage the patient still had structural foot problems and previous explanations in the history; no DaTscan or specialist examination had yet occurred.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The nature and cause of the gait, movement-initiation and “muscle tension” problems.

#### F. Decision point

12 June is a clear referral decision point: independent concern altered the plan from waiting for old records to proceeding with Neurology referral.

#### G. Expert question

None needed to establish the decision sequence.

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact

#### I. Notes

This finding concerns the threshold for referral, not whether the eventual diagnosis was predictable at an earlier date.

---

### Finding [F-023]: Late June 2017 functional deterioration justified urgent assessment while diagnosis remained open

**Date / period:**  
2017-06-23 to 2017-06-29

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Prospective

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Contemporaneous vs later-attributed rationale

#### A. Case fact

The GP then documented marked increased tone in both legs, brisk upper-limb reflexes, work difficulty, worsening dexterity and inability to perform usual tasks. The urgent MAU referral described definite increased tone, abnormal gait and deterioration, with “?parkinsonian or other underlying diagnosis.”

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, Document 01, CLIN-000190–CLIN-000235; Document 02, CLIN-000851–CLIN-000884.

#### B. Record fact

The referral wording itself preserves diagnostic openness: Parkinsonism was raised as a possibility, alongside “other underlying diagnosis,” before specialist investigation.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-000877–CLIN-000884.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The urgent referral can prove that a neurological syndrome was sufficiently concerning to require prompt assessment; it cannot prove that Parkinson's disease, its subtype, or the cause of the foot pain was already known.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The patient had considered alcohol as potentially relevant and had a history of anxiety-related attribution and structural foot problems. These remained possible contextual factors at the time.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The neurological diagnosis, disease form, relation of the feet to the neurological problem, and significance of alcohol or other historical factors.

#### F. Decision point

29 June: escalation from routine Neurology referral to urgent MAU assessment because of functional deterioration and employment impact.

#### G. Expert question

Whether the urgency and breadth of assessment were clinically appropriate is external to the documentary analysis.

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact

#### I. Notes

Later genetic confirmation does not alter the referral's contemporaneous uncertainty.

---

### Finding [F-024]: July 2017 achieved operational neurological closure without causal closure of the foot syndrome

**Date / period:**  
2017-07-03 to 2017-07-13

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Prospective

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Contemporaneous vs later-attributed rationale

#### A. Case fact

Specialist examination showed marked rigidity/bradykinesia and a striking, partly dystonic-looking gait. The working formulation was young-onset Parkinsonism/Parkinson's disease, with atypical and genetic possibilities still under consideration. At the same time the neurologist recorded that the corns, calluses and burning might be completely separate or possibly a secondary peripheral neuropathy and explicitly wrote “Not clear.” The DaTscan later in the admission was markedly abnormal bilaterally.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-001085–CLIN-001145; CLIN-001128–CLIN-001149; CLIN-001139–CLIN-001140; CLIN-001633–CLIN-001638.

#### B. Record fact

The record reached enough confidence in a Parkinsonian syndrome to drive management while expressly leaving the causal relationship of the painful/structural foot manifestations unresolved.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-001128–CLIN-001149; CLIN-001153–CLIN-001180.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

A prospective reading separates **operational closure** of the neurological syndrome from **causal non-closure** of the foot-pain/corn/callus question. The later Parkinson diagnosis cannot be used to erase the explicit uncertainty that remained at this stage.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Structural pathology was directly observed, while the gait also had dystonic/Parkinsonian features. Both were contemporaneously present.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether and how structural lesions, abnormal posturing/loading, peripheral neuropathy, Parkinsonian motor state and pain experience interacted.

#### F. Decision point

The July assessment established the baseline formulation against which later foot-related evidence could require reassessment.

#### G. Expert question

What clinical evidence would have been required in 2017 to distinguish structural pain generation, neuropathy, dystonic/postural contribution and neurological modulation?

#### H. Status

Documentary tension / Unresolved proposition

#### I. Notes

The categorical diagnosis field in the discharge summary does not prospectively resolve the separately stated causal uncertainty about the feet.

---

### Finding [F-025]: The original levodopa-deferral decision was made before later treatment-response evidence existed

**Date / period:**  
July–August 2017

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Prospective

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Contemporaneous vs later-attributed rationale

#### A. Case fact

Neurology documented a strategy of beginning rasagiline and slowly titrating procyclidine while delaying levodopa/dopamine agonists because of the patient's very young age and concern about dyskinesia and impulse-control effects. The same document acknowledged that levodopa might work very well and that there could later be regret that it had not been started earlier.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-001166–CLIN-001223; CLIN-001561–CLIN-001612.

#### B. Record fact

At the time the deferral strategy was set, the record did not yet contain the September Healy review, the October procyclidine withdrawal experiment, years of later OFF-related pain observations, the 2025 Sinemet CR response, or the 2026 genetic confirmation.

**Source location(s):**  
Chronological relation of the July 2017 treatment plan to later entries.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The 2017 decision must be assessed against the evidence available then. Later benefit from levodopa cannot itself prove that the original decision was unsound; equally, the contemporaneous note's express acknowledgment that earlier levodopa might later be regretted shows that the trade-off was recognized rather than the potential benefit being unknown.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The patient was severely functionally affected, but the team contemporaneously documented concern about long-term treatment complications in a very young patient.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether the balance struck between current function and future treatment risk was clinically appropriate.

#### F. Decision point

Initial symptomatic-treatment architecture: procyclidine/rasagiline first; levodopa/dopamine agonists deferred.

#### G. Expert question

Given the clinical state actually documented in July 2017, was the chosen sequencing of symptomatic therapies within a reasonable range of practice at that time?

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact / Expert question

#### I. Notes

The register does not infer negligence or correctness from the deferral itself.

---

### Finding [F-026]: The September 2017 second opinion added PARK2 suspicion and strong treatment-response evidence, but did not direct immediate levodopa

**Date / period:**  
2017-09-12

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Prospective

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Contemporaneous vs later-attributed rationale

#### A. Case fact

Prof Healy recorded that the patient probably had PARK2, recommended testing it first, and documented dramatic motor improvement with low-dose procyclidine plus rasagiline. He noted that PARK2 patients may do well for long periods on relatively low doses of dopamine replacement and may become dyskinetic if over-used.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, Document 06, CLIN-001741–CLIN-001760.

#### B. Record fact

This was genuinely new information available after the initial July plan: a movement-disorder specialist's PARK2 hypothesis and an observed/reported strong response to the existing regimen. The surviving letter does not instruct immediate levodopa commencement.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-001742–CLIN-001760.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Prospectively, the second opinion could reinforce both the genetic hypothesis and caution about dyskinesia while also providing a concrete recommendation to test PARK2. It does not by itself establish that levodopa should then have been started.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The later 2026 genetic result was not available; the 2017 view remained probabilistic.

#### E. What remains unresolved

What follow-through occurred on the recommendation for PARK2 testing and whether any result would then have altered treatment.

#### F. Decision point

Receipt of a specialist recommendation to test PARK2 was a potential investigation decision point.

#### G. Expert question

Would confirmation of PARK2 at that stage have materially altered symptomatic-treatment decisions, prognosis counselling, or monitoring?

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact / Unresolved proposition

#### I. Notes

The source itself states only what Healy recommended; it does not establish what Galway subsequently did with the recommendation.

---

### Finding [F-027]: The 2017 procyclidine withdrawal created new prospective evidence that the feet were medication-state sensitive

**Date / period:**  
September–October 2017

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Prospective

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Medication-state trace / Non-monotonic treatment response

#### A. Case fact

During a planned procyclidine interruption for visual side effects, the patient contemporaneously reported rising tension, restless feet and deterioration of gait while vision improved. The medication was then reintroduced at a lower dose and later increased because the lower dose did not cover all symptoms.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-001770–CLIN-001801; CLIN-001822–CLIN-001834.

#### B. Record fact

Unlike the earlier July uncertainty, this episode added within-patient temporal evidence obtained during an intentional medication change. The foot symptoms changed alongside neurological motor symptoms when treatment state changed.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-001782–CLIN-001790; CLIN-001826–CLIN-001834.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Prospectively, the safe new proposition was not that the structural corn was caused by Parkinsonism, but that the foot symptom complex had demonstrated neurological medication-state sensitivity. From this point onward, that fact became available to inform later assessments of recurrent foot symptoms.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Visual toxicity improved off procyclidine, creating a real treatment constraint. Structural corn/callus pathology also remained present.

#### E. What remains unresolved

What the medication-state sensitivity represented mechanistically and how much of the pain/foot problem it explained.

#### F. Decision point

The withdrawal/reintroduction period was a potential re-synthesis point for the earlier “Not clear” relationship between neurological state and foot manifestations.

#### G. Expert question

How should a clinician interpret foot symptoms that worsen during withdrawal of an effective anti-Parkinsonian medication when structural lesions are also present?

#### H. Status

Supported proposition / Unresolved proposition

#### I. Notes

Treatment response alone does not prove mechanism.

---

### Finding [F-028]: By April 2018 the clinician had recorded the rapid return of the outside-left-foot problem off procyclidine, but the earlier causal question is not expressly re-closed in the cited note

**Date / period:**  
2018-04-24

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Prospective

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Medication-state trace / Contemporaneous vs later-attributed rationale

#### A. Case fact

The 2018 Neurology note states that when procyclidine had been stopped for two weeks, the first thing noticed changing rapidly was the return on the foot of the outside-left corn; the patient was more “shifty” and “unrelaxed” off procyclidine. The note also states that he could be very much better with levodopa or another dopamine agonist, while continuing the earlier decision not to use them yet.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-001914–CLIN-001951.

#### B. Record fact

By this review, the treating neurologist's own record contained the medication-foot interaction, not merely a later patient reconstruction. In the cited surviving note, no explicit statement is located that reclassifies or closes the July 2017 “Not clear” causal relationship in light of that new observation.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-001914–CLIN-001951; compare earlier CLIN-001142–CLIN-001149.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The absence of an explicit re-synthesis in this cited note is a fact about documentation, not proof that no clinical thought occurred. Prospectively, however, the evidential state had changed: medication-state sensitivity at the same foot region was now known to the treating team.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The structural corn remained real and recurrent, and procyclidine had treatment-limiting visual effects. The note discusses pain amplification in Parkinson's more generally.

#### E. What remains unresolved

How the clinician understood the rapid foot change off procyclidine and whether it altered the working causal model of the foot problem.

#### F. Decision point

April 2018 is a documentary decision/review point at which the earlier foot uncertainty could have been explicitly revisited.

#### G. Expert question

Did the medication-state evidence then available warrant additional neurological investigation, a different treatment trial, or only continued observation?

#### H. Status

Unresolved proposition / Documentary tension

#### I. Notes

Do not infer from documentary silence that reassessment did not occur; the finding is limited to what is expressly visible in the cited note.

---

### Finding [F-029]: Dopamine-agonist treatment in 2018–2019 demonstrated a narrow symptomatic/adverse-effect balance before levodopa was used

**Date / period:**  
Late 2018–2019

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Prospective

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Dose-formulation-timing-coverage / Observation-window

#### A. Case fact

Rotigotine/Neupro was escalated above 10 mg and was associated with choreiform/dyskinetic movement and behavioural/impulsivity concerns; at 10 mg the patient preferred the side-effect balance but felt under-treated. He also stated that he would look worse later in the day than at the clinic review. Neurology continued to prefer delaying levodopa while recognizing that it would eventually need to be considered.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-002111–CLIN-002161.

#### B. Record fact

At this stage, the team had direct evidence that stronger dopaminergic stimulation could produce troublesome adverse effects and that clinic appearance might not capture the worse later-day state.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-002120–CLIN-002158.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Prospectively, both undertreatment and overtreatment risks were visible. The later fact that levodopa eventually helped cannot erase the contemporaneous evidence of treatment sensitivity.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The patient sometimes appeared nearly normal in clinic at the lower dose despite reporting subjective stiffness and later-day deterioration.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether a different drug, formulation, timing or lower levodopa exposure could have improved the balance at that stage.

#### F. Decision point

The emergence of dyskinesia/behavioural effects on higher-dose dopamine agonist was a treatment-strategy decision point.

#### G. Expert question

Given these adverse effects and residual symptoms, what alternative dopaminergic strategies would have been reasonable at the time?

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact / Expert question

#### I. Notes

The dedicated observation-window pass will later examine the representativeness of clinic findings in more detail.

---

### Finding [F-030]: First levodopa use in 2021 arose from perceived undertreatment and the need to remove procyclidine for ocular reasons

**Date / period:**  
September–October 2021

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Prospective

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Contemporaneous vs later-attributed rationale / Dose-formulation-timing-coverage

#### A. Case fact

The patient reported feeling undertreated. Sinemet 62.5 mg twice daily was started as the first levodopa treatment. Raised eye pressures/visual concerns then prompted a plan to remove Kemadrin/procyclidine and replace it with Sinemet, which increased to four times daily. Early chorea was noted and the neurologist cautioned against substantial further increase.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-002330–CLIN-002359.

#### B. Record fact

The contemporaneous initiation rationale visible here is treatment of undertreatment and substitution for procyclidine in the setting of ocular concerns. The record at this point does not frame first levodopa initiation as a specific treatment for chronic foot pain.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-002335–CLIN-002353.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Any later description of levodopa's pain role must be distinguished from the contemporaneous reason visible when levodopa was first started.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The patient had earlier foot symptoms that were medication-state sensitive, but the 2021 initiation note itself focuses on undertreatment and removal of Kemadrin.

#### E. What remains unresolved

When the treating team first understood levodopa as materially modifying the patient's foot pain, as distinct from motor symptoms.

#### F. Decision point

2021 marks the transition from a levodopa-deferral strategy to actual levodopa treatment.

#### G. Expert question

None is required to establish the contemporaneous rationale; clinical appropriateness of dose escalation remains an external question.

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact

#### I. Notes

Later complaint-response wording should not be used to rewrite the 2021 initiation rationale.

### Finding [F-031]: The 2022 morning foot pattern was a prospective clue but did not by itself establish an OFF mechanism

**Date / period:**  
2022

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Prospective

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Medication-state trace / Dose-formulation-timing-coverage

#### A. Case fact

The patient reported that most mornings his feet took 20–25 minutes to warm up and were sore around the corns/calluses; he wondered whether the pain was neuralgic. He was using small Sinemet doses flexibly according to expected demands.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-002375–CLIN-002400.

#### B. Record fact

The time-of-day pattern was documented after levodopa introduction but before the more explicit 2023–2025 OFF-related pain wording.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-002382–CLIN-002386.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Prospectively, the morning pattern could raise a medication-state or overnight-coverage question, but the 2022 entry alone does not establish that explanation and also directly references corns/calluses.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Overall function was reported as very good, with extensive walking and minimal cramping.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether the 20–25-minute morning pattern reflected local mechanical pain, medication onset, warming/loading effects, dystonia, or a mixture.

#### F. Decision point

Potential but not clearly documented as a treatment-change decision point in this note.

#### G. Expert question

What additional history or timed examination would best distinguish medication-state effects from structural/mechanical morning pain?

#### H. Status

Unresolved proposition

#### I. Notes

The stronger OFF-related evidence appears later and should not be back-projected into 2022 as already proven.

### Finding [F-032]: The March 2023 pain crisis contained genuinely competing prospective evidence

**Date / period:**  
March 2023

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Prospective

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Medication-state trace / Non-monotonic treatment response

#### A. Case fact

The patient had severe bilateral burning foot/lower-leg pain despite rapid escalation of pregabalin to high dose. He believed the pain was not Parkinson-related and reported ten days off Parkinson medication without noticing a Parkinsonian difference. The GP, however, recalled an earlier episode in which pains had settled with better Parkinson's control and urgently requested Neurology review.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-002623–CLIN-002688.

#### B. Record fact

At the time of urgent re-referral, the record contained conflicting contemporaneous signals: severe “neuropathic” pain despite high-dose pregabalin, the patient's report of no difference off Parkinson medication, and the GP's recollection that an earlier pain episode had improved with better Parkinson control.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-002637–CLIN-002683.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

A prospective reader should not treat either the “neuropathic” label or the Parkinson-related hypothesis as settled at this stage. The entry called for reassessment precisely because the existing evidence did not fit a single clean explanation.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The patient had structural foot disease and later reported strong pregabalin benefit; later 2023 records would also document OFF-related pain/shuffling. None of that later evidence was yet available at the initial March referral.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The cause of the severe burning pain, the significance of the ten-day medication interruption, and the relative roles of pregabalin-responsive pain and dopaminergic state.

#### F. Decision point

Urgent Neurology re-referral for severe pain despite escalating treatment.

#### G. Expert question

How much evidential weight should be given to a ten-day self-withdrawal report in assessing dopaminergic contribution to pain, and what confounders would need to be considered?

#### H. Status

Documentary tension / Unresolved proposition

#### I. Notes

The register preserves the conflict rather than selecting one explanation.

---

### Finding [F-033]: Later 2023 added explicit OFF-related pain/shuffling evidence and a therapeutic response decision

**Date / period:**  
Later 2023

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Prospective

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Medication-state trace / Dose-formulation-timing-coverage / Observation-window

#### A. Case fact

A nurse contact records severe foot pain with reduced mobility/shuffling, worse in the morning and later in the day if Sinemet had not been taken. After discussion with the consultant, the working view was that the leg pain and shuffling appeared OFF-related and Stalevo was started in the morning. In the later clinic account, the patient reported that Stalevo facilitated darts and that pain was better when levodopa was on board; Neurology considered regular evening levodopa but remained concerned about chorea/dyskinesia.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-002573–CLIN-002583; CLIN-002453–CLIN-002492.

#### B. Record fact

This was stronger prospective evidence than the 2022 morning pattern because the record now explicitly linked symptom worsening to missed/not-yet-taken Sinemet and used that interpretation to change treatment.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-002574–CLIN-002581.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

From this point, an OFF-related component to at least some pain/shuffling was an explicit working clinical proposition, not merely a later retrospective explanation. That still does not resolve the underlying pain generator.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Pregabalin was also reported to have largely removed the pain, structural lesions remained part of the history, and additional levodopa carried a documented dyskinesia risk. The patient did not regard the better darts video as representative of ordinary evenings.

#### E. What remains unresolved

How much of the disabling evening/morning syndrome was due to dopaminergic under-coverage versus other pain or structural factors, and what dosing/formulation strategy would optimize benefit without unacceptable dyskinesia.

#### F. Decision point

Commencement of morning Stalevo and consideration of regular evening levodopa were explicit treatment decision points based on new OFF-related evidence.

#### G. Expert question

Given documented OFF-related pain/shuffling and dyskinesia sensitivity, what medication timing/formulation options would have been reasonable in 2023?

#### H. Status

Supported proposition / Expert question

#### I. Notes

The note itself distinguishes pain experience from the underlying cause of pain.

---

### Finding [F-034]: The 2024 record showed pain/motor dissociation and moved one levodopa timing variable

**Date / period:**  
2024

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Prospective

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Dose-formulation-timing-coverage / Non-monotonic treatment response

#### A. Case fact

At 600 mg/day pregabalin, the right foot pain was described as much better and some left pain remained, while toe curling during exertion persisted. Evening function remained limited. Neurology proposed moving the evening Sinemet to before food with a good volume of water, while emphasizing the balance between evening benefit and dyskinesia.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-002695–CLIN-002719.

#### B. Record fact

The record prospectively separates improvement in pain from persistence of toe-curling/motor phenomena and treats **timing/absorption conditions** as a therapeutic variable rather than simply increasing dose.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-002698–CLIN-002710.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

By 2024, the treatment problem was demonstrably multidimensional: pregabalin could reduce pain without eliminating motor/postural phenomena, and levodopa optimization was constrained by dyskinesia. This does not establish which mechanism generated the pain.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The patient described the medication overall as “spot on,” and no dyskinesia was observed in that clinic visit.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether further change in timing, formulation or overnight coverage could improve symptoms without increasing dyskinesia.

#### F. Decision point

Adjustment of the evening Sinemet relative to food/fluid was a modest timing-focused intervention.

#### G. Expert question

What evidential weight should be given to response to timing/absorption changes when pain and motor symptoms are partly dissociated?

#### H. Status

Supported proposition / Unresolved proposition

#### I. Notes

Dose, formulation, timing and duration of coverage remain distinct variables.

---

### Finding [F-035]: The 2025 clinic converted the overnight-coverage hypothesis into a controlled-release treatment trial

**Date / period:**  
2025 Neurology clinic, before the later complaint response

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Prospective

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Medication-state trace / Dose-formulation-timing-coverage / Non-monotonic treatment response

#### A. Case fact

The patient had reduced pregabalin substantially and again described severe morning foot pain. The clinician recorded that the feet improved once Parkinson medication reached the system, while also believing that reduced pregabalin might be contributing. The patient raised the possibility that morning severity reflected the overnight period without medication. Neurology then planned bedtime Sinemet CR, explicitly warning that it might not work and might cause dyskinesia or dreaming.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-002767–CLIN-002800.

#### B. Record fact

At the point of prescribing Sinemet CR, the record contained both a competing pregabalin explanation and a reproducible temporal observation that pain improved when Parkinson medication took effect. The CR prescription therefore functioned prospectively as a test of a different therapeutic variable: overnight duration of levodopa coverage.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-002780–CLIN-002800.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The decision can be understood without hindsight as a reasonable hypothesis-testing step within the record. The later profound relief was not yet known when the trial was chosen.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Maximum-dose pregabalin had not eliminated pain; its later reduction coincided with deterioration. The clinician expected that increased Sinemet might provide only partial relief and carried dyskinesia risk.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Before the trial outcome, whether overnight dopaminergic under-coverage materially contributed to the morning pain and whether benefit would be sustainable or tolerable.

#### F. Decision point

Initiation of bedtime Sinemet CR specifically to extend overnight/early-morning coverage.

#### G. Expert question

Given the evidence available by this point, should a controlled-release or other timing/formulation trial have been considered earlier, and if so, at what earlier evidential threshold?

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact / Expert question

#### I. Notes

This question is not answered by the later response alone; it requires comparison with earlier decision points and external clinical judgment.

---

### Finding [F-036]: The March 2025 complaint response is prospective evidence only of the state of knowledge in March 2025, not of what was known years earlier

**Date / period:**  
2025-03-25

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Prospective

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Contemporaneous vs later-attributed rationale

#### A. Case fact

By the complaint-response date, the consultant described Sinemet CR as having provided profound current relief and stated that Sinemet had long been understood to help the patient's experience of pain. The response also retrospectively described pregabalin as introduced partly to spare further Sinemet increase.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-003239–CLIN-003269.

#### B. Record fact

Prospectively, those statements establish what the consultant was saying and understanding in March 2025. They do not, merely by being stated in 2025, establish that the same rationale or degree of certainty existed at earlier dates.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-003247–CLIN-003268.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

For Pass 2, the complaint response may inform the 2025 evidential state but must be quarantined from earlier prospective analyses unless contemporaneous support is separately located.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The 2021 levodopa initiation note gives contemporaneous reasons centered on undertreatment and removal of Kemadrin; the March 2023 pregabalin history identifies GP initiation for severe burning/neuropathic-type pain.

#### E. What remains unresolved

How far each 2025 historical statement is supported by the contemporaneous record of the period it describes.

#### F. Decision point

Not an earlier clinical decision point; this is a later explanatory/documentary event.

#### G. Expert question

None for the temporal-documentary proposition.

#### H. Status

Retrospective reconstruction

#### I. Notes

Detailed testing of the historical claims belongs to the retrospective and complaint-response passes.

---

### Finding [F-037]: The 2026 PRKN confirmation changes the evidential state from that date forward but not backward

**Date / period:**  
2026-02-03 to 2026-02-04

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Prospective

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Contemporaneous vs later-attributed rationale

#### A. Case fact

St Vincent's recorded two pathogenic Parkin variants and concluded that the Parkinsonism and dystonia were due to compound heterozygous Parkin mutations.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-003220–CLIN-003229.

#### B. Record fact

From February 2026 onward, PRKN/Parkin causation of the Parkinsonism/dystonia is documentary knowledge. Before that date the record contained suspicion, not this confirmed result.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-003227–CLIN-003229; compare 2017 suspicion at CLIN-001742 and CLIN-001520–CLIN-001525.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The genetic result can retrospectively strengthen the accuracy of the earlier PARK2 hypothesis, but a prospective reading does not permit it to be treated as knowledge available during the 2017–2025 decisions.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

None within the compiled record as to the stated genetic finding. The result still does not establish the cause of every painful foot lesion or pain episode.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The relationship between genetically confirmed PRKN Parkinsonism/dystonia and the separate structural, mechanical and pain phenomena in the feet.

#### F. Decision point

Future management may change after confirmation, but analysis of post-confirmation decisions is outside the current record period.

#### G. Expert question

What aspects of the earlier phenotype and treatment response are expected in PRKN disease, and which foot-pain propositions remain independent of the genetic diagnosis?

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact / Expert question

#### I. Notes

This is a temporal-control finding: later confirmation must not be back-projected as earlier certainty.

---


## 3B. Pass 3 — Retrospective Reading

**Pass scope:** This pass reads earlier entries in light of later evidence while preserving the rule that **retrospective coherence is not contemporaneous knowledge**. It asks whether later treatment responses, later classifications, later genetics, later symptom patterns, and later explanatory documents support, weaken, clarify, or merely reorganise earlier propositions. It does not treat later success as proof that the same mechanism was known earlier, and it does not convert later confirmation into earlier certainty.

### Finding [F-038]: 2026 PRKN confirmation retrospectively validates the 2017 PARK2 hypothesis without converting it into 2017 certainty

**Date / period:**  
2017 viewed from 2026

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Retrospective

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Contemporaneous vs later-attributed rationale

#### A. Case fact

In 2017 Neurology considered an autosomal-recessive young-onset Parkinsonian disorder, and Prof Healy recorded that the patient “probably has Park II” and recommended testing. In February 2026 genetic testing confirmed two pathogenic Parkin/PRKN variants and attributed the Parkinsonism and dystonia to compound heterozygous Parkin mutations.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-001128–CLIN-001156; CLIN-001520–CLIN-001525; CLIN-001742–CLIN-001749; CLIN-003220–CLIN-003229.

#### B. Record fact

The same broad genetic proposition appears first as a 2017 hypothesis and only much later as a confirmed result.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, cited above.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The 2026 result materially strengthens the historical accuracy of the 2017 PARK2/PRKN suspicion. It does not permit the 2026 certainty to be back-projected into 2017, when the record still used provisional language and retained other differential possibilities.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The 2017 record expressly considered multiple possible Parkinsonian forms and stated uncertainty about the exact disease form.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether obtaining the genetic confirmation materially earlier would have altered symptomatic management, treatment timing, or prognosis counselling.

#### F. Decision point

Retrospectively, the 2017 recommendation for PARK2 testing becomes a material historical junction. Whether the testing should have been completed earlier is not answered by the later result alone.

#### G. Expert question

Would confirmed biallelic PRKN disease in 2017–2018 have been expected to change the reasonable choice, timing, formulation, or dose of symptomatic therapy?

#### H. Status

Supported proposition / Retrospective clarification

#### I. Notes

The later result validates the hypothesis; it does not establish that the hypothesis was already proven.

---

### Finding [F-039]: PRKN confirmation narrows the neurological aetiology but does not retrospectively identify the generator of the structural foot pain

**Date / period:**  
2017–2026

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Retrospective

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Causal-scope control

#### A. Case fact

The 2026 genetic letter attributes the Parkinsonism and dystonia to PRKN mutations. The 2017 specialist record separately documented pes planus, hammertoes, corns/calluses/burning and stated that their relationship to the neurological syndrome was “Not clear.” Podiatry subsequently documented recurrent focal callus/HD, toe clawing, pronation, pressure sites, offloading and repeated debridement.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-001119–CLIN-001149; CLIN-002864–CLIN-002988; CLIN-003227–CLIN-003229.

#### B. Record fact

The later genetic result answers the disease-form question for Parkinsonism/dystonia but does not contain a finding about the origin of the recurrent focal foot lesions or every pain episode.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-003227–CLIN-003229.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The genetic result can retrospectively strengthen attribution of dystonic/Parkinsonian motor phenomena to PRKN disease. It cannot silently extend to a conclusion that the structural corn/callus pathology, the pain generated at those sites, or every neuropathic-type symptom was itself genetically caused.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Repeated podiatry findings establish genuine local structural/mechanical pathology.

#### E. What remains unresolved

How much of the painful foot syndrome arose from local pathology, abnormal loading/posture, dystonia, neurological state modulation, neuropathic processes, or combinations of these.

#### F. Decision point

None created by the 2026 result for the earlier record; the result changes retrospective interpretation, not the historical sequence of decisions.

#### G. Expert question

What components of the historical foot syndrome can properly be attributed to PRKN-associated dystonia or motor state, as distinct from structural pain generation or other mechanisms?

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact / Unresolved proposition

#### I. Notes

This finding applies the rule that evidence resolving one causal proposition must not automatically resolve adjacent propositions.

---

### Finding [F-040]: The reported 2025 Sinemet CR response retrospectively strengthens a medication-state modulation proposition for the foot pain

**Date / period:**  
2025, read against 2017–2025

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Retrospective

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Dose-formulation-timing-coverage / Treatment response does not prove mechanism

#### A. Case fact

The 2025 clinic documented severe morning foot pain, a long overnight interval after the last evening Sinemet, and relief once Parkinson medication reached the system. A bedtime Sinemet CR trial was then planned. The later March 2025 complaint response stated that Sinemet CR had provided profound relief, and the April Quality and Patient Safety letter described it as effective for the foot pain.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-002776–CLIN-002800; CLIN-003243–CLIN-003269; CLIN-003282–CLIN-003284.

#### B. Record fact

The later improvement followed a change in **formulation and overnight coverage**, not merely a simple increase in a daytime immediate-release dose.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-002793–CLIN-002800; CLIN-003266–CLIN-003269.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Retrospectively, the response materially strengthens the proposition that at least part of the foot-pain experience was modifiable by dopaminergic medication state and temporal coverage. It does not prove the original pain generator or establish that the same intervention would necessarily have produced the same result years earlier.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The patient had materially reduced pregabalin before the 2025 deterioration; structural foot pathology was longstanding; and the record itself continued to consider non-Parkinsonian pain mechanisms.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The mechanism of the CR-associated benefit and the relative contribution of improved overnight motor state, dystonia/posture, pain modulation, altered loading, pregabalin state, or other factors.

#### F. Decision point

The later success makes the earlier appearance of morning and OFF-patterned symptoms more important to review, but does not itself establish an earlier breach or missed treatment indication.

#### G. Expert question

What can the magnitude and timing of the Sinemet CR response legitimately establish about mechanism, and what cannot it establish?

#### H. Status

Supported proposition

#### I. Notes

Retrospective treatment success is evidence, but not a counterfactual answer.

---

### Finding [F-041]: Later levodopa-related pain evidence increases the retrospective significance of the 2017 procyclidine withdrawal episode

**Date / period:**  
2017 viewed from 2023–2025

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Retrospective

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Medication-state trace / Treatment response does not prove mechanism

#### A. Case fact

During the planned 2017 procyclidine interruption, contemporaneous patient evidence recorded increasing tension, restless feet and a deteriorating walk; the 2018 Neurology note recorded that the first thing changing rapidly during the interruption was return of the outside-left-foot/corn phenomenon. Years later the record explicitly described foot/leg pain and shuffling as OFF-related and documented relief in the feet when Sinemet reached the system.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-001782–CLIN-001790; CLIN-001914–CLIN-001925; CLIN-002574–CLIN-002581; CLIN-002780–CLIN-002800.

#### B. Record fact

Two different neurological medication periods, separated by years, contain evidence that the foot-related symptom complex changed with neurological treatment state.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, cited above.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Later levodopa-state evidence makes the early procyclidine episode harder to treat retrospectively as an isolated or irrelevant foot event. The safe proposition remains limited: the foot symptom complex was neurologically modifiable. The record does not establish that procyclidine and levodopa acted through the same mechanism or that the exact same symptom was being measured each time.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Podiatry continued to document focal structural pathology, and the 2017 specialist formulation had expressly separated leg tension from corns/calluses/pain in the patient's own account.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Which component of the foot syndrome changed with medication state and whether that component was pain generation, posture/loading, pain amplification, or another neurological manifestation.

#### F. Decision point

Retrospectively, the 2017 withdrawal episode becomes an evidential junction that later medication-state findings can legitimately illuminate, but not rewrite.

#### G. Expert question

How much evidential weight should be assigned to concordant symptom-state changes across different neurological medications and different years?

#### H. Status

Supported proposition / Documentary tension

#### I. Notes

Convergence across time strengthens association, not proof of mechanism.

---

### Finding [F-042]: The 2022 morning “warm-up” foot pattern becomes more salient after the 2025 overnight-coverage evidence

**Date / period:**  
2022 viewed from 2025

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Retrospective

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Dose-formulation-timing-coverage / Observation-window

#### A. Case fact

In 2022 the record stated that most mornings the feet were sore and took about 20–25 minutes to “warm up.” In 2025 the patient described severe pain on first getting out of bed, relief after Parkinson medication reached the system, and an overnight gap from the last evening Sinemet; Sinemet CR was then used to extend overnight coverage and was later reported to provide profound relief.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-002382–CLIN-002386; CLIN-002776–CLIN-002800; CLIN-003266–CLIN-003269.

#### B. Record fact

The 2022 morning pattern predates the explicit 2025 formulation of overnight dopaminergic under-coverage as a testable proposition.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, cited above.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Later evidence makes the 2022 morning pattern a retrospectively relevant clue to possible medication-state or coverage dependence. It does not establish that this was the meaning contemporaneously assigned in 2022 or that the warm-up phenomenon was necessarily pharmacological rather than mechanical, activity-related, or mixed.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The 2022 description specifically referred to corns and calluses, and no ON/OFF examination or medication-timing experiment is documented in that entry.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether the 2022 morning improvement reflected medication onset, mechanical warm-up/loading, natural symptom variation, or more than one process.

#### F. Decision point

The retrospective question is whether the 2022 pattern, combined with later evidence as it accumulated, should have led to earlier testing of timing or coverage. That is not answered by hindsight alone.

#### G. Expert question

At what evidential threshold would a movement-disorders clinician reasonably test overnight or early-morning levodopa coverage in a patient with this pattern and dyskinesia sensitivity?

#### H. Status

Supported proposition / Retrospective clarification

#### I. Notes
