The later CR response increases relevance; it does not create contemporaneous knowledge in 2022.

---

### Finding [F-043]: The later OFF-related account does not erase the conflicting March 2023 self-withdrawal evidence

**Date / period:**  
2023 viewed from 2025

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Retrospective

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Medication-state trace / Non-monotonic treatment response

#### A. Case fact

In March 2023 the patient reported severe debilitating bilateral foot/leg pain despite high-dose pregabalin and said that after stopping Parkinson medications for ten days he perceived no Parkinsonian difference and believed the pain was not Parkinson-related. Later 2023 records described pain/shuffling worsening when Sinemet had not been taken and treated the symptoms as OFF-related. By 2025 the record described reliable foot relief when Sinemet reached the system and major benefit from Sinemet CR.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-002637–CLIN-002683; CLIN-002574–CLIN-002581; CLIN-002780–CLIN-002800; CLIN-003243–CLIN-003269.

#### B. Record fact

The longitudinal record contains materially different medication-state observations rather than one monotonic or continuously consistent account.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, cited above.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Later OFF-related and CR-response evidence strengthens a dopaminergic-modulation proposition but cannot make the March 2023 ten-day withdrawal report disappear. The conflict itself remains evidentially important and may reflect differences in symptom state, medication combinations, timing, observation quality, adaptation, or other factors that the record does not fully resolve.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The GP's March 2023 referral also recalled an earlier pain episode settling with better Parkinson control, so even the March 2023 documentary state contained competing evidence.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Why the 2023 self-withdrawal appeared neutral while later OFF/ON relationships were described more clearly.

#### F. Decision point

The conflict justified reassessment rather than retrospective selection of only the evidence that fits the later outcome.

#### G. Expert question

How should a ten-day self-withdrawal report be weighed against later repeated OFF/ON symptom reports and a later formulation response?

#### H. Status

Documentary tension

#### I. Notes

Retrospective coherence must preserve discordant natural experiments.

---

### Finding [F-044]: Later neurological treatment response does not displace the independent structural evidence from Podiatry

**Date / period:**  
2017–2019 viewed from 2025–2026

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Retrospective

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Causal-scope control

#### A. Case fact

Podiatry repeatedly documented left fifth-MTPJ callus/HD, clawed toes, pronating foot type, focal pain, pressure/offloading sites, toe props, insoles and repeated debridement. Later neurological records documented medication-state effects on foot symptoms and pain.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-002864–CLIN-002988; CLIN-003019–CLIN-003081; CLIN-002574–CLIN-002581; CLIN-002780–CLIN-002800.

#### B. Record fact

The structural observations and the later medication-response observations address overlapping but non-identical propositions.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, cited above.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

A later dopaminergic response can strengthen a proposition about modulation of pain or motor/postural state without negating the existence of focal structural pathology. Conversely, local structural pathology does not negate demonstrated neurological state sensitivity.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The record contains both strands repeatedly; neither is documentary absence.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The causal contribution of each strand to particular episodes of pain and disability.

#### F. Decision point

Retrospective synthesis should preserve a multi-factor model unless external expertise justifies a narrower conclusion.

#### G. Expert question

Can the record support a clinically defensible decomposition of structural generator, mechanical consequence, dystonic contribution and central/non-motor pain modulation?

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact / Supported proposition

#### I. Notes

This is a scope finding, not a clinical attribution.

---

### Finding [F-045]: The 2025 statement that Sinemet had helped pain “for quite some time” is supported by 2023 evidence but its reach back to 2021 is not established

**Date / period:**  
2025 claim reaching back to 2021–2023

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Retrospective

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Contemporaneous vs later-attributed rationale

#### A. Case fact

The March 2025 complaint response stated that the treating team had had a clear understanding “for quite some time” that Sinemet helped pain. The 2021 first-levodopa note, however, frames initiation as treatment of undertreatment and replacement of Kemadrin because of ocular-pressure concerns; it does not state a chronic-foot-pain rationale. By later 2023 the record expressly documents the patient's use of Stalevo “for pain,” clinician discussion of OFF-related pain amplification, and a nurse assessment that leg pain and shuffling appeared OFF-related.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-002335–CLIN-002353; CLIN-002470–CLIN-002474; CLIN-002574–CLIN-002581; CLIN-003247–CLIN-003264.

#### B. Record fact

The phrase “for quite some time” is a 2025 retrospective description whose earliest clearly located contemporaneous support for a specific levodopa-pain relationship is in 2023, not in the 2021 initiation rationale.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, cited above.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The later statement is **partly supported** but its temporal reach should be bounded. It can safely describe an established understanding by 2023; the cited record does not establish that pain relief was a reason for first introducing Sinemet in 2021.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The 2021 note contains a clear contemporaneous rationale unrelated to foot pain, while the patient was already receiving Sinemet by the time later pain-state observations emerged.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether there are uncited or missing contemporaneous records between late 2021 and 2023 that documented a levodopa-pain relationship earlier than the located 2023 material.

#### F. Decision point

The historical question is not whether Sinemet eventually helped pain, but when that proposition became sufficiently established to influence management.

#### G. Expert question

None needed to classify the documentary timing; clinical significance of that timing remains an expert issue.

#### H. Status

Retrospective reconstruction / Partly supported

#### I. Notes

A later accurate summary can still overextend temporally.

---

### Finding [F-046]: The 2025 account of pregabalin as partly levodopa-sparing is not visible as the original initiation rationale

**Date / period:**  
2023 initiation viewed from 2025

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Retrospective

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Contemporaneous vs later-attributed rationale

#### A. Case fact

The March 2023 referral and Parkinson's nurse email state that pregabalin/Lyrica had been started by the GP at 25 mg twice daily for severe burning/neuropathic-type foot/leg pain and then escalated. In the 2025 clinic, avoidance of further Parkinson-medication dose increase was explicitly given as a reason to consider increasing Lyrica. The later complaint response stated that Lyrica had been “introduced for the neuropathic pain, of whatever cause, in part to try to spare further Sinemet increase.”

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-002637–CLIN-002683; CLIN-002788–CLIN-002800; CLIN-003265–CLIN-003268.

#### B. Record fact

The levodopa-sparing rationale is visible as a later management function, but is not located in the contemporaneous record of the original GP initiation.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, cited above.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The 2025 statement may accurately describe a function pregabalin later acquired within the treatment strategy. It does not, on the cited record, establish that levodopa-sparing caused the original prescription.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The initiation record gives a direct alternative contemporaneous reason: severe burning/neuropathic-type pain.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether any separate contemporaneous communication at initiation also discussed levodopa-sparing but is absent from the compiled record.

#### F. Decision point

The distinction matters when reconstructing why pregabalin entered management versus why it was later continued or increased.

#### G. Expert question

None needed for the documentary distinction; the clinical reasonableness of later levodopa-sparing use is an expert question.

#### H. Status

Retrospective reconstruction

#### I. Notes

Original initiation rationale and later management function must remain separate propositions.

---

### Finding [F-047]: The 2025 statement that responsiveness was “known” is stronger than the 2017 contemporaneous language

**Date / period:**  
2017 viewed from 2025–2026

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Retrospective

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Contemporaneous vs later-attributed rationale

#### A. Case fact

The 2025 complaint response states that “we have known” the patient would be Sinemet or dopamine-agonist responsive. In 2017 the treating neurologist wrote that it was **possible** he would do very well with levodopa. Prof Healy later in 2017 suspected PARK2, recorded dramatic improvement with procyclidine/rasagiline, and noted that PARK2 patients can manage well for long periods with relatively low doses of dopamine replacement therapy while being dyskinesia-sensitive. Later dopamine-agonist and levodopa responses provided actual response evidence.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-001601–CLIN-001612; CLIN-001742–CLIN-001749; CLIN-002114–CLIN-002158; CLIN-002335–CLIN-002353; CLIN-003260–CLIN-003268.

#### B. Record fact

The later wording compresses an earlier **prediction/expectation** and later observed responses into a stronger continuous statement of knowledge.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, cited above.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The later treatment history retrospectively supports the earlier expectation of dopaminergic responsiveness. It does not make “known” an exact synonym for the 2017 contemporaneous wording “possible.”

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The 2017 treatment strategy deliberately proceeded under uncertainty about exact disease form and future response, and the record anticipated possible regret about deferral rather than asserting a proven response before levodopa was used.

#### E. What remains unresolved

How much confidence clinicians reasonably had in likely levodopa response before it was directly tested.

#### F. Decision point

This distinction is relevant to any later account of what was known when levodopa was deferred.

#### G. Expert question

What degree of expected levodopa responsiveness would ordinarily follow from the 2017 phenotype and PARK2 suspicion, before genetic confirmation or direct levodopa exposure?

#### H. Status

Retrospective reconstruction / Partly supported

#### I. Notes

Later correctness of a prediction does not alter the contemporaneous degree of certainty with which it was expressed.

---

### Finding [F-048]: The later explanation for caution with levodopa is materially supported by contemporaneous records

**Date / period:**  
2017–2025

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Retrospective

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Contemporaneous vs later-attributed rationale

#### A. Case fact

The 2025 complaint response attributes slow Sinemet escalation to dyskinesia and other dopaminergic risks in a young patient. The 2017 treatment plan already recorded concern that very young patients could develop dyskinesia rapidly and be more susceptible to impulse-control problems; 2019 records document dyskinesia and behavioural/impulsivity problems on higher-dose Neupro; and the 2021 first-Sinemet period records early chorea and a desire to keep the dose minimal.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-001166–CLIN-001180; CLIN-001601–CLIN-001612; CLIN-002114–CLIN-002158; CLIN-002335–CLIN-002353; CLIN-003245–CLIN-003268.

#### B. Record fact

Unlike some other retrospective rationales, this later explanation has clear earlier documentary counterparts predating the complaint.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, cited above.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The record supports that dyskinesia/impulse-control risk and long-term caution were genuine contemporaneous elements of the treatment strategy, rather than reasons appearing only after the complaint.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The same 2017 record also anticipated that levodopa might work very well and that later regret about not starting it earlier was possible; later benefit therefore does not by itself settle the reasonableness of the duration of deferral.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether the degree and duration of caution remained clinically proportionate as the symptom pattern and treatment-response evidence evolved.

#### F. Decision point

The later explanation is historically supported as a rationale, but the standard-of-care assessment at each later decision point remains external to documentary analysis.

#### G. Expert question

Was the continued balance between symptomatic benefit and dyskinesia/ICD risk reasonable at each stage from 2017 to 2025?

#### H. Status

Supported retrospective account

#### I. Notes

Historical support for a rationale is distinct from expert endorsement of the decision made under that rationale.

---

### Finding [F-049]: A broad retrospective statement that management was “about Parkinson's” cannot be used to resolve the foot-causation question

**Date / period:**  
2017 viewed from 2025

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Retrospective

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Causal-scope control

#### A. Case fact

The 2025 complaint response states that from the outset the majority of management had been about the Parkinson's disorder. That is broadly consistent with the extensive neurological investigation and treatment strategy begun in 2017. However, the same 2017 specialist record expressly stated that the relationship between the corns/calluses/burning and the neurological disorder was unclear, while Podiatry separately treated structural foot pathology.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-001128–CLIN-001180; CLIN-002864–CLIN-002988; CLIN-003253–CLIN-003268.

#### B. Record fact

A statement about the **dominant management context** is not the same proposition as a statement about the **cause of a particular symptom or lesion**.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, cited above.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The later statement can be accepted as a broad description of management emphasis without treating it as evidence that the foot-pain mechanism had been understood as Parkinsonian from the outset.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The 2017 “Not clear” wording and the separate Podiatry pathway directly qualify any stronger retrospective causal reading.

#### E. What remains unresolved

How the foot problem was functionally allocated between Neurology and Podiatry over time and whether later medication-state evidence prompted adequate re-synthesis.

#### F. Decision point

Reserved for later referral-pathway and management-plan passes.

#### G. Expert question

None required for the documentary scope distinction.

#### H. Status

Retrospective reconstruction / Partly supported

#### I. Notes

Management focus and causal attribution must not be collapsed.

---

### Finding [F-050]: The present recollection that the outside-left-foot pain was the first symptom to return during procyclidine withdrawal is partly corroborated but remains more specific than the surviving 2017 email

**Date / period:**  
2017 episode reconstructed later

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Retrospective

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Contemporaneous vs retrospective evidence

#### A. Case fact

The present patient recollection says that the focal outside-left-foot pain was the first pain to return when procyclidine was withdrawn and that it persisted until 2025. The surviving October 2017 email contemporaneously records increasing tension, restless feet and gait deterioration during withdrawal. The April 2018 Neurology note records that the first thing noticed changing rapidly was return “on the foot, of the outside left corn.”

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-001782–CLIN-001790; CLIN-001803–CLIN-001808; CLIN-001914–CLIN-001919.

#### B. Record fact

The later recollection is not wholly unsupported: a treating-clinician note from 2018 independently records the “first thing”/outside-left-foot sequence. The direct 2017 email, however, does not itself specify that focal pain was the first returning symptom or establish uninterrupted persistence to 2025.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, cited above.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The precise “first pain to return” proposition can be classified as partly supported retrospective reconstruction rather than either accepted wholesale or dismissed as unsupported.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The 2017 email uses broader language about restless feet and worsening gait; later years contain variable descriptions of pain severity and treatment response.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether the same focal pain was continuously present from the withdrawal episode to 2025, and how its intensity varied over that period.

#### F. Decision point

None; this is a source-weight and temporal-reach finding.

#### G. Expert question

None required for the documentary classification.

#### H. Status

Retrospective reconstruction / Partly supported

#### I. Notes

Near-contemporaneous corroboration increases weight without converting later recollection into a 2017 verbatim fact.

---

### Finding [F-051]: Retrospective coherence is strongest for diagnosis and medication-state modulation, but remains incomplete for the foot-pain mechanism

**Date / period:**  
2017–2026

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Retrospective

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Causal-scope control / Treatment response does not prove mechanism

#### A. Case fact

Across the full record, later PRKN confirmation strongly supports the early genetic Parkinsonism hypothesis; repeated medication-state observations and the later Sinemet CR response support neurological modulation of at least part of the foot symptom experience; and repeated Podiatry records independently establish structural/mechanical pathology.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-001128–CLIN-001149; CLIN-001742–CLIN-001749; CLIN-001782–CLIN-001790; CLIN-002864–CLIN-002988; CLIN-002574–CLIN-002581; CLIN-002780–CLIN-002800; CLIN-003227–CLIN-003269.

#### B. Record fact

The later record makes some earlier propositions more coherent but does not collapse the separate evidential tracks into one demonstrated mechanism.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, cited above.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

A defensible retrospective synthesis is that the Parkinsonism/dystonia diagnosis became genetically confirmed and the painful foot syndrome showed repeated neurological medication-state sensitivity, while the underlying generator and contribution of structural pathology remained incompletely resolved. A cleaner single-cause story would exceed the documents.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Structural lesions, variable pregabalin response, the March 2023 self-withdrawal report, and changing pain descriptions all qualify a unitary neurological explanation.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The causal decomposition of the foot syndrome and the counterfactual question of whether different earlier treatment would have produced the later outcome.

#### F. Decision point

Retrospective synthesis identifies questions for later management-plan, causal-proposition, treatment-response and expert passes; it does not answer them.

#### G. Expert question

What causal and counterfactual conclusions, if any, can properly be drawn from the converging but non-identical later evidence?

#### H. Status

Supported proposition / Documentary tension

#### I. Notes

This is the bounded conclusion of Pass 3.

---


## 3C. Pass 4 — Document-Architecture Reading

**Pass scope:** This pass examines how the documents themselves are constructed: diagnosis fields, headings, addenda, copied-forward material, source-export duplication, layered annotations, administrative timestamps, partial reproductions, and retrospective quotation. It asks what the document's physical or electronic structure can safely establish and what it cannot. The governing control is that **physical placement in a document does not necessarily establish chronology of thought**, and categorical or compressed fields must be read alongside the narrative that qualifies them.

### Finding [F-052]: The July 2017 categorical diagnosis field is more definite than the surrounding specialist narrative

**Date / period:**  
July 2017

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Document architecture

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Document structure / Temporal reach

#### A. Case fact

The discharge summary contains a structured **Primary Diagnosis** field reading “Parkinsons Disease.” In the same source, the longer specialist narrative describes “Young Onset Parkinsonsm,” asks whether this is young-onset Parkinson's disease, retains multiple alternative Parkinsonian/synucleinopathy possibilities, and states that the leg/foot symptoms including corns, calluses and burning may have been separate or related to a secondary peripheral neuropathy and were “Not clear.”

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-000935–CLIN-000936; CLIN-001128–CLIN-001149.

#### B. Record fact

A categorical structured field and a qualified narrative coexist in the same documentary episode. The field compresses the syndrome into an operational diagnosis label, while the narrative preserves uncertainty about subtype and about the relation of the foot symptom complex to the neurological disorder.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, cited above.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The diagnosis field can establish that “Parkinsons Disease” was entered as the discharge diagnosis. It cannot, by itself, establish that every diagnostic or causal question discussed in the narrative had been resolved to the same degree of certainty.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The DaTscan was markedly abnormal and the neurological syndrome was sufficiently established to drive treatment. The architectural point does not weaken the existence of a Parkinsonian disorder; it limits what the categorical field can prove about subtype and foot causation.

#### E. What remains unresolved

How much operational diagnostic coding should be taken as evidence of contemporaneous causal closure beyond the proposition actually encoded.

#### F. Decision point

None created by document architecture alone; the finding changes evidential weight rather than the historical event.

#### G. Expert question

None required for the documentary proposition.

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact / Documentary tension

#### I. Notes

This is a paradigmatic example of why structured diagnosis fields should not be read in isolation from the narrative surrounding them.

---

### Finding [F-053]: The 13 July 2017 DaTscan addendum is physically appended to reasoning that began before the scan result existed

**Date / period:**  
July 2017

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Document architecture

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Temporal reach / Addendum chronology

#### A. Case fact

The long 2017 specialist document contains counselling and treatment reasoning, followed by an “ADDENDUM at Transcription finalisation 2017-07-13th_21:41hrs” reporting that the DaTscan had now been completed and was abnormal.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-001583–CLIN-001629; CLIN-001629–CLIN-001638.

#### B. Record fact

The document's final physical form places pre-existing reasoning and a later test-result addendum within one continuous source page. The addendum itself identifies its later time of entry.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The final document can establish that the DaTscan result was incorporated by 13 July. It should not be read as though the result necessarily informed every sentence of reasoning physically appearing above it when that reasoning had already been developed before the addendum.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The clinicians expected the DaTscan to be abnormal, so some pre-scan reasoning anticipated its direction. Anticipation is still distinct from possession of the validated result.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The exact drafting time of every preceding sentence is not recoverable from the compiled document alone.

#### F. Decision point

The DaTscan result itself was a clinical evidential event; the addendum architecture controls when it may be treated as available.

#### G. Expert question

None required for the documentary proposition.

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact

#### I. Notes

Physical sequence in the final document is not equivalent to contemporaneous sequence of thought.

---

### Finding [F-054]: The 2017 document places Podiatry referral and foot issues inside a Parkinson's discharge framework without thereby resolving their cause

**Date / period:**  
July 2017

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Document architecture

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Causal scope / Referral-purpose control

#### A. Case fact

The discharge material lists Parkinson's disease as the primary diagnosis and separately records that a Podiatry referral had been sent to review foot issues reported by the patient. Elsewhere in the same specialist material, the relationship of corns/calluses/burning to the neurological syndrome is explicitly uncertain.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-001645–CLIN-001653; CLIN-001142–CLIN-001149.

#### B. Record fact

The foot referral is documentarily nested inside a neurological discharge episode. Nesting and proximity can make the foot issue appear incorporated into one unified diagnosis even though the narrative expressly retains causal uncertainty.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The document proves that Neurology arranged/recorded Podiatry review during Parkinsonian management. It does not prove, merely from that placement, whether Podiatry was being asked to treat a manifestation, investigate causation, or resolve the neurological relationship.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The patient had objectively documented structural foot abnormalities, and later Podiatry records show local treatment. Those facts are compatible with adjunctive care inside a broader neurological pathway.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The precise clinical question transmitted with the original Podiatry referral.

#### F. Decision point

Referral-purpose analysis belongs primarily to the later referral-pathway pass.

#### G. Expert question

None required for the architectural proposition.

#### H. Status

Unresolved proposition / Documentary tension

#### I. Notes

Co-location in a discharge summary must not be converted into causal closure.

---

### Finding [F-055]: The GP source export repeats entries, but repeated layout is not independent corroboration

**Date / period:**  
2017 GP record export

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Document architecture

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Evidential independence

#### A. Case fact

The GP source export reproduces numerous clinical and administrative entries more than once.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-000809–CLIN-000814.

#### B. Record fact

The source itself identifies the repetitions as export/document-format behaviour and notes repeated page headers and interrupted entries.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Two identical appearances of the same exported entry remain one underlying documentary event unless a genuinely separate source or observation is shown. Counting duplicates as corroboration would artificially increase apparent evidential weight.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Separate GP entries on different dates can, of course, provide independent longitudinal evidence. The control applies to duplicated reproductions of the same entry.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Nothing material for this architectural point.

#### F. Decision point

None.

#### G. Expert question

None.

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact

#### I. Notes

This control should be applied anywhere source-system export formatting duplicates content.

---

### Finding [F-056]: A 2019 Neurology section carries forward substantial 2017 narrative, so repeated old text is not a new 2019 observation

**Date / period:**  
2019 record containing 2017 material

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Document architecture

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Inherited framing / Evidential independence

#### A. Case fact

Under the 2019 Neurology material, a heading “CLINIC NOVEMEBR 2019” is followed shortly by text stating that the patient “was seen by our team and myself at Neurology Consults in AMU on Monday 2017 07 03rd,” after which the long 2017 history, differential and management explanation is reproduced.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-002167–CLIN-002220 and following reproduced 2017 material.

#### B. Record fact

The document architecture mixes a 2019 section with copied-forward or reproduced historical 2017 content. The historical text retains value as evidence of what the record says, but its appearance under a later section heading does not make it a new 2019 examination, diagnosis, or independently repeated rationale.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Later physical repetition can show persistence or reuse of framing, but not independent corroboration unless the later note clearly adds a new observation or renewed decision.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The 2019 section also contains genuinely current material about Neupro response, adverse effects, and management. Those new observations should be separated from carried-forward historical text.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Which portions were intentionally copied forward verbatim versus re-entered from prior material cannot always be determined from the compiled text alone.

#### F. Decision point

The 2019 current-treatment decisions remain real; the copied 2017 narrative should not inflate the evidential basis for them.

#### G. Expert question

None for the architectural proposition.

#### H. Status

Inherited framing / Established documentary fact

#### I. Notes

This is especially important when counting how many times a rationale was “documented.”

---

### Finding [F-057]: The 2017 Podiatry referral form is a layered document; same-page handwriting cannot all be dated to 10 April 2017

**Date / period:**  
Underlying referral received 2017-04-10; later annotations uncertain

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Document architecture

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Source fidelity / Temporal reach

#### A. Case fact

The Podiatry SAR includes a referral form stamped as received on 10 April 2017. The transcription expressly warns that the page appears layered and that the separately placed “PARKINSONS” annotation and some neurological/gait handwriting may have been added later.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-002990–CLIN-003017.

#### B. Record fact

A single physical page contains marks that cannot safely be assigned one common date of authorship. The original receipt stamp dates the underlying referral, not every annotation later visible on the page.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The form may establish that foot/gait issues were in the Podiatry pathway from April 2017, but the “PARKINSONS” annotation cannot be used as proof that Parkinson's was written on that form on 10 April unless its provenance is independently established.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

A separate GP entry dated 6 July 2017 records a telephone discussion with Podiatry and says the case would be marked as priority “as Parkinson's.” That independently dates Parkinson's framing in the pathway by July, but does not date the annotation on the April-stamped form.

#### E. What remains unresolved

When the later handwriting was added and by whom.

#### F. Decision point

The later referral-pathway pass should distinguish the April referral from July prioritisation/reframing.

#### G. Expert question

None.

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact / Source-fidelity limitation

#### I. Notes

This finding prevents a false April chronology from being created by page layout alone.

---

### Finding [F-058]: The Podiatry administrative interface separates referral, registration, and first treatment dates that a summary view could otherwise collapse

**Date / period:**  
2017 Podiatry pathway

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Document architecture

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Administrative timestamp disaggregation

#### A. Case fact

The Podiatry record interface lists **Referral Date: 10 Apr 2017**, **Registration Date: 10 Jul 2017**, and **First Treated: 14 Sep 2017** as separate fields.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-002822–CLIN-002839.

#### B. Record fact

The source-system architecture distinguishes three different workflow events. They are not interchangeable dates for one event.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

A chronology must preserve the interval between referral, registration and treatment. Using one of these dates as a proxy for the others would distort when the service actually received, registered, and clinically saw the patient.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

None. The record itself explicitly separates the fields.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The operational reason for the three-month referral-to-first-treatment interval is not established by these fields alone.

#### F. Decision point

Referral-pathway analysis may later assess what happened during the interval.

#### G. Expert question

None for the architectural point.

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact

#### I. Notes

Administrative metadata can be clinically important when pathway delay or transmission is being reconstructed.

---

### Finding [F-059]: The 2026-produced Podiatry appointment letters contain historical appointment dates and must not be mistaken for contemporaneous 2017–2019 letters

**Date / period:**  
2026 documents referring to 2017–2019 appointments

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Document architecture

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Document-generation date vs event date

#### A. Case fact

The Podiatry SAR contains appointment-letter pages bearing a letter date of 14 August 2026 while listing appointment dates in September and October 2017, January, February, March, June and December 2018, and March 2019.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-003123–CLIN-003145; CLIN-003146–CLIN-003214.

#### B. Record fact

The letter-generation date and the historical appointment date are different temporal fields within the same generated document.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

These pages corroborate the system's recorded historical appointments, but their 2026 production date prevents them from being treated automatically as surviving contemporaneous appointment letters actually sent on those earlier dates.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The treatment notes independently document attendance on the historical dates, so the underlying appointments are supported by other contemporaneous clinical entries.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether equivalent appointment letters were originally issued at the time and, if so, their exact original wording.

#### F. Decision point

None.

#### G. Expert question

None.

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact / Source-fidelity limitation

#### I. Notes

This distinction matters particularly if correspondence timing or notice is later disputed.

---

### Finding [F-060]: The 2025 complaint response embeds 2017 quotations inside a later explanatory narrative; quotation does not create continuous contemporaneous reasoning

**Date / period:**  
2017 material quoted in 2025 response

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Document architecture

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Retrospective reconstruction / Temporal reach

#### A. Case fact

The 25 March 2025 complaint response quotes the 2017 statements that the patient understood why levodopa/dopamine agonists were being delayed and that later regret about not starting earlier was possible. It then summarizes the history as one strategy of knowing the disease would be dopaminergic-responsive while trying to spare those treatments.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-003260–CLIN-003268; original 2017 wording at CLIN-001601–CLIN-001612.

#### B. Record fact

The 2025 document physically juxtaposes authentic earlier quotations with a new 2025 synthesis. The quotations preserve earlier words; the surrounding continuity claim is a later act of documentary organization.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The response can accurately quote the 2017 rationale without proving that every intermediate treatment decision, pain interpretation, or later management function formed part of one unchanged contemporaneous strategy. Continuity must be tested against intervening records rather than inferred from quotation layout.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Substantial continuity in caution about dyskinesia is independently supported by 2019 and 2021 records. The architectural control therefore narrows, rather than rejects, the later synthesis.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Which elements of the 2025 summary reflect continuously documented strategy and which are later compression of distinct phases.

#### F. Decision point

No historical decision is created by the 2025 arrangement itself.

#### G. Expert question

The later complaint-response pass will assess the explanatory claims in detail; no expert judgment is needed for the architectural distinction.

#### H. Status

Retrospective reconstruction / Documentary tension

#### I. Notes

Quoted primary material and the later narrator's connective explanation have different evidential functions even when printed together.

---

### Finding [F-061]: The separately reproduced 2021 clinic excerpt is not independent corroboration of the full 2021 Neurology note

**Date / period:**  
2021 source, separately reproduced in the compiled record

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Document architecture

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Evidential independence / Source provenance

#### A. Case fact

Document 13 contains the 2021 Neurology note beginning 27 October 2021. Document 18 later presents an “Exact supplied text” excerpt beginning with the same clinic heading and opening lines and states that it was supplied directly in the project conversation.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-002325–CLIN-002368; CLIN-002809–CLIN-002817.

#### B. Record fact

The compiled record contains more than one representation of the same underlying 2021 clinical episode. One is a year-split source transcription; the other is a short separately supplied excerpt.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Agreement between the two representations can support transcription fidelity for the overlapping words, but it should not be counted as two independent clinical observations of the patient.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

If the two representations derive from genuinely distinct original copies, they may independently support document authenticity, but not independence of the clinical event itself.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The precise source relationship between all reproduced copies is not fully established in the compiled text.

#### F. Decision point

None.

#### G. Expert question

None.

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact / Evidential-independence limitation

#### I. Notes

Source multiplicity and event multiplicity must not be conflated.

---

### Finding [F-062]: Year-split Neurology headings do not create exact clinic dates where the source extract lacks them

**Date / period:**  
2017–2025 year-split Neurology material

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Document architecture

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Source fidelity / Date precision

#### A. Case fact

Multiple year-split Neurology sections state in their preparation note that where the source extract did not print an exact clinic date, year assignment is based on internal chronological context and neighbouring dated entries.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, e.g. CLIN-001845; CLIN-002328; CLIN-002373; CLIN-002417; CLIN-002693; CLIN-002762.

#### B. Record fact

The compiled record's section heading may be more precise about **year placement** than the underlying extract is about **exact date**.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

A finding may safely use the assigned year where the compilation explains its basis, but should not invent a day/month or sequence finer than the source supports. The section order is editorial organization, not automatically an original timestamp.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Some entries within the same year-split pages do print exact dates; those dates remain usable where explicitly present.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Exact dates for undated extracts.

#### F. Decision point

None.

#### G. Expert question

None.

#### H. Status

Source-fidelity limitation

#### I. Notes

This is a precision control, not a challenge to the substantive content of the entries.

---

### Finding [F-063]: The reproduced 2017 clinician-to-Healy page establishes content but not an exact original date from page order alone

**Date / period:**  
2017, exact date not visible

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Document architecture

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Provenance / Date precision

#### A. Case fact

The compiled record reproduces a clinician-to-Healy letter in which Galway asks for genetic advice and states that “Myself and Tim are keen to hold back on Levodopa insofar as maybe possible unless you'd advise otherwise?” The source-status note says the exact date is not visible on the reproduced page currently held in the project.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-001712–CLIN-001733.

#### B. Record fact

The page's placement before the dated 12 September 2017 Healy response supports a 2017 consultative sequence at a broad level, but the reproduced page itself does not carry a visible exact date.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The letter can prove that this advice request and collective levodopa-deferral stance were documented in the 2017 context. It should not be assigned an exact day or used to order same-period events more finely than the source permits.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The dated Healy response establishes that the request necessarily preceded or generated the specialist review in some form, but does not itself restore the missing date on the reproduced outgoing page.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The exact original date/header and any missing surrounding pages.

#### F. Decision point

The request for specialist advice is a genuine decision point; exact-day sequencing remains limited.

#### G. Expert question

None.

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact / Source-fidelity limitation

#### I. Notes

Compilation order should never silently substitute for absent source metadata.

---


## 3D. Pass 5 — Management-Plan Reading

**Pass scope:** This pass traces the governing management strategy independently of the diagnostic narrative. It asks what became an explicit policy, what risks justified it, what information was expected to change it, what uncertainties were treated as non-determinative, and when the policy was reconsidered. It does **not** yet perform the dedicated referral-pathway, causal-proposition, evidential-development, patient–clinician perspective, uncertainty-and-closure, treatment-response, observation-window or complaint-response passes except where prior findings must be preserved as controls.

### Finding [F-064]: The original 2017 management architecture explicitly separated immediate symptomatic treatment from deferred dopaminergic replacement

**Date / period:**  
July 2017

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Management-plan reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Prospective timing / Contemporaneous rationale

#### A. Case fact

Neurology planned clonazepam, rasagiline and a deliberately slow procyclidine titration while advising great caution with the more usual dopamine agonist or levodopa treatments. The stated concerns were the patient's very young age and the possibility of rapid dyskinesia and impulse-control/punding complications. The same plan said that sooner or later such treatments might be needed and explicitly allowed that levodopa might work very well when eventually introduced.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-001166–CLIN-001223; CLIN-001561–CLIN-001612.

#### B. Record fact

A durable management policy appears at the outset: **use other symptomatic measures first; defer levodopa/dopamine agonists because of anticipated long-term treatment risk, not because dopaminergic responsiveness was thought absent.**

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The later chronology should be read against this explicit policy. A later failure to escalate dopaminergic treatment cannot be understood solely from symptom severity; the governing plan gave long-term dyskinesia/behavioural risk independent weight.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The same 2017 plan acknowledged that levodopa could prove highly effective and that later regret about not starting it earlier was possible.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The 2017 documents do not define a precise symptom, functional, temporal or evidential threshold that would end the deferral policy.

#### F. Decision point

Adoption of the original dopaminergic-deferral strategy.

#### G. Expert question

Was the initial policy and its open-ended threshold clinically reasonable for the evidence then available?

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact

#### I. Notes

This finding concerns the architecture of management, not whether the policy was clinically optimal.

---

### Finding [F-065]: The unresolved foot question was managed in parallel rather than made a prerequisite for the core Parkinson treatment plan

**Date / period:**  
July–September 2017

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Management-plan reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Causal-scope control / Operational versus causal closure

#### A. Case fact

The July specialist assessment recorded the relationship between the corns/calluses/burning foot symptoms and the neurological disorder as unclear, while the plan nevertheless proceeded with Parkinson-focused medication, investigations, physiotherapy/occupational therapy and Podiatry referral. The discharge material states that Podiatry referral had been sent to review the reported foot issues.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-001142–CLIN-001180; CLIN-001225–CLIN-001229; CLIN-001645–CLIN-001704.

#### B. Record fact

The management architecture did not require causal closure of the foot syndrome before neurological treatment proceeded. The foot problem occupied a parallel podiatric/manifestation-management track while the Parkinsonian syndrome drove the main neurological plan.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The record can show legitimate **operational closure** for overall Parkinson management while preserving **causal non-closure** of the foot syndrome. The existence of a Podiatry plan does not itself show that the causal foot question was resolved.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The foot manifestations were clinically active and structurally documented; they were not ignored. Later medication-state effects also made their neurological relevance more salient.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether and when the foot-causation question should have been re-integrated into the core neurological management architecture.

#### F. Decision point

Allocation of the foot problem to Podiatry/adjunctive management while neurological treatment proceeded.

#### G. Expert question

At what evidential point, if any, should a parallel foot pathway have been re-synthesized into the neurological management plan?

#### H. Status

Supported proposition

#### I. Notes

The dedicated referral-pathway pass remains to be done; this finding only identifies the management allocation.

---

### Finding [F-066]: The 2017 specialist second opinion supported both PARK2 testing and cautious low-dose dopamine replacement rather than simple non-use

**Date / period:**  
12 September 2017

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Management-plan reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Prospective timing / Genetic-management separation

#### A. Case fact

Prof Healy recorded that the patient probably had PARK2, recommended testing PARK2 first and a panel if negative, and stated that patients with PARK2 tend to manage well for long periods on relatively low doses of dopamine replacement but can become quite dyskinetic if over-used.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-001734–CLIN-001759.

#### B. Record fact

The outside specialist opinion was compatible with caution but framed the therapeutic issue as **low-dose, careful dopamine replacement** rather than an absolute bar to dopaminergic therapy.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The second opinion reinforced the dyskinesia-risk rationale while also supplying a potentially relevant positive therapeutic proposition. Later management should therefore be traced for how these two parts of the advice were incorporated.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The patient was already reporting dramatic improvement on procyclidine/rasagiline, so there was no contemporaneous necessity in this letter to switch immediately to levodopa.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The record does not show a formal written re-synthesis explicitly stating how Healy's low-dose dopamine-replacement advice altered the existing Galway deferral policy.

#### F. Decision point

Post-second-opinion continuation or modification of the original strategy.

#### G. Expert question

Did the Healy advice materially change the range of reasonable management options at that time?

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact / Management implication supported

#### I. Notes

The question of whether PARK2 testing was completed then is separate from the treatment-policy question.

---

### Finding [F-067]: The 2017 procyclidine withdrawal changed the evidential state but the visible management response focused on restoring a tolerable procyclidine regimen

**Date / period:**  
September–October 2017; reviewed again April 2018

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Management-plan reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Medication-state trace / Contemporaneous rationale

#### A. Case fact

Procyclidine was withdrawn because of visual problems. During withdrawal the patient contemporaneously reported increasing tension, restless feet and gait deterioration; after reintroduction at a lower dose he later reported that the lower dose did not control all symptoms and was advised to increase it again while monitoring vision. In April 2018 the treating neurologist recorded that, when procyclidine had been stopped, the outside-left-foot/corn phenomenon returned rapidly and symptoms worsened.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-001782–CLIN-001801; CLIN-001826–CLIN-001834; CLIN-001914–CLIN-001925.

#### B. Record fact

A new medication-state signal entered the record, but the visible management response was primarily a procyclidine tolerability exercise—withdrawal, lower-dose reintroduction and re-escalation—rather than a documented redefinition of the levodopa-deferral criteria or foot-causation strategy.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The treatment-response evidence altered what was knowable about symptom modifiability, but the documentary record does not show that it became a formal trigger to reconsider the governing dopaminergic strategy at that point.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The patient had major overall benefit from procyclidine and significant visual toxicity; continuing to optimize it was a real management problem in its own right.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether the new foot/gait state-dependence should have changed the treatment hierarchy in 2017–2018.

#### F. Decision point

Reintroduction and re-escalation of procyclidine after withdrawal.

#### G. Expert question

Should this natural experiment have altered the threshold for testing a low-dose dopamine-replacement strategy?

#### H. Status

Documentary tension

#### I. Notes

The record establishes a change in evidence and a management response; it does not establish that an alternative response was required.

---

### Finding [F-068]: By 2018–2019 the governing plan evolved into a documented “tightrope” between under-treatment and dopaminergic adverse effects

**Date / period:**  
Late 2018–2019

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Management-plan reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Non-monotonic treatment response / Risk-benefit trace

#### A. Case fact

After procyclidine became less satisfactory, amantadine was tried and stopped after adverse mood effects, then Neupro was introduced and titrated. Higher doses produced choreiform/dyskinetic movement and behavioural/impulsivity concerns; the patient preferred the lower 10 mg dose despite residual stiffness/cramping. The treating neurologist described management as an “extremely difficult tight rope” and stated a preference to leave levodopa “for as long as is humanly possible.”

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-002111–CLIN-002166.

#### B. Record fact

The original abstract risk rationale had become empirically reinforced by observed dopaminergic adverse effects. Management was now explicitly governed by a balance between inadequate movement/comfort on one side and dyskinesia/compulsive effects on the other.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The continuing levodopa caution after 2018 cannot be described as resting only on theoretical concern: the record documents real dose-related problems on a dopamine agonist. At the same time, residual symptoms were knowingly accepted within that risk-management strategy.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Neupro 10 mg produced a substantially more normal appearance and some periods of excellent function; the adverse effects were most evident at higher doses.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether the adverse-effect experience on Neupro justified continued deferral of carefully dosed levodopa to the same degree.

#### F. Decision point

Maintenance of lower-dose Neupro and continued levodopa deferral.

#### G. Expert question

How transferable should dopamine-agonist adverse effects have been to decisions about low-dose levodopa?

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact / Expert question retained

#### I. Notes

The record itself recognizes that dopamine agonists and levodopa are not identical treatment experiences.

---

### Finding [F-069]: In 2021 the governing policy changed from levodopa avoidance to levodopa minimization

**Date / period:**  
September–October 2021

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Management-plan reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Contemporaneous rationale / Therapeutic-variable disaggregation

#### A. Case fact

The patient felt undertreated and was started on a very small Sinemet dose. When ocular-pressure concerns made removal of Kemadrin desirable, Sinemet was increased to compensate. The clinician then stated that the dose had risen relatively quickly, noted early chorea, and expressly did not want it to rise significantly above the existing level.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-002330–CLIN-002359.

#### B. Record fact

The management architecture did not simply end in 2021; it changed form. The earlier rule “delay levodopa” became “use levodopa, but keep exposure as minimal as possible and watch dyskinesia/ICD risk.”

#### C. Interpretive consequence

From 2021 onward, later treatment decisions should be evaluated against a **minimization** strategy rather than an **avoidance** strategy. This matters when interpreting later questions about dose, timing, formulation and coverage.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Levodopa was tolerated sufficiently to remain part of treatment, and the patient hoped it would compensate for removal of procyclidine.

#### E. What remains unresolved

What symptom threshold, if any, would justify moving beyond the minimal-dose strategy.

#### F. Decision point

First levodopa initiation and subsequent ceiling-setting.

#### G. Expert question

Was the transition from avoidance to minimization appropriately calibrated?

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact

#### I. Notes

The 2021 note does not frame first initiation as a chronic-foot-pain intervention.

---

### Finding [F-070]: By 2022–2023 levodopa had become a demand- and state-responsive management variable rather than a fixed background treatment

**Date / period:**  
2022–2023

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Management-plan reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Medication-state trace / Therapeutic-variable disaggregation

#### A. Case fact

In 2022 the record says the patient adjusted Sinemet according to expected demands such as playing darts. In 2023 the clinician described evening difficulty in terms resembling OFF Parkinsonian symptoms and discussed whether extra evening levodopa might facilitate the fourth walk, while also emphasizing that the patient was very sensitive to levodopa and at risk of chorea.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-002382–CLIN-002400; CLIN-002456–CLIN-002495.

#### B. Record fact

The plan had evolved from merely minimizing total levodopa to actively considering **when** additional dopaminergic effect might be useful for predictable functional demand or OFF periods.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

By 2023 the management question was no longer simply “levodopa or no levodopa.” Dose, timing, expected activity and symptom cycle were all becoming separate therapeutic variables.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The patient was not confident that extra evening Sinemet would help, mild choreiform movement was already present, and the clinician wanted to avoid pushing stimulation too far.

#### E. What remains unresolved

How systematically timing/formulation alternatives were tested before 2024–2025.

#### F. Decision point

Consideration, but not routine adoption, of additional evening levodopa.

#### G. Expert question

At what point should predictable OFF periods lead to structured timing/formulation trials despite dyskinesia risk?

#### H. Status

Supported proposition

#### I. Notes

This finding concerns management variables and does not prove the mechanism of the pain.

---

### Finding [F-071]: Later 2023 management explicitly integrated severe leg pain and shuffling into the dopaminergic treatment plan as OFF-related

**Date / period:**  
Later 2023, exact contact date redacted

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Management-plan reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Contemporaneous rationale / Medication-state trace

#### A. Case fact

The Parkinson's nurse recorded severe ongoing foot pain, reduced mobility and shuffling, with worse symptoms in the morning and later in the day if Sinemet had not been taken. The note states that the description appeared to show undertreatment and OFF-related leg pain/shuffling. After discussion with the consultant, morning Stalevo was started while the later Sinemet doses continued.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-002573–CLIN-002583.

#### B. Record fact

This is a clear management transition: pain and shuffling were not merely described alongside Parkinson treatment; they directly drove a dopaminergic regimen change under an explicit OFF-related working formulation.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

From this point, the record clearly supports that at least some pain/function symptoms were being managed as part of the dopaminergic coverage problem, even though the underlying pain generator remained unsettled.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Earlier in March 2023 the patient had reported no Parkinsonian difference during a ten-day self-withdrawal, and pregabalin was also materially involved in pain control.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The proportion of the pain syndrome attributable to OFF state versus structural/peripheral/other mechanisms.

#### F. Decision point

Addition of morning Stalevo.

#### G. Expert question

How much weight should this treatment response pathway carry in later management planning?

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact

#### I. Notes

The March and later-2023 evidence must both be preserved.

---

### Finding [F-072]: In 2024 the plan targeted absorption/timing before increasing dopaminergic quantity

**Date / period:**  
2024

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Management-plan reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Therapeutic-variable disaggregation

#### A. Case fact

The patient reported persistent evening limitations and toe curling despite substantial pregabalin benefit for pain. The neurologist described a balancing problem between helping the evening state and producing dyskinesia, and recommended moving the approximately 5 PM Sinemet to before food with a good volume of fluid rather than simply increasing the dose.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-002698–CLIN-002712.

#### B. Record fact

The management architecture explicitly recognized **timing/absorption** as a separate intervention from **dose escalation**.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The record shows that by 2024 the team was attempting to improve coverage while preserving the minimal-dose principle. This is important when evaluating the later controlled-release decision: formulation and timing were now active management dimensions.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The patient himself felt the medication was “spot on” overall, and dyskinesia remained visible enough to limit aggressiveness.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether the timing change materially improved the evening problem.

#### F. Decision point

Change in relation of evening Sinemet to food/fluid.

#### G. Expert question

Was timing optimization sufficient before considering formulation change?

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact

#### I. Notes

Dose ≠ timing ≠ formulation ≠ duration of coverage.

---

### Finding [F-073]: In 2025 the plan explicitly tested overnight duration of coverage with controlled-release Sinemet

**Date / period:**  
2025 clinic

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Management-plan reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Therapeutic-variable disaggregation / Medication-state trace

#### A. Case fact

The patient described severe morning foot pain after a major pregabalin reduction and stated that the feet improved once Parkinson medications reached his system. The clinician noted the overnight interval without levodopa, considered the possibility that morning severity reflected that gap, and proposed bedtime Sinemet CR while warning about dyskinesia and dreaming. The plan also retained the option of increasing pregabalin rather than simply increasing daytime Parkinson medication.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-002777–CLIN-002800.

#### B. Record fact

The governing plan now tested **duration of overnight coverage** as a distinct therapeutic proposition. This differed from the earlier questions of whether to start levodopa, how much to use, or when to take immediate-release doses.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The 2025 CR trial represents a meaningful evolution of the management architecture, not merely a higher dose of the same plan. Its later reported profound benefit must be interpreted as evidence about this new coverage strategy.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Pregabalin had been substantially reduced, creating a major confounder in the morning-pain deterioration; dyskinesia risk remained real.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether an overnight/formulation strategy would have been indicated earlier.

#### F. Decision point

Bedtime controlled-release Sinemet trial.

#### G. Expert question

At what earlier evidential threshold, if any, would overnight coverage have become a reasonable test?

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact

#### I. Notes

The later complaint response reports profound relief, but that later outcome was not available when the trial was chosen.

---

### Finding [F-074]: Genetic clarification was treated as potentially informative but not as a prerequisite for symptomatic management

**Date / period:**  
2017–2026

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Management-plan reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Genetic-management separation / Prospective timing

#### A. Case fact

In 2017 Neurology considered recessive Parkinsonism and specifically PRKN/PARK2, discussed the limited immediate management value of genetic diagnosis, and considered whether testing might assist prognostication or understanding levodopa sensitivity. Prof Healy recommended PARK2 testing. Definitive biallelic PRKN confirmation appears only in 2026.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-001493–CLIN-001560; CLIN-001734–CLIN-001759; CLIN-003220–CLIN-003229.

#### B. Record fact

Genetics was a diagnostic/prognostic management thread, but the record did not make genetic confirmation a gate that had to be passed before symptomatic treatment could proceed.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The absence of early genetic confirmation does not by itself explain levodopa deferral; the contemporaneous rationale for deferral was already stated in terms of age and treatment risk. Conversely, later PRKN confirmation may still be relevant to expert assessment of whether the strategy should have changed.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The 2017 neurologist explicitly wondered whether a genetic result could help with prognosis or levodopa sensitivity, so it was not regarded as wholly irrelevant.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether earlier confirmation would have materially altered treatment strategy.

#### F. Decision point

Whether and when to pursue genetic testing while continuing symptomatic management.

#### G. Expert question

Would confirmed PRKN disease have altered the risk-benefit architecture of treatment at the time?

#### H. Status

Supported proposition

#### I. Notes

This question remains distinct from whether testing should have occurred for diagnostic reasons alone.

---

### Finding [F-075]: The 2025 complaint response describes a durable levodopa-sparing strategy, but the contemporaneous record shows several distinct management phases

**Date / period:**  
2017–2025, reconstructed 25 March 2025

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Management-plan reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Contemporaneous rationale versus later-attributed rationale / Retrospective reconstruction

#### A. Case fact

The complaint response states that clinicians knew Parkinson's would be dopaminergic-responsive, tried to spare levodopa because of age/dyskinesia/impulse-control risks, used pregabalin partly to spare further Sinemet increase, and later escalated Sinemet CR slowly and carefully. Earlier records do support long-running caution about dopaminergic adverse effects, but they also document distinct phases: procyclidine/rasagiline first-line therapy, dopamine-agonist titration, first levodopa in 2021 because of undertreatment and Kemadrin ocular concerns, later OFF-related pain management, timing changes, and finally overnight controlled-release coverage.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-003245–CLIN-003269; compared with CLIN-001166–CLIN-001223; CLIN-002111–CLIN-002166; CLIN-002330–CLIN-002359; CLIN-002573–CLIN-002583; CLIN-002698–CLIN-002712; CLIN-002777–CLIN-002800.

#### B. Record fact

One durable theme—caution about excessive dopaminergic exposure—is well supported. But the management plan was not static: the role of levodopa, pain, pregabalin, timing and formulation changed materially over time.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The later complaint response can be used as evidence of the clinician's 2025 synthesis, but should not collapse the treatment history into one unchanged rationale. Management continuity and management evolution must both be preserved.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The long-running dyskinesia/ICD concern is genuinely continuous across multiple contemporaneous records.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether the management plan evolved at the clinically appropriate points and whether each re-synthesis was adequately explicit.

#### F. Decision point

Retrospective explanation of the treatment strategy after the Sinemet CR response.

#### G. Expert question

Did the observed management evolution remain within a reasonable range of practice as the evidential state changed?

#### H. Status

Retrospective reconstruction with substantial contemporaneous support for one durable theme

#### I. Notes

The dedicated complaint-response pass will later test the 2025 document in greater detail.

---

### Finding [F-076]: The governing treatment strategy changed in identifiable stages rather than remaining a single fixed plan

**Date / period:**  
2017–2025

**Reading method(s) used:**  
Management-plan reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):**  
Longitudinal policy trace

#### A. Case fact

Across the record, the main strategy can be traced through successive stages: (1) defer levodopa/dopamine agonists and use alternative symptomatic therapy; (2) optimize procyclidine despite visual toxicity; (3) use dopamine agonist with a “tightrope” risk-benefit framework; (4) introduce levodopa and minimize dose; (5) use levodopa in relation to demand/OFF state; (6) optimize timing/absorption; and (7) test overnight controlled-release coverage.

**Source location(s):**  
`complete-record.md`, CLIN-001166–CLIN-001223; CLIN-001782–CLIN-001925; CLIN-002111–CLIN-002166; CLIN-002330–CLIN-002359; CLIN-002382–CLIN-002400; CLIN-002456–CLIN-002495; CLIN-002573–CLIN-002583; CLIN-002698–CLIN-002712; CLIN-002777–CLIN-002800.

#### B. Record fact

The management architecture is dynamic. A single phrase such as “levodopa sparing” accurately captures one recurrent constraint but not the whole sequence of operative policies.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Any evaluation of the case should identify **which management phase was operative at the relevant date**. A decision made under the 2017 avoidance strategy cannot be analyzed as though the 2025 coverage strategy already existed, and the 2025 strategy cannot be summarized as merely continuing the 2017 plan unchanged.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The concern about dyskinesia and excessive dopaminergic stimulation remained remarkably persistent across all phases.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether each transition occurred at the clinically appropriate time.

#### F. Decision point

The sequence itself contains multiple decision points already captured in DP-003–DP-012.

#### G. Expert question

Which transitions, if any, occurred too early, too late, or within a reasonable range?

#### H. Status

Supported proposition

#### I. Notes

This is the principal Pass 5 synthesis finding.

---

### Pass 5 management-plan trace

| ID | Period | Governing policy | Main rationale | Evidence expected or capable of changing policy | What actually changed the policy | Documentary status |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| MP-001 | Jul 2017 | Use rasagiline/procyclidine; defer levodopa/dopamine agonists | Very young age; dyskinesia and ICD/punding risk | Worsening function, failure/intolerance of alternatives, later treatment need; no fixed threshold stated | Alternatives trialled; procyclidine initially highly effective | Established |
| MP-002 | Sep–Oct 2017 | Preserve procyclidine benefit while managing visual toxicity | Major physical benefit but visual adverse effects | Withdrawal/rechallenge response and tolerability | Withdrawal, lower-dose restart, then re-escalation | Established |
| MP-003 | Late 2018–2019 | Dopamine agonist at tolerable dose; continue levodopa deferral | Residual symptoms versus dyskinesia/behavioural effects | Quality of life, adverse effects, failure of available balance | Neupro dose reduced after higher-dose adverse effects | Established |
| MP-004 | 2021 | Introduce levodopa but minimize exposure | Undertreatment; need to remove Kemadrin for ocular concerns; early chorea | Symptom control versus dyskinesia/ICD | First Sinemet and cautious escalation | Established |
| MP-005 | 2022–2023 | Tailor levodopa to demand/OFF periods while limiting excess stimulation | Variable functional state; later-day problems; dyskinesia sensitivity | Demonstrated OFF pattern or functional need | Demand-based use; later Stalevo for OFF-related pain/shuffling | Supported |
| MP-006 | 2023 pain phase | Treat severe pain with pregabalin while also testing dopaminergic contribution | Severe burning pain; uncertain mechanism; dyskinesia risk | Pain response, OFF relation, tolerability | Pregabalin continuation plus Stalevo/Sinemet adjustments | Established |
| MP-007 | 2024 | Optimize timing/absorption before dose escalation | Evening limitation with dyskinesia risk | Response to pre-food timing and fluid | Evening Sinemet moved before food | Established |
| MP-008 | 2025 | Test overnight duration of dopaminergic coverage; retain pregabalin as alternative lever | Severe morning pain, long overnight gap, medication-onset relief, dyskinesia risk | Response to CR and/or pregabalin re-escalation | Bedtime Sinemet CR trial | Established |
| MP-009 | Mar 2025 retrospective synthesis | Describe overall course as careful levodopa-sparing strategy | Long-term adverse-effect risk | Historical record itself | Complaint response integrates prior phases | Retrospective reconstruction |


## 3E. Pass 6 — Referral-Pathway Reading

**Pass scope:** This pass treats each referral or transfer of clinical work as a bounded task. It asks what facts were transmitted, what question was actually asked, what the receiving service was expected to do, what it actually did, and what cannot safely be inferred from the mere existence of the referral. The controlling rule is that referral to manage a manifestation is not automatically referral to resolve its cause.

### Finding [F-077]: The original April 2017 Podiatry pathway was directed to foot manifestations and biomechanics, not an expressly framed neurological causation question

**Date / period:** 2017-04-10

**Reading method(s) used:** Referral-pathway reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Referral-purpose control; document-architecture control; causal-scope control

#### A. Case fact

The GP documented abnormal gait, bilateral foot pain, mallet toes, onychogryphosis and callus/pressure areas and planned referral to Podiatry. The surviving Podiatry referral material is stamped received 10 April 2017 and visibly identifies corn/callus, thickened nails, rearfoot/arch pain, forefoot pain, toe deformities and gait abnormality. The same physical referral page is later recognised in the compiled record as layered, so not every neurological annotation on that page can safely be dated to 10 April.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-000018–CLIN-000028; CLIN-002990–CLIN-003017.

#### B. Record fact

The surviving referral evidence shows transmission of a foot/biomechanical problem for Podiatry assessment and treatment. It does not show an express question asking Podiatry to determine whether the foot pain, structural lesions or gait abnormality were caused by a neurological disorder, peripheral neuropathy, or another process.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The fact that Podiatry later treated structural pathology cannot, by itself, be used as proof that the broader neurological causation question had been referred to Podiatry or resolved by it.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Gait abnormality was visibly part of the referral material, and later annotations include Parkinson's-related wording. Those facts establish clinical context, but the layered-document warning prevents treating every annotation as part of the original 10 April task.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The full content of any oral discussion accompanying the original referral is not visible in the surviving documentation.

#### F. Decision point

The initial Podiatry referral created a parallel structural/biomechanical pathway before the later neurological diagnosis was established.

#### G. Expert question

None required to establish the documentary referral scope.

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact with source-layering qualification

#### I. Notes

This finding addresses the referral task, not the clinical adequacy of that task.

---

### Finding [F-078]: The June 2017 Neurology/MAU pathway transmitted a broad neurological diagnostic question while carrying the foot history as context

**Date / period:** 2017-06-08 to 2017-06-29

**Reading method(s) used:** Referral-pathway reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Referral-purpose control; prospective control

#### A. Case fact

On 8 June the GP considered a Neurology opinion for the patient's gait, feet and “muscle tension.” On 12 June, after the Occupational Health doctor independently echoed concern about the gait, the GP decided to refer to Neurology before waiting for old notes. By 29 June the formal MAU referral described abnormal/festinant stiff gait, difficulty initiating movement, increased tone, brisk reflexes, worsening dexterity and work impairment and asked for assessment of a possible Parkinsonian or other underlying diagnosis.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-000064–CLIN-000111; CLIN-000190–CLIN-000235; CLIN-000847–CLIN-000884.

#### B. Record fact

This referral pathway did transmit an open neurological diagnostic question and did not merely transmit a pre-existing Parkinson's label. The foot problems were part of the history, but the formal question was broader neurological assessment rather than a specifically framed adjudication of the cause of the structural foot lesions or burning pain.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Neurology's subsequent Parkinsonian formulation can properly be read as a response to the diagnostic question that was actually referred. It does not follow that every adjacent foot-causation proposition was thereby answered.
