#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The referral expressly mentioned the abnormal gait and the patient's earlier attribution of gait to calluses/corns; therefore the feet were not absent from the neurological pathway.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether additional oral detail beyond the written referral explicitly asked Neurology to separate neurological from structural causes of foot pain is not documented.

#### F. Decision point

The Occupational Health concern changed the threshold for Neurology referral on 12 June; functional deterioration then changed the threshold again to urgent MAU assessment on 29 June.

#### G. Expert question

None needed for the documentary sequence.

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact

#### I. Notes

The later Neurology record itself preserves a separate “Not clear” proposition concerning corns/calluses/burning.

---

### Finding [F-079]: The July 2017 Podiatry prioritisation transmitted the Parkinson's diagnosis as context but not the specialist uncertainty attached to the feet

**Date / period:** 2017-07-06

**Reading method(s) used:** Referral-pathway reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Referral-purpose control; inheritance control

#### A. Case fact

The GP recorded a telephone discussion with Podiatry and stated that the case would be marked as a priority case and “as Parkinson's,” with an expected appointment in September.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-000236–CLIN-000245; Document 03 CLIN-000899–CLIN-000913.

#### B. Record fact

The surviving note establishes transmission of the Parkinson's label to Podiatry. It does not record transmission of Neurology's contemporaneous uncertainty that the corns/calluses/burning might be separate or perhaps neuropathic.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

A later Podiatry record that proceeds on a Parkinson's background cannot automatically be treated as independent confirmation that Parkinson's caused the foot pathology. The referral pathway itself may have supplied that framing.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The wording “as Parkinson's” may have been intended simply to prioritise a clinically complex patient rather than to make a causal assertion about every foot symptom.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The full oral content of the GP-Podiatry conversation is not visible.

#### F. Decision point

The new neurological diagnosis changed how the existing Podiatry referral was prioritised and labelled.

#### G. Expert question

None required to identify the documentary inheritance risk.

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact

#### I. Notes

This is a referral-framing finding, not a conclusion that the Podiatry service was biased by the framing.

---

### Finding [F-080]: The hospital Podiatry task was framed as review of foot issues while Neurology simultaneously retained causal uncertainty

**Date / period:** July 2017

**Reading method(s) used:** Referral-pathway reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Referral-purpose control; causal-scope control

#### A. Case fact

The July Neurology/discharge material records that a Podiatry referral had been sent to review issues with the feet and asks for outpatient Chiropody/Podiatry referral. The same specialist episode states that the leg symptoms including corns, calluses and burning may have been separate or perhaps related to a secondary peripheral neuropathy and records the position as “Not clear.”

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-001142–CLIN-001149; CLIN-001225–CLIN-001229; CLIN-001646–CLIN-001653.

#### B. Record fact

The task visible in the referral wording is review/management of foot issues. The surviving record does not show Podiatry being given the explicit task of resolving the neurological-versus-structural-versus-neuropathic causal question that Neurology had left open.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Podiatry's subsequent treatment within its remit can establish the presence and management of structural manifestations without constituting causal closure of the specialist Neurology question.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The receiving Podiatry service had Parkinson's context and documented gait/toe abnormalities, so its work was not isolated from the neurological history.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether a fuller referral letter or oral handover containing a causal question existed outside the surviving record.

#### F. Decision point

Neurology chose to continue Parkinson management while referring foot manifestations for parallel Podiatry care.

#### G. Expert question

Whether that division of tasks was clinically sufficient is an external referral/governance question, not a documentary conclusion.

#### H. Status

Supported proposition

#### I. Notes

This finding is an example of operational closure without causal closure.

---

### Finding [F-081]: The 2017 Healy referral asked specific genetics and levodopa-strategy questions and the response should be confined to that scope

**Date / period:** 2017 correspondence; specialist review 2017-09-12

**Reading method(s) used:** Referral-pathway reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Referral-purpose control; scope control

#### A. Case fact

The outgoing clinician-to-clinician correspondence asked Prof Daniel Healy what genetic testing might be appropriate, whether testing was reasonable and likely to help, whether he had genetic contacts, whether an in-person Dublin opinion would be useful, and expressly asked whether the Galway clinicians should continue to hold back on levodopa. Healy then recorded that the patient probably had PARK II, recommended initial PARK2 testing and a panel if negative, and stated that PARK II patients may manage well for long periods on relatively low-dose dopamine replacement but can become dyskinetic if over-used.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-001712–CLIN-001733; CLIN-001734–CLIN-001759.

#### B. Record fact

This is a referral in which the questions are unusually explicit and the response materially answers them. The surviving transcribed correspondence does not show a specific question to Healy about the cause of the corns/calluses/burning foot syndrome.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Healy's opinion is strong evidence about the genetic hypothesis and dopamine-replacement strategy within the scope asked. Silence on the foot-causation question cannot safely be treated as an endorsement, rejection or resolution of that question.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Healy examined the patient and had imaging, so his overall clinical exposure was broader than the short questions alone. The surviving partial transcription may not reproduce every topic discussed.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether the foot-causation issue was discussed orally or in portions of the original correspondence not presently transcribed.

#### F. Decision point

The referral created an opportunity for external specialist input on genetics and levodopa strategy.

#### G. Expert question

Whether the advice should have altered treatment timing is reserved for movement-disorders expertise.

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact with scope limitation

#### I. Notes

The exact date of the outgoing Galway page is not visible in the reproduced source.

---

### Finding [F-082]: Podiatry's 2017–2019 responses document treatment of manifestations within its remit rather than an express causal adjudication

**Date / period:** 2017-09-14 to 2019-03-25

**Reading method(s) used:** Referral-pathway reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Referral-purpose control; response-scope control

#### A. Case fact

Podiatry repeatedly documented callus/HD lesions, clawed toes, nail disease, tinea, tight gastrocnemius/soleus, pronating foot type and other local findings. It debrided callus, made toe props, supplied/offloaded insoles and gave stretching and antifungal advice. The September 2017 note records the patient's statement that his gait had been very bad but was no longer so.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002864–CLIN-002988.

#### B. Record fact

The receiving service's documented actions are treatment and biomechanical management of local manifestations. No explicit causal determination is visible in these treatment notes that the foot syndrome was neurological, non-neurological, neuropathic, or independent of Parkinsonism.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The Podiatry records are independent evidence that structural/mechanical pathology existed and was treated. They are not independent evidence that a broader causal question—if it was not clearly referred—was considered and excluded.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The notes document gait and toe posture and therefore contain clinically relevant neurological context; the absence of a causal conclusion should not be read as proof that no clinical consideration occurred.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Any undocumented multidisciplinary discussion or communication back to Neurology/GP beyond the visible treatment record.

#### F. Decision point

Repeated structural treatment continued as symptoms and foot lesions recurred.

#### G. Expert question

What causal implications, if any, can be drawn from the podiatric findings requires clinical expertise beyond the referral record.

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact

#### I. Notes

The referral-pathway reading preserves both the independent structural evidence and the limit of Podiatry's documented task.

---

### Finding [F-083]: The 2019 Podiatry re-referral carried stronger Parkinson-linked framing but still primarily requested foot management

**Date / period:** 2019-03

**Reading method(s) used:** Referral-pathway reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Referral-purpose control; inherited-framing control; scope control

#### A. Case fact

The 2019 Podiatry referral states that the patient had previously been seen for feet issues “in relating to increased tone and abnormal gait relating to Parkinson's disease,” that foot pain/issues had recurred, and that re-referral was sought. Visible referral reasons include callus, rearfoot/arch pain and gait abnormalities, with comments referring to young-onset Parkinson's, “clawing feet” and pain. The form's low-risk section visibly includes “No neuropathy.”

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-003083–CLIN-003120.

#### B. Record fact

By 2019 the referring document itself frames the foot problem as related to increased tone/abnormal gait/Parkinson's. The referral remains a request for Podiatry care rather than an explicit request for neurological causal adjudication or formal peripheral-neuropathy investigation.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Later Podiatry documentation may inherit a Parkinson-linked formulation from the referral itself. The “No neuropathy” risk-status tick is evidence of what the Podiatry form recorded in that context; it should not automatically be expanded into a comprehensive neurological exclusion of peripheral neuropathy.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The referral's Parkinson-linked wording may reflect a genuine clinical synthesis by the GP rather than mere copying. The record fact concerns evidential independence, not whether the framing was correct.

#### E. What remains unresolved

What assessment underlay the “No neuropathy” risk tick and whether it was based on formal neurological testing are not shown on the referral form.

#### F. Decision point

Recurrent pain and foot issues prompted renewed Podiatry input within an already Parkinson-framed pathway.

#### G. Expert question

What clinical weight should be given to a podiatric risk-status “No neuropathy” tick outside its service context?

#### H. Status

Documentary tension / inherited framing

#### I. Notes

The 2019 referral is materially different from the initial April 2017 pathway because the Parkinson's diagnosis is now embedded in the referral wording.

---

### Finding [F-084]: The March 2023 urgent GP referral transmitted both severe pain and a possible relationship to Parkinson control, but did not reduce the problem to one mechanism

**Date / period:** 2023-03-26

**Reading method(s) used:** Referral-pathway reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Referral-purpose control; prospective control; causal-scope control

#### A. Case fact

The GP made an urgent Neurology referral for severe bilateral burning foot/lower-leg pain, described very high symptom burden, documented escalation of pregabalin from an initial low dose, and referred to an earlier episode in which pain had settled after better control of Parkinson's. The GP asked for urgent help/review.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002623–CLIN-002671.

#### B. Record fact

The referral transmitted a possible Parkinson-control relationship as relevant historical evidence, but its operative question was broad urgent specialist review rather than a tightly bounded request to decide among dystonia, peripheral neuropathy, mechanical pain, OFF-state modulation or another mechanism.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The 2023 referral should not be characterised as either a purely “neuropathy referral” or a referral that had already decided the pain was Parkinson-related. It transmitted severity plus competing causal possibilities.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The referral uses neuropathic-type symptom language and documents pregabalin treatment, while also specifically recalling a prior pain episode that improved with better Parkinson control.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Only page 1 of 2 is transcribed in the compiled record; additional content on a second page could further define the task.

#### F. Decision point

Severe recurrence and treatment escalation prompted urgent return to Neurology.

#### G. Expert question

None required to establish that the written referral preserved more than one causal possibility.

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact

#### I. Notes

This finding should be read together with the next-day nurse communication.

---

### Finding [F-085]: The March 2023 nurse communication transmitted the patient's contrary hypothesis to the same receiving neurologist

**Date / period:** 2023-03-28

**Reading method(s) used:** Referral-pathway reading; patient–clinician perspective control

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Referral-purpose control; perspective preservation

#### A. Case fact

The Parkinson's nurse told the consultant that the patient had ongoing debilitating bilateral foot/leg pain despite 600 mg/day pregabalin, wanted specialist review, believed the pain was not Parkinson-related, and reported having stopped Parkinson medication for ten days without noticing a Parkinson-related difference. The nurse explicitly asked what the consultant recommended.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002675–CLIN-002689.

#### B. Record fact

Within two days of the GP referral, the receiving Neurology team had documentary access to both a clinician's recollection of pain improving with better Parkinson control and the patient's contrary current belief plus a self-withdrawal report.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The referral pathway itself preserved conflicting hypotheses before the later Neurology review. A later synthesis should therefore not be read as though only one causal story had been transmitted into the consultation.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The ten-day self-withdrawal was not a controlled medication challenge, and the nurse did not claim it resolved causation.

#### E. What remains unresolved

How the consultant weighted each component before the eventual clinic review is not fully documented in the referral communications alone.

#### F. Decision point

The referral/communication package created a clear need for re-assessment rather than simple continuation of a settled explanation.

#### G. Expert question

How much clinical weight should have been placed on the self-withdrawal report is an external medical question.

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact

#### I. Notes

This is a strong example of why referral-pathway reading must preserve the patient's transmitted perspective separately from the referrer's interpretation.

---

### Finding [F-086]: The receiving Neurology review in 2023 explored several causal levels, but the referral pathway did not itself create external causal closure

**Date / period:** 2023 Neurology review

**Reading method(s) used:** Referral-pathway reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Referral-purpose control; causal-proposition control

#### A. Case fact

At the subsequent Neurology review the clinician discussed severe foot pain, clawing/cramping, possible Parkinsonian dystonia, psychological burden, pregabalin benefit, later-day loss of mobility, and whether lack of levodopa contributed to the inability to walk in the evening. Rare alternative disease was also investigated with alpha-galactosidase/Fabry testing. Later contact records explicitly treated leg pain and shuffling as appearing OFF-related and started Stalevo.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002422–CLIN-002495; CLIN-002574–CLIN-002583.

#### B. Record fact

The receiving service did not simply mirror one referral label; it considered multiple propositions and altered treatment. However, this was an internal Neurology reassessment. The surviving referral record does not show a separate external pain/neuropathy or multidisciplinary service being tasked at that stage with adjudicating the full mixed foot-pain mechanism.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The 2023 review can support specific working propositions—such as an OFF-related component—without being treated as proof that every structural, neuropathic and mechanical component had been finally resolved.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Pregabalin benefit and persistent local foot pathology remained in the record, and the clinician continued to discuss possible non-Parkinson causes.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether a more targeted external referral occurred outside the surviving documentation.

#### F. Decision point

The review moved at least part of the pain/shuffling problem into the dopaminergic management architecture.

#### G. Expert question

Whether further targeted referral was clinically indicated remains an expert question.

#### H. Status

Supported proposition

#### I. Notes

Investigation for Fabry is not itself equivalent to a referral for comprehensive peripheral-neuropathy assessment.

---

### Finding [F-087]: A chronic-pain referral was discussed in 2025, but the surviving record does not establish that one was made

**Date / period:** 2025

**Reading method(s) used:** Referral-pathway reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Silence/absence control; referral-purpose control

#### A. Case fact

The 2025 Neurology note records that the patient wondered about seeing a chronic pain specialist. The neurologist speculated that such a specialist would probably recommend amitriptyline or increased pregabalin.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002800.

#### B. Record fact

The cited record shows consideration of a chronic-pain opinion, not a completed referral or a receiving-service response.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The register must not convert discussion of referral into proof that referral occurred. Conversely, the absence of a referral document in the compiled record is not proof that no referral occurred outside the surviving material.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

No receiving pain-service document is present in the cited compiled record.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether a chronic-pain, neuropathy or other specialist referral was subsequently made elsewhere.

#### F. Decision point

A possible external pain-service pathway was contemplated while Neurology instead proceeded with Sinemet CR and discussed pregabalin adjustment.

#### G. Expert question

Whether external pain-specialist input was indicated at that stage is an expert question.

#### H. Status

Unresolved proposition / documentary absence only

#### I. Notes

The wording here is deliberately “not visible in the surviving record,” not “did not happen.”

---

### Finding [F-088]: The referral history contains several different clinical tasks and cannot be collapsed into the proposition that “the foot problem was referred and resolved”

**Date / period:** 2017–2025

**Reading method(s) used:** Referral-pathway reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Referral-purpose control; causal-scope control; inheritance control

#### A. Case fact

Across the record, referrals served distinct purposes: Podiatry addressed foot/biomechanical manifestations; Neurology/MAU addressed an evolving neurological syndrome; Prof Healy addressed genetics and the dopamine-replacement strategy; later Podiatry re-referral managed recurrent local foot problems within a Parkinson-framed history; and the 2023 urgent Neurology referral reopened severe pain in the setting of conflicting causal clues.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-000018–CLIN-000028; CLIN-000847–CLIN-000884; CLIN-000236–CLIN-000245; CLIN-001712–CLIN-001759; CLIN-003083–CLIN-003120; CLIN-002623–CLIN-002689.

#### B. Record fact

No single surviving referral text located in these sources expressly assigns an external receiving service the entire composite proposition: determine the relative roles of structural lesion, abnormal loading/posture, dystonia/Parkinsonism, peripheral neuropathy, medication-state modulation and pain amplification.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The existence of multiple referrals cannot substitute for proof that one particular causal question was actually transmitted and answered. Each receiving service's response must be interpreted within the task it was given.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Neurology itself did consider several of those causal levels internally, particularly in 2023. The point is not that the issue was never considered; it is that referral existence alone cannot prove causal closure.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether oral handovers, untranscribed pages, or external records contained a more explicit causal referral question.

#### F. Decision point

Repeated recurrence of foot symptoms created several opportunities to reformulate or reassign the question, especially after the 2017 medication-withdrawal evidence and the 2023 pain crisis.

#### G. Expert question

Whether a more explicit multidisciplinary or specialist referral was clinically required at any point is reserved for expert judgment.

#### H. Status

Supported proposition

#### I. Notes

This is the central Pass 6 referral-pathway finding.


## 3F. Pass 7 — Causal-Proposition Reading

**Pass scope:** This pass disaggregates broad labels such as “foot pain,” “the feet,” “Parkinson's symptoms,” “neuropathic pain,” and “clawing” into narrower causal propositions. It asks which proposition each item of evidence actually addresses. It does **not** decide which mechanism is medically correct. In particular, it keeps separate: documented structural pathology; focal painful lesions; dystonic/Parkinsonian posture or motor phenomena; abnormal mechanical loading; peripheral-neuropathic hypotheses; medication-state modulation; amplification of pain experience; and downstream functional consequences.

### Finding [F-089]: The April 2017 presentation already contained at least three separable propositions: structural foot pathology, pain, and abnormal gait

**Date / period:** 2017-04-10

**Reading method(s) used:** Causal-proposition reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Causal-scope control

#### A. Case fact

The GP documented bilateral foot pain and abnormal gait together with marked mallet toes, onychogryphosis and callus/pressure areas. The patient proposed an older sciatica/habitual explanation for the gait.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-000018–CLIN-000028.

#### B. Record fact

The entry does not state that the structural findings caused the gait, that the gait caused the structural findings, or that either proposition explained all of the pain.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

From the first surviving 2017 entry, “the feet” cannot safely be treated as one causal proposition. The record separately supports the existence of structural pathology, pain and gait abnormality while leaving their relationship open.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The patient's own sciatica/habitual explanation was present, but it was not independently established.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The direction and degree of causation among structural deformity, loading, gait abnormality and pain.

#### F. Decision point

Initial choice to pursue Podiatry while awaiting prior records and later escalating neurological assessment.

#### G. Expert question

What causal weight should the documented structural findings have carried in explaining the pain and gait at this stage?

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact / Unresolved causal relationship

#### I. Notes

This finding prevents later shorthand such as “foot problem” from retroactively collapsing distinct propositions.

---

### Finding [F-090]: July 2017 Neurology explicitly separated motor/tension phenomena from structural foot pain and retained more than one causal hypothesis

**Date / period:** July 2017

**Reading method(s) used:** Causal-proposition reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Causal-scope control / Perspective preservation

#### A. Case fact

Neurology documented marked rigidity/bradykinesia and a dystonic-looking gait while also documenting pes planus and hammertoes. The specialist note stated that corns, calluses and burning might be completely separate or might represent a secondary peripheral neuropathy, and recorded that the patient himself distinguished the predominant leg tension from the corns/calluses and foot pains.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-001085–CLIN-001149.

#### B. Record fact

The specialist record itself contains several propositions at once: Parkinsonian motor syndrome; dystonic/postural abnormality; structural foot lesions; focal/burning pain; and a possible neuropathic process.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The Parkinsonian diagnosis cannot, by itself, be used to prove the cause of the foot pain; equally, structural lesions cannot be used to prove that neurological state played no role in the symptom complex.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The record had strong objective evidence of Parkinsonism and strong objective evidence of local foot pathology.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Which components shared a common mechanism and which were parallel coexisting problems.

#### F. Decision point

The July assessment produced operational diagnostic closure for Parkinsonism without causal closure for the foot syndrome.

#### G. Expert question

How should the motor, dystonic, structural and possible neuropathic propositions have been clinically separated and tested?

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact / Documentary tension

#### I. Notes

This is the foundational causal-disaggregation entry for the register.

---

### Finding [F-091]: Repeated Podiatry findings establish persistent local pathology capable of producing symptoms, but not the full cause of the longitudinal pain syndrome

**Date / period:** 2017–2019

**Reading method(s) used:** Causal-proposition reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Structural-pathology control / Causal-scope control

#### A. Case fact

Podiatry repeatedly documented callus/HD at the left fifth MTPJ, clawed toes, tight gastrocnemius/soleus, pronating foot type, pressure/offloading needs and other local pathology, and repeatedly treated these findings with debridement, toe props, padding and insoles.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002864–CLIN-002988.

#### B. Record fact

These are repeated direct observations of local structural/mechanical abnormalities, not merely inherited diagnostic labels.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The record establishes a persistent potential local pain generator and mechanical contributor. It does not establish that this pathology explains every episode of burning, morning pain, cramping, toe curling, medication-linked change or later-day loss of function.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The same anatomical symptom complex changed with neurological medication state, and later records describe OFF-related features.

#### E. What remains unresolved

How much pain arose directly from local lesions versus being modified by posture, loading, dystonia or central dopaminergic state.

#### F. Decision point

Repeated local treatment created opportunities to compare symptom response to structural treatment against response to neurological treatment.

#### G. Expert question

What portion of the symptom burden would ordinarily be expected from the documented podiatric abnormalities alone?

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact / Causal scope limited

#### I. Notes

Structural pathology and neurological modulation can both be true.

---

### Finding [F-092]: The 2017 procyclidine withdrawal establishes medication-state modulation of the foot symptom complex without establishing its underlying pain generator

**Date / period:** September–October 2017; retrospectively documented by treating Neurology in April 2018

**Reading method(s) used:** Causal-proposition reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Medication-state trace / Treatment-response limitation

#### A. Case fact

During procyclidine withdrawal, contemporaneous material records worsening tension, gait and restless feet; the later treating-neurologist note records that the outside-left-foot/corn phenomenon was the first thing to change rapidly off procyclidine.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-001782–CLIN-001808; CLIN-001914–CLIN-001925.

#### B. Record fact

The evidence addresses a **modulation proposition**: symptoms at the foot site varied with neurological medication state.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

This supports neurological modifiability but does not, by itself, prove that procyclidine treated the structural lesion, that the lesion was caused by dystonia, or that the pain mechanism was exclusively neurological.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The structural lesion remained objectively present and required Podiatry treatment.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether the medication changed posture/loading, dystonic tone, pain processing, movement efficiency or some combination of these.

#### F. Decision point

Withdrawal/rechallenge provided a natural experiment that could have prompted explicit causal re-synthesis.

#### G. Expert question

What mechanisms could plausibly account for rapid change in a focal foot symptom during procyclidine withdrawal?

#### H. Status

Supported proposition: medication-state modulation

#### I. Notes

“Responsive to neurological medication” and “neurologically generated” are not equivalent propositions.

---

### Finding [F-093]: The 2019 Podiatry re-referral links “clawing feet” and pain to Parkinson-related tone/gait, but that framing does not independently prove mechanism

**Date / period:** March 2019

**Reading method(s) used:** Causal-proposition reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Causal-scope control / Inherited-framing control

#### A. Case fact

The GP re-referral states that the patient had previously been seen for foot issues “relating to increased tone and abnormal gait relating to Parkinson's disease,” and comments that very-young-onset Parkinson's was associated with “clawing feet” and pain. The same form records local callus, arch pain and gait abnormality and carries a low-risk “No neuropathy” tick.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-003083–CLIN-003120.

#### B. Record fact

The referral combines a Parkinson-linked causal framing with structural/biomechanical categories. The “No neuropathy” tick appears in a Podiatry risk-status field, not in a documented specialist neurological work-up.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The referral supports that tone/gait and Parkinson's were being treated as clinically relevant to the feet by 2019, but it does not independently establish that Parkinsonism generated the painful lesion or that peripheral neuropathy had been comprehensively excluded.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Podiatry continued to document local callus/pressure pathology; the 2017 specialist record had left neuropathic/separate possibilities open.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The evidential basis and intended causal reach of the 2019 “No neuropathy” tick.

#### F. Decision point

Re-referral could have been used to specify whether the task was manifestation treatment or causal reassessment.

#### G. Expert question

What diagnostic meaning, if any, can legitimately be assigned to a Podiatry “No neuropathy” risk-status tick in this context?

#### H. Status

Documentary tension / Inherited framing

#### I. Notes

This pass does not treat the Podiatry risk classification as a neurological diagnosis.

---

### Finding [F-094]: The 2022 record supports heterogeneity within the foot syndrome rather than a single continuous symptom type

**Date / period:** 2022

**Reading method(s) used:** Causal-proposition reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Symptom-disaggregation control

#### A. Case fact

The patient reported that the feet took 20–25 minutes to “warm up” in the morning and described a sore “coronal” pain around the edges of the feet, wondering whether it was neuralgic. In the same review, cramping was described as almost absent.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002378–CLIN-002395.

#### B. Record fact

Pain, morning warm-up behaviour and cramping were documented as distinguishable phenomena rather than as one undifferentiated Parkinson symptom.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Later references to “the foot pain” should not automatically import the same mechanism into cramping, toe posture, local callus pain and morning mobility difficulty.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

No definitive neuropathic test result is recorded here; “neuralgic” is the patient's question, not an established diagnosis.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether the morning warm-up pattern reflected mechanical adaptation, medication state, dystonia, pain sensitization or another process.

#### F. Decision point

This was an early documented morning-pattern clue before the later CR trial.

#### G. Expert question

What clinical differential is suggested by morning warm-up pain with minimal contemporaneous cramping?

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact / Unresolved mechanism

#### I. Notes

The causal value of the morning pattern is reserved for expert interpretation.

---

### Finding [F-095]: The severe 2023 “burning” presentation supports a neuropathic-type pain phenotype but does not establish peripheral neuropathy as its cause

**Date / period:** March 2023

**Reading method(s) used:** Causal-proposition reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Phenotype-versus-mechanism control

#### A. Case fact

The urgent referral described severe bilateral burning pain in the feet/lower legs and documented some benefit from pregabalin, escalating to high doses. The Parkinson's nurse also described the pain as ongoing “? neuropathic” pain.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002623–CLIN-002689.

#### B. Record fact

“Burning,” “neuropathic-type,” and pregabalin responsiveness describe phenotype and treatment response; the record does not at that point contain a definitive peripheral-neuropathy diagnosis explaining the syndrome.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Neuropathic language should be treated as a working proposition rather than as proof that the neurological/Parkinsonian, dystonic or structural propositions had been excluded.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The GP reminded Neurology of a prior pain episode that settled with better Parkinson control; later 2023 notes describe OFF-related leg pain/shuffling.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether a peripheral neuropathy existed and, if so, what share of the pain it explained.

#### F. Decision point

The severe pain crisis prompted urgent neurological review while multiple causal possibilities remained in play.

#### G. Expert question

What investigations would be required to distinguish neuropathic phenotype from confirmed peripheral neuropathy in this record?

#### H. Status

Supported pain phenotype / Unresolved cause

#### I. Notes

Response to pregabalin is not by itself diagnostic of peripheral neuropathy.

---

### Finding [F-096]: The March 2023 self-withdrawal report and later OFF-related response are competing causal evidence, not mutually exclusive proof

**Date / period:** March 2023 through later 2023

**Reading method(s) used:** Causal-proposition reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Counterevidence preservation / Non-monotonic response control

#### A. Case fact

In March 2023 the patient reported stopping Parkinson medication for ten days without perceiving a Parkinson-related difference. Later in 2023, a nurse recorded that pain and shuffling were worse when Sinemet had not been taken and explicitly interpreted the picture as OFF-related, prompting Stalevo initiation.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002675–CLIN-002689; CLIN-002573–CLIN-002583.

#### B. Record fact

The record contains both a relatively neutral self-withdrawal observation and later medication-timing evidence supporting an OFF-related component.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Neither episode should erase the other. The documentary pattern argues against reducing the whole syndrome to a simple proposition of either “dopaminergic” or “not dopaminergic.”

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The withdrawal was uncontrolled; medication regimen, symptom state and other variables differed across episodes.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Why the apparent treatment relationship differed across time and context.

#### F. Decision point

Later 2023 treatment change recognized an OFF-related working proposition despite the earlier neutral self-withdrawal report.

#### G. Expert question

What weight should be given to these differing medication-state observations?

#### H. Status

Documentary tension / Non-monotonic treatment response

#### I. Notes

This is causal counterevidence, not a contradiction that must be “solved” by choosing one account.

---

### Finding [F-097]: The 2023 Neurology review itself separates pain generation, dystonia, psychological burden and functional limitation

**Date / period:** 2023 Neurology review

**Reading method(s) used:** Causal-proposition reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Causal-scope control / Functional-consequence control

#### A. Case fact

The review records bilateral foot symptoms, repeated left-lower-limb cramping raising the question of Parkinsonian dystonia, focal discomfort around the outside of the left foot, a patient-described “pain of the attention it takes to walk,” and inability to complete a fourth evening walk. The clinician expressly states that the situation was not viewed as simply psychological or functional neurological disorder.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002424–CLIN-002448.

#### B. Record fact

The same consultation distinguishes at least four levels: physical pain at a focal site; cramping/dystonia hypothesis; attentional/psychological burden; and functional walking limitation.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

“Psychological burden” cannot safely be used as a substitute explanation for the physical symptom, and physical pathology cannot by itself explain the full functional burden of sustained walking.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The patient himself attributed part of the limitation to psychological factors, while the clinician retained physical/Parkinsonian explanations.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The relative causal contribution of each level to the inability to sustain evening walking.

#### F. Decision point

The consultation created a basis for treating pain, dystonia/motor state and functional limitation as separable management targets.

#### G. Expert question

How should these distinct causal and functional levels be assessed clinically without collapsing one into another?

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact / Mixed causal model supported

#### I. Notes

The record preserves both patient and clinician formulations; Pass 9 will examine their perspectives more directly.

---

### Finding [F-098]: The 2024 record documents dissociation between pain relief and persistent toe curling

**Date / period:** 2024

**Reading method(s) used:** Causal-proposition reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Symptom-disaggregation / Treatment-response limitation

#### A. Case fact

The patient reported that Lyrica removed the foot pain to a large extent—right foot much better with some left-sided pain remaining—but did not relieve toe curling while standing/playing darts.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002698–CLIN-002710.

#### B. Record fact

Pain and toe curling responded differently to the same treatment state.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The record supports treating pain and toe-curling/postural motor phenomena as at least partly dissociable propositions. Improvement in one does not prove resolution of the other.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Both remained within the same broader foot syndrome and could still interact mechanically or neurologically.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether toe curling was dystonic, mechanical, medication-state related or mixed, and how much it contributed to residual pain.

#### F. Decision point

Persistent toe curling despite pain relief created a reason to consider a motor/postural target independently of neuropathic-pain treatment.

#### G. Expert question

What is the likely mechanism and management significance of toe curling that persists despite substantial analgesic response to pregabalin?

#### H. Status

Established documentary fact / Causal dissociation supported

#### I. Notes

This is one of the clearest within-record examples that “foot pain” and “clawing/toe curling” should not be treated as synonyms.

---

### Finding [F-099]: The 2025 morning pattern and Sinemet CR response establish dopaminergic modulation of pain experience, not a single proven pain generator

**Date / period:** 2025

**Reading method(s) used:** Causal-proposition reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Medication-state trace / Therapeutic-variable disaggregation / Treatment-response limitation

#### A. Case fact

The 2025 Neurology note records severe morning foot pain, relief once Parkinson medication reached the system, and a long overnight gap before morning medication. The plan introduced bedtime Sinemet CR; the subsequent complaint-response material records profound current relief with Sinemet CR.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002776–CLIN-002800; CLIN-003243–CLIN-003270.

#### B. Record fact

These entries directly support a **pain-modulation/coverage proposition**: the experience of foot pain varied with dopaminergic medication timing and formulation.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The response strengthens the proposition that dopaminergic state materially modulated the symptom. It does not, by itself, distinguish among painful dystonia, altered loading from motor state, central/non-motor pain modulation, amplification of another pain generator, or a mixed mechanism.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Persistent structural pathology and prior pregabalin benefit remain relevant; dyskinesia sensitivity constrained further dopaminergic exposure.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The mechanism through which CR produced relief and whether the same benefit could have been achieved earlier with acceptable adverse effects.

#### F. Decision point

The CR trial explicitly tested duration-of-coverage rather than simply increasing daytime dose.

#### G. Expert question

What causal conclusions, if any, can safely be drawn from this formulation-specific response?

#### H. Status

Established treatment response / Mechanism unresolved

#### I. Notes

A response can be clinically important without being mechanistically specific.

---

### Finding [F-100]: The 2025 complaint response introduces a pain-amplification proposition that is distinct from the proposition that Parkinson's directly caused the pain

**Date / period:** 2025-03-25

**Reading method(s) used:** Causal-proposition reading / Complaint-response control

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Causal-scope control / Retrospective reconstruction

#### A. Case fact

The complaint response states that Parkinson's can magnify symptomatology and pain from other causes, that Sinemet may reduce the experience of pain, and that Parkinson's does not directly cause all pain a person with Parkinson's may have.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-003240–CLIN-003258.

#### B. Record fact

The response distinguishes **pain amplification/modulation** from **pain generation**.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The proposition “Parkinson's or levodopa changes the experience of pain” cannot be used interchangeably with “Parkinson's caused the underlying painful lesion/process.” Conversely, a non-Parkinson pain generator would not exclude clinically meaningful Parkinson-related amplification.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The response is retrospective and does not itself prove that this exact causal model was contemporaneously governing every earlier decision.

#### E. What remains unresolved

When this amplification model first became explicit in the treating record and how it should be reconciled with earlier dystonia/OFF formulations.

#### F. Decision point

The complaint response is explanatory rather than a new treatment decision, but it changes the documentary articulation of causal scope.

#### G. Expert question

How should central pain amplification/modulation be distinguished clinically from painful dystonia and peripheral pain generation in this case?

#### H. Status

Retrospective explanatory proposition / Causal distinction valid as documentary fact

#### I. Notes

Pass 12 will audit the complaint response as a whole; this pass only separates the propositions contained within it.

---

### Finding [F-101]: The 2026 PRKN result establishes the cause of the Parkinsonism and dystonia, but not automatically the cause of every foot-pain component

**Date / period:** 2026-02-04

**Reading method(s) used:** Causal-proposition reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Genetic-scope control / Retrospective control

#### A. Case fact

The St Vincent's letter states that two pathogenic Parkin/PRKN variants were confirmed and that the patient's Parkinsonism and dystonia are due to compound heterozygous Parkin mutations.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-003220–CLIN-003229.

#### B. Record fact

The genetic letter expressly attributes **Parkinsonism and dystonia** to PRKN. It does not expressly attribute every corn, callus, focal pressure lesion, neuropathic-type pain complaint or pain-amplification phenomenon to PRKN.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The genetic result materially strengthens the neurological/dystonic branch of the causal model but does not eliminate separately documented structural or possible peripheral pain generators.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Local structural pathology was independently documented for years, and the pain phenotype varied across time and treatments.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Which specific foot manifestations were consequences of PRKN-related dystonia/posture versus independent or interacting processes.

#### F. Decision point

The result changes diagnostic certainty from 2026 onward but cannot be back-projected as earlier causal closure.

#### G. Expert question

Which of the documented foot manifestations are characteristic or plausible consequences of PRKN-related dystonia, and which require separate explanation?

#### H. Status

Established genetic causation for Parkinsonism/dystonia / Foot-pain scope unresolved

#### I. Notes

Genetic causation has a defined object; the object should not silently expand beyond the letter's wording.

---

### Finding [F-102]: The surviving record supports a multi-level causal model of the foot syndrome, but does not establish the weighting among its components

**Date / period:** 2017–2026

**Reading method(s) used:** Causal-proposition reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Scope control / Counterevidence preservation

#### A. Case fact

Across the record there is direct evidence of structural foot pathology; Parkinsonian/dystonic motor abnormalities; medication-state-sensitive symptom change; neuropathic-type descriptors and pregabalin benefit; focal pressure-site pain; psychological/attentional burden; and major functional consequences. Later genetics confirms PRKN-related Parkinsonism and dystonia.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-000018–CLIN-000028; CLIN-001085–CLIN-001149; CLIN-001782–CLIN-001925; CLIN-002378–CLIN-002400; CLIN-002424–CLIN-002495; CLIN-002574–CLIN-002583; CLIN-002698–CLIN-002710; CLIN-002776–CLIN-002800; CLIN-003227–CLIN-003229.

#### B. Record fact

No single evidential strand in the surviving record resolves all of the others. Several mechanisms can coexist without contradiction.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The strongest documentary conclusion at Pass 7 is not “the pain was Parkinson's,” “the pain was neuropathy,” “the pain was structural,” or “the pain was psychological.” It is that the record requires those propositions to be tested separately and then related, rather than collapsed into one label.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Some later notes favor particular working explanations at particular times, including OFF-related pain and pain amplification; these remain important but proposition-specific.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The relative causal contribution and temporal importance of each mechanism across different phases of the illness.

#### F. Decision point

Any later decision purporting to treat or explain “the foot pain” should specify which causal proposition it is addressing.

#### G. Expert question

Can an external multidisciplinary review apportion the documented symptom complex among structural, dystonic, mechanical, neuropathic, central-modulatory and functional components, and identify which were treatment-responsive at each stage?

#### H. Status

Supported proposition / Expert synthesis required

#### I. Notes

This is the central Pass 7 causal-proposition finding.

## 3G. Pass 8 — Evidential-Development Reading

**Pass scope:** This pass classifies later material according to what it actually adds to the evidential state. It uses the guide's four categories: **A. new observation**, **B. new interpretation of new evidence**, **C. inherited framing**, and **D. retrospective reconstruction**. The question is not simply whether later wording is stronger, but whether new evidence accompanied it. Repeated terminology is not treated as independent corroboration unless the later entry contributes a genuinely new observation, test result, treatment-state effect, or interpretation grounded in new evidence.

### Finding [F-103]: The June 2017 escalation contained genuinely new observations rather than mere repetition of the April gait/foot presentation

**Date / period:** April–June 2017

**Reading method(s) used:** Evidential-development reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Inheritance control / Evidential-independence control

#### A. Case fact

The April GP entry documented abnormal gait, bilateral foot pain and structural foot abnormalities. In June, the GP documented impaired initiation of movement, later increased tone and brisk reflexes, worsening dexterity and inability to perform work tasks; an Occupational Health doctor independently echoed concern about the gait.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-000018–CLIN-000028; CLIN-000064–CLIN-000111; CLIN-000190–CLIN-000235; CLIN-000847–CLIN-000884.

#### B. Record fact

The June entries did not simply restate “abnormal gait.” They added new observed and reported phenomena and an independent external clinical concern.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The evidential state materially changed before the July specialist assessment. Later neurological escalation was supported by new evidence, not merely by repetition of the April label.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The patient's earlier sciatica/habitual explanation and anxiety-related interpretation remained in the history.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The cause of the accumulating motor findings at that stage.

#### F. Decision point

The threshold for urgent neurological assessment was crossed after new functional and examination evidence appeared.

#### G. Expert question

None required for the documentary classification.

#### H. Status

A — New observation

#### I. Notes

This distinction matters because repeated use of “gait” across April and June would otherwise understate how much the evidential state changed.

---

### Finding [F-104]: The July 2017 specialist examination and DaTscan added new evidence; the stronger Parkinsonian formulation was not merely inherited framing

**Date / period:** 2017-07-03 to 2017-07-13

**Reading method(s) used:** Evidential-development reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** New-evidence classification / Document-architecture control

#### A. Case fact

Neurology newly documented marked rigidity, bradykinesia, a striking abnormal/dystonic-looking gait, reduced arm swing and other Parkinsonian findings. The 13 July DaTscan then showed marked bilateral truncation of basal-ganglia uptake.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-001085–CLIN-001145; CLIN-001139–CLIN-001140; CLIN-001629–CLIN-001638; CLIN-001857–CLIN-001879.

#### B. Record fact

The syndrome-level interpretation was supported by a new specialist examination and then by a new imaging result. The DaTscan was an addendum and should be treated as evidence entering after the earlier clinical reasoning had already begun.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The shift from open GP concern to a strong Parkinsonian diagnosis represents genuine evidential development. It does not, however, supply new evidence resolving the separately stated foot-causation uncertainty.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The same specialist assessment retained atypical features and multiple disease-form possibilities and described the relationship of corns/calluses/burning to the neurological syndrome as unclear.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Exact Parkinsonian subtype at that date and the causal relation of the foot symptoms to the syndrome.

#### F. Decision point

The new specialist findings and imaging supported initiation of a Parkinson-focused treatment plan.

#### G. Expert question

None required for the evidential classification; clinical implications remain for later expert review.

#### H. Status

A + B — New observation and new interpretation of new evidence

#### I. Notes

A stronger diagnosis here has new evidential support; it is not simply a repeated label.

---

### Finding [F-105]: Healy's September 2017 PARK2 opinion was an additional specialist interpretation, not genetic confirmation

**Date / period:** 2017-09-12

**Reading method(s) used:** Evidential-development reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Evidential-independence control / Temporal reach

#### A. Case fact

After specialist review, Prof Healy recorded that the patient “probably has Park II,” recommended testing that first, and documented dramatic motor improvement with procyclidine and rasagiline.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-001734–CLIN-001760.

#### B. Record fact

This is not merely a later Galway note repeating its own earlier PRKN/PARK2 suspicion. It is a separate specialist assessment adding a new interpretation after review. But it remained a hypothesis pending testing.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The PARK2 proposition became more strongly supported in September 2017 without becoming genetically established. The 2026 result is a separate later evidential event.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Other genetic and atypical Parkinsonian possibilities remained open in the 2017 record.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether testing was completed then and what intermediate genetic evidence, if any, existed before 2026.

#### F. Decision point

Genetic testing was explicitly recommended.

#### G. Expert question

Whether earlier confirmation would have changed management remains an external expert question already reserved elsewhere.

#### H. Status

B — New interpretation of new evidence/review

#### I. Notes

Independent specialist agreement strengthens a hypothesis but is not equivalent to a confirmatory test.

---

### Finding [F-106]: The 2017 procyclidine withdrawal was a genuinely new within-patient observation that changed the evidential state of the foot question

**Date / period:** September–October 2017

**Reading method(s) used:** Evidential-development reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Medication-state trace / Treatment-response limitation

#### A. Case fact

During a planned procyclidine break, contemporaneous patient material recorded increasing tension, restless feet and deterioration in walking while vision improved off treatment. Reintroduction at a lower dose did not control all symptoms and the dose was increased again.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-001770–CLIN-001801; CLIN-001822–CLIN-001839.

#### B. Record fact

This was not a new label applied to old symptoms. It was a new treatment-state observation occurring during an actual withdrawal/rechallenge sequence.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

After this event, the evidential state was no longer identical to the July position. The record now contained direct within-patient evidence that neurological medication state could alter a symptom complex that included the feet.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The observation did not identify the mechanism and did not remove the independently documented structural foot pathology.

#### E. What remains unresolved

What component of the foot syndrome was being altered by procyclidine.

#### F. Decision point

The withdrawal/rechallenge created a natural point for causal re-synthesis, whether or not such re-synthesis was documented.

#### G. Expert question

What clinical weight should that new treatment-state evidence have carried?

#### H. Status

A — New observation

#### I. Notes

This is one of the clearest examples in the record of evidence changing while the underlying diagnostic language remained less changed.

---

### Finding [F-107]: The April 2018 Neurology note added specificity to the 2017 withdrawal history, but it did not create a second contemporaneous withdrawal event

**Date / period:** 2018-04-24, referring back to Sep–Oct 2017

**Reading method(s) used:** Evidential-development reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Retrospective-event control / Source hierarchy

#### A. Case fact

The treating neurologist recorded that when procyclidine had been stopped for two weeks, the “first thing” changing rapidly was return of the outside-left-foot/corn phenomenon, and that the patient was more “shifty” and “unrelaxed” off treatment.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-001914–CLIN-001925.

#### B. Record fact

The 2018 note adds clinically relevant specificity to the earlier withdrawal episode, but the underlying treatment interruption occurred in 2017. It therefore adds a later clinician-recorded account of the event, not a second independent withdrawal experiment.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The evidential weight of the 2017 medication-foot association is strengthened by later treating-clinician documentation, but it should not be double-counted as two separate natural experiments.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The direct 2017 email records restless feet and gait deterioration but is less specific about the focal outside-left-foot sequence.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether the added specificity came from contemporaneous clinical knowledge not otherwise preserved or from retrospective patient report at the 2018 visit.

#### F. Decision point

By April 2018 the treating Neurology record itself contained the more specific foot-withdrawal history.

#### G. Expert question

Whether that degree of evidence should have altered investigation or treatment remains external judgment.

#### H. Status

D/B hybrid — Retrospective recording with added clinical interpretation; not a new contemporaneous experiment

#### I. Notes

The event and the later documentation of the event have different evidential functions.

---

### Finding [F-108]: Repeated Podiatry visits from 2017 to 2019 contributed genuine new local observations rather than mere copied terminology

**Date / period:** 2017-09-14 to 2019-03-25

**Reading method(s) used:** Evidential-development reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Repetition versus corroboration control

#### A. Case fact

Across separate visits, Podiatry repeatedly documented callus/HD at the left fifth MTPJ, later clawed toes, pronating foot type, pressure areas and continuing need for debridement/offloading/orthotic management.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002864–CLIN-002988.

#### B. Record fact

These entries are not simply the same historical phrase copied forward. They record serial examinations and treatment actions on different dates.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Repeated local structural pathology gains genuine longitudinal support from repeated new observations. That corroboration is stronger than repeated diagnostic wording without a new examination.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The local findings do not resolve the neurological contribution to pain or motor state.

#### E. What remains unresolved

How much of the symptom burden those local findings generated at each stage.

#### F. Decision point

Serial structural care offered repeated opportunities to compare local-treatment response with neurological-treatment response.

#### G. Expert question

What causal weight should the serial local findings carry relative to medication-state evidence?

#### H. Status

A — Repeated new observation

#### I. Notes

Pass 8 distinguishes serial re-examination from mere terminological repetition.

---

### Finding [F-109]: The 2019 Parkinson-linked Podiatry re-referral strengthened the framing more than the underlying causal evidence

**Date / period:** March 2019

**Reading method(s) used:** Evidential-development reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Inheritance control / Referral-scope control

#### A. Case fact

The re-referral described prior foot issues as relating to increased tone and abnormal gait related to Parkinson's disease, referred to “clawing feet” and pain, and included a low-risk “No neuropathy” tick.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-003083–CLIN-003120.

#### B. Record fact

The referral contains stronger Parkinson-linked language than the original July 2017 “Not clear” formulation, but the visible form does not document a new neurological examination or diagnostic test establishing that causal link. The “No neuropathy” entry is a Podiatry risk-status classification whose broader evidential basis is not visible.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The later language should not be counted as independent causal corroboration merely because it is more categorical. For the causal proposition, much of the entry functions as inherited or operational framing.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The referral was prompted by genuine recurrent symptoms, so the event itself is new; what is inherited is the causal framing, not the recurrence.

#### E. What remains unresolved

What new assessment, if any, supported the stronger causal wording and the neuropathy risk classification.

#### F. Decision point

The re-referral was an opportunity to test whether the inherited Parkinson framing still adequately described the problem.

#### G. Expert question

None required to classify the documentary development; clinical meaning of the risk tick remains expert territory.

#### H. Status

C — Inherited framing, with a new recurrence/referral event

#### I. Notes

This is a central example of language becoming stronger without clearly visible new causal evidence.

---

### Finding [F-110]: Copied-forward 2017 Neurology narrative appearing in later material is inherited framing unless a new observation is separately identified

**Date / period:** 2019 Neurology material carrying earlier narrative

**Reading method(s) used:** Evidential-development reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Inheritance control / Document-architecture control

#### A. Case fact

Later Neurology material reproduces substantial 2017 narrative about the original diagnosis, atypical features, treatment logic and counselling.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002167–CLIN-002220 and surrounding carried-forward material.

#### B. Record fact

Physical reappearance of earlier text in a later section does not itself mean the underlying proposition was newly observed or independently reconsidered in 2019.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Copied-forward material must be classified as inherited framing unless the later note clearly adds a new examination, symptom behaviour, treatment response, test or interpretation grounded in new evidence.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The 2019 visit also contains genuinely new observations about Neupro response, dyskinesia and behaviour; those must be separated from the copied history.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Which portions of later summaries were actively re-endorsed versus simply carried forward for context.

#### F. Decision point

None beyond the need to separate new 2019 evidence from inherited 2017 narrative.

#### G. Expert question

None.

#### H. Status

C — Inherited framing

#### I. Notes

This prevents repeated text from becoming artificial corroboration.

---

### Finding [F-111]: The 2021 first-levodopa episode added new treatment-response evidence but did not yet add a documented foot-pain response proposition

**Date / period:** September–October 2021

**Reading method(s) used:** Evidential-development reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Therapeutic-variable disaggregation / Rationale control

#### A. Case fact

Sinemet was introduced because the patient felt undertreated and was then used to replace Kemadrin in the context of ocular-pressure/visual concerns. The dose was increased to four times daily, and slight chorea was already noted.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002330–CLIN-002359.

#### B. Record fact

The first levodopa exposure and early chorea are genuinely new therapeutic observations. The cited entry does not document foot pain as the reason for initiation or a newly observed levodopa effect on foot pain.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The evidential state changed substantially for levodopa tolerability and dyskinesia sensitivity, but not yet—on this cited record—for the specific proposition that levodopa relieved chronic foot pain.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Later records clearly do document pain/OFF relationships.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether an unlocated 2021–2022 note recorded foot-pain response earlier than the currently identified 2023 evidence.

#### F. Decision point

Levodopa moved from deferred therapy to active treatment with a newly observed adverse-effect constraint.

#### G. Expert question

None for the evidential classification.

#### H. Status

A — New therapeutic observation; no new documented pain-response observation located

#### I. Notes

This bounds the temporal reach of later “known for quite some time” wording.

---

### Finding [F-112]: The 2022 morning “warm-up” pattern was new symptom behaviour, not simply another repetition of chronic foot pain

**Date / period:** 2022

**Reading method(s) used:** Evidential-development reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Symptom-pattern control / Observation-window control

#### A. Case fact

The patient reported that on most mornings the feet took approximately 20–25 minutes to warm up and were sore around the corns/calluses, while overall function was otherwise good and cramping was near zero.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002375–CLIN-002400.

#### B. Record fact

This entry adds a new temporal pattern and a dissociation between morning foot soreness and otherwise good activity/cramp control.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The evidential state of the foot problem became more specific: there was now a recurring time-linked morning phenomenon rather than only a generic history of pain.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The note does not itself prove that the morning pattern was an OFF phenomenon; the patient also localized soreness around known corns/calluses and wondered if it was neuralgic.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Which mechanism produced the morning warm-up pattern.

#### F. Decision point

The new pattern could later be compared against medication timing and overnight coverage.

#### G. Expert question

What diagnostic significance should be assigned to this time-linked pattern?

#### H. Status

A — New observation

#### I. Notes

Pass 8 preserves the difference between recurrence and new phenomenological detail.

---

### Finding [F-113]: The March 2023 crisis added genuinely new and internally conflicting evidence rather than simply strengthening one pre-existing pain theory

**Date / period:** 2023-03-26 to 2023-03-28

**Reading method(s) used:** Evidential-development reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Counterevidence preservation / Perspective separation

#### A. Case fact

The urgent referral documented severe bilateral burning foot/lower-leg pain, pregabalin escalation and major distress. The GP recalled an earlier pain episode settling with better Parkinson control. The Parkinson's nurse separately transmitted the patient's view that the current pain was not Parkinson-related and his report that ten days off Parkinson medication produced no perceived Parkinson difference.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002623–CLIN-002689.

#### B. Record fact

The 2023 episode introduced new severity, new treatment exposure, a new self-withdrawal report and competing causal perspectives. The GP's recollection of a prior episode is retrospective evidence about that earlier event rather than a new observation of the earlier event.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The evidential state became more conflicted, not simply more certain. A later summary that presents 2023 as straightforward evidence for one mechanism would lose material counterevidence that entered at the same time.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Later 2023 records do add positive OFF-related evidence.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Why the ten-day self-withdrawal report appeared neutral while later timing-linked observations were positive.

#### F. Decision point

The urgent review presented a clear need for renewed synthesis rather than inheritance of a single earlier label.

#### G. Expert question

How should a self-directed ten-day withdrawal report be weighed against later structured medication-state observations?

#### H. Status

A + D — New observations plus retrospective recollection of an earlier episode

#### I. Notes

New evidence can widen uncertainty as well as narrow it.

---

### Finding [F-114]: Later 2023 OFF-related pain/shuffling was a new interpretation based on new symptom-timing evidence

**Date / period:** Later 2023

**Reading method(s) used:** Evidential-development reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Medication-state trace / Interpretation-of-new-evidence control

#### A. Case fact

A nurse contact recorded severe foot pain and shuffling that were worse in the morning and later in the day if Sinemet had not been taken. The clinician concluded from the description that the patient appeared undertreated and that the leg pain and shuffling were OFF-related; after discussion with the consultant, morning Stalevo was commenced.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002573–CLIN-002583.

#### B. Record fact

The OFF-related formulation was not simply copied from an earlier note. It followed newly reported state/timing information and directly changed treatment.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

This is a genuine evidential-development point: later 2023 is the clearest located date at which pain/shuffling were expressly classified as OFF-related on new contemporaneous evidence.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Pregabalin-responsive pain and structural/local pathology remained present, so the new interpretation did not establish the sole pain generator.

#### E. What remains unresolved

How much of the pain was OFF-related and which component responded to the regimen change.

#### F. Decision point

Morning Stalevo was introduced specifically in response to the new interpretation.

#### G. Expert question

What conclusions about mechanism can reasonably follow from this timing-linked response?

#### H. Status

B — New interpretation of new evidence

#### I. Notes

This should carry more evidential weight than a later bare repetition of “OFF-related” without fresh state information.

---

### Finding [F-115]: The 2024 persistence of toe curling despite major pregabalin-related pain improvement added dissociating evidence

**Date / period:** 2024

**Reading method(s) used:** Evidential-development reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Non-monotonic response / Symptom-domain disaggregation

#### A. Case fact

The patient reported that Lyrica had removed much of the foot pain, especially on the right, while left-sided discomfort and toe curling during activities such as pumping bicycle tyres remained.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002695–CLIN-002710.

#### B. Record fact

The later entry did not merely restate improvement. It added a new dissociation: pain and toe-curling motor/postural phenomena were not moving together.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The evidential state became less compatible with treating “foot pain” and “toe curling” as one identical process. This is new evidence against over-collapsing those domains.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Both phenomena remained within the same broader neurological/foot history and could still interact.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The mechanism of the residual toe curling and left-sided pain.

#### F. Decision point

Management began to focus on evening Sinemet timing while preserving pregabalin.

#### G. Expert question

What does the symptom dissociation imply about separate treatment targets?

#### H. Status

A — New dissociating observation

#### I. Notes

Evidence development can occur by separation of previously bundled symptoms.

---

### Finding [F-116]: The 2025 morning pain/medication-onset pattern and controlled-release trial added new coverage-specific evidence

**Date / period:** 2025 clinic before complaint response

**Reading method(s) used:** Evidential-development reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Therapeutic-variable disaggregation / Medication-state trace

#### A. Case fact

After major pregabalin reduction, the patient described severe morning foot pain, a long overnight gap after the last immediate-release Sinemet, and relief once Parkinson medication reached his system. A bedtime Sinemet CR trial was then planned to alter overnight/early-morning coverage.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002767–CLIN-002800.

#### B. Record fact

This was new coverage-specific evidence. It separated formulation/duration of action from the broader question of simply increasing total daytime levodopa.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The evidential state changed from a general pain/levodopa association to a more testable overnight-coverage proposition.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The same note records that pregabalin had been self-reduced and that higher dopaminergic exposure carried dyskinesia risk.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether later improvement reflected dystonia control, reduced OFF state, pain amplification, altered movement/loading or another mechanism.

#### F. Decision point

Bedtime controlled-release levodopa was introduced as a natural experiment in coverage.

#### G. Expert question

What conclusions can be drawn from response to the formulation change, and what cannot?

#### H. Status

A + B — New symptom-state observation and new coverage hypothesis

#### I. Notes

Dose, timing and formulation are separate evidential variables.

---

### Finding [F-117]: The 2025 complaint response primarily reorganised earlier evidence rather than creating new historical evidence about what had been known

**Date / period:** 2025-03-25

**Reading method(s) used:** Evidential-development reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Retrospective reconstruction / Inheritance control

#### A. Case fact

The complaint response states that Sinemet had been understood for some time to help pain, describes pregabalin as having been introduced partly to spare Sinemet increase, and presents a continuous cautious dopaminergic strategy dating back to 2017.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-003239–CLIN-003269.

#### B. Record fact

The response is new evidence of the clinician's 2025 explanatory position. It is not, merely by being written in 2025, new contemporaneous evidence that those same rationales or levels of certainty existed in 2017, 2021 or early 2023. Each backward-reaching claim must be tested against earlier entries.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

For historical propositions, much of the complaint response functions as retrospective reconstruction rather than independent corroboration. Where it accurately quotes earlier material or aligns with intervening records, that support should be credited separately.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The dyskinesia/ICD caution is strongly supported contemporaneously across years; other parts, including the original pregabalin rationale and the temporal reach of pain-related Sinemet knowledge, require narrower treatment.

#### E. What remains unresolved

How much of the 2025 synthesis reflects contemporaneous but unrecorded clinical reasoning versus later reconstruction.

#### F. Decision point

None retrospectively; the response changes the documentary record by adding a later explanation.

#### G. Expert question

None for evidential classification; clinical reasonableness remains external judgment.

#### H. Status

D — Retrospective reconstruction

#### I. Notes

A retrospective explanation is evidence of the later explanation, not automatically of the earlier state of mind.

---
