### Finding [F-118]: The 2026 PRKN result is definitive new evidence, not merely a stronger repetition of the 2017 PARK2 hypothesis

**Date / period:** 2026-02-03 to 2026-02-04

**Reading method(s) used:** Evidential-development reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Temporal reach / New-test-result control

#### A. Case fact

The St Vincent's letter records two pathogenic Parkin variants and states that the Parkinsonism and dystonia are due to compound heterozygous Parkin mutations.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-003220–CLIN-003229.

#### B. Record fact

Unlike repeated suspicion or diagnostic wording, this is a new confirmatory genetic test result. It materially changes the evidential status of the PRKN proposition from hypothesis to established genetic causation from 2026 onward.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The result strongly validates the earlier PARK2 hypothesis retrospectively but does not convert that hypothesis into confirmed knowledge at the earlier dates.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

None within the record to the genetic conclusion as stated; the remaining limitation is scope, because the letter attributes Parkinsonism and dystonia, not every foot lesion or pain mechanism, to PRKN.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The causal relation between PRKN-related dystonia and each specific foot manifestation.

#### F. Decision point

The diagnostic evidential state changed definitively when the genetic result became available.

#### G. Expert question

How the genetic result should inform causal apportionment of specific foot manifestations remains expert judgment.

#### H. Status

A — New definitive test result

#### I. Notes

This is the clearest example of a proposition moving from hypothesis to confirmation through genuinely new evidence.

---

### Finding [F-119]: Across the record, some propositions genuinely accumulated evidence while others mainly accumulated stronger wording

**Date / period:** 2017–2026

**Reading method(s) used:** Evidential-development reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Inheritance control / Source-independence control

#### A. Case fact

The longitudinal record contains serial examinations, treatment-state observations, treatment changes, symptom-pattern changes, specialist opinions, copied-forward summaries, stronger referral language, retrospective explanations and ultimately a confirmatory genetic test.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, sources cited in F-103–F-118.

#### B. Record fact

The evidential trajectory is heterogeneous. Structural foot pathology, Parkinsonian motor findings, medication-state modulation, dyskinesia sensitivity and PRKN causation each gained genuinely new supporting evidence over time. By contrast, some later Parkinson-linked or neuropathic language appears without a clearly new causal test or observation and therefore functions mainly as inherited framing or working terminology.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The number of times a proposition appears in the record is not a valid proxy for its evidential strength. Weight depends on whether each later entry contributes independent evidence, new interpretation grounded in new evidence, inherited framing, or retrospective reconstruction.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Even genuine new evidence may support only a narrow proposition and may coexist with contrary evidence on adjacent causal questions.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The exact evidential basis for some later neuropathic and Parkinson-linked shorthand where no new examination/test is visible.

#### F. Decision point

Later syntheses should identify which new evidence changed the working model rather than relying on repetition alone.

#### G. Expert question

Where clinical judgments depended on inherited framing rather than new evidence, whether that reliance was reasonable is a matter for external expert assessment.

#### H. Status

Supported record-level proposition

#### I. Notes

This is the central Pass 8 evidential-development finding.

## 3H. Pass 9 — Patient–Clinician Perspective Reading

**Scope of this pass:** This pass preserves the distinction between (1) what the patient experienced, (2) what the patient believed caused it, (3) what clinicians observed, (4) what clinicians inferred at the time, and (5) what clinicians later explained. Agreement is not treated as proof of mechanism, and disagreement is not treated as proof that either perspective was unreasonable. The question in this pass is how the perspectives aligned, diverged, and changed as new evidence entered the record.

### Finding [F-120]: The April 2017 presentation contains an early patient causal hypothesis alongside independently observed structural findings

**Date / period:** 10 April 2017

**Reading method(s) used:** Patient–clinician perspective reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Perspective separation / Causal-scope control

#### A. Case fact

The patient reported bilateral foot pain and abnormal gait and said he felt the gait had started because of an older left-sided sciatica problem and might by then be more “habitual.” The GP separately documented marked mallet toes, onychogryphosis and callus/pressure areas and planned Podiatry referral and screening blood tests.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-000018–CLIN-000028.

#### B. Record fact

The patient's explanation of the gait was recorded as his causal view, while the structural foot abnormalities were clinician observations. The entry does not show the GP adopting the sciatica/habit explanation as a settled diagnosis.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The earliest record already contains different evidential categories: lived symptom history, patient causal belief and clinician-observed pathology. They should not be merged into one proposition.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Later neurological signs and specialist assessment substantially changed the explanation of the abnormal gait, while the structural foot findings remained independently documented.

#### E. What remains unresolved

How much, if any, of the early gait impairment was attributable to the foot lesions, prior sciatica, Parkinsonian motor dysfunction or their interaction.

#### F. Decision point

Podiatry referral and later neurological review proceeded without requiring the patient's initial causal explanation to be either accepted or disproved.

#### G. Expert question

None at this stage beyond later causal apportionment.

#### H. Status

Established perspective distinction

#### I. Notes

A patient explanation is evidence of what the patient believed at that time, not objective proof of mechanism.

---

### Finding [F-121]: By June 2017 the patient and clinicians increasingly converged that the movement problem required neurological assessment, although their framing was not identical

**Date / period:** 8–29 June 2017

**Reading method(s) used:** Patient–clinician perspective reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Perspective separation / Prospective-state preservation

#### A. Case fact

The patient described difficulty initiating movement, consciously planning movement, work-related dexterity failure and inability to perform tasks that had previously been manageable. The GP and occupational-health clinician separately became concerned about abnormal gait, increased tone, brisk reflexes and deterioration and escalated to Neurology/MAU assessment.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-000064–CLIN-000111; CLIN-000190–CLIN-000235; CLIN-000847–CLIN-000884.

#### B. Record fact

The patient's functional account and the clinicians' objective concern moved in the same general direction: the problem was no longer treated as merely a foot or habitual-gait issue. The clinicians, however, supplied the neurological interpretation and urgency.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Convergence in concern should be distinguished from convergence in diagnosis. The patient supplied key phenomenology and functional impact; clinicians supplied examination findings and the decision to seek neurological assessment.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The exact neurological diagnosis remained open in the referral (“?parkinsonian or other”).

#### E. What remains unresolved

The degree to which the patient himself then understood the symptoms as neurological rather than mechanical, psychological or habitual.

#### F. Decision point

Urgent neurological assessment was arranged.

#### G. Expert question

None for this perspective classification.

#### H. Status

Supported convergence in concern; diagnosis still open

#### I. Notes

The record should not turn the patient's later understanding of Parkinsonism into his June 2017 belief unless contemporaneously stated.

---

### Finding [F-122]: In July 2017 the patient distinguished foot pain from leg tension, while the neurologist also preserved causal uncertainty

**Date / period:** July 2017 specialist assessment

**Reading method(s) used:** Patient–clinician perspective reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Perspective preservation / Causal-proposition separation

#### A. Case fact

The specialist record states that the patient had “markedly divorced” the feelings of leg tension from the corns/calluses and foot pains in his own mind. The neurologist separately recorded that the leg symptoms including corns, calluses and burning might be completely separate or might reflect a secondary peripheral neuropathy and concluded “Not clear.”

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-001142–CLIN-001149.

#### B. Record fact

Patient and clinician both preserved a distinction between the major Parkinsonian tension syndrome and the foot-pain/corn complex, but for different evidential reasons. The patient's statement records phenomenological separation; the clinician's statement records diagnostic uncertainty.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

This is an area of partial alignment, not causal closure. The record does not show either participant proving that the problems were independent.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The same specialist examination described a striking dystonic-looking gait plus pes planus and hammertoes, and later medication-state evidence showed that symptoms at the feet could change with neurological treatment state.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether the patient's experiential distinction mapped onto separate generators, partially overlapping mechanisms or different dimensions of one mixed syndrome.

#### F. Decision point

Neurological management and Podiatry management proceeded in parallel.

#### G. Expert question

How much causal weight should be given to preserved phenomenological separation when later medication-state coupling appears?

#### H. Status

Established perspective alignment on distinction; mechanism unresolved

#### I. Notes

A patient can accurately distinguish two experiences without thereby establishing that they have unrelated causes.

---

### Finding [F-123]: The September 2017 second opinion records agreement about a dramatic motor treatment response while adding a specialist interpretation the patient did not supply

**Date / period:** 12 September 2017

**Reading method(s) used:** Patient–clinician perspective reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Perspective separation / Treatment-response scope

#### A. Case fact

Prof Healy recorded that the patient described a dramatic improvement in motor symptoms with low-dose procyclidine four times daily and rasagiline. Healy then interpreted the broader syndrome as probably PARK II and discussed low-dose dopamine replacement and dyskinesia risk.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-001742–CLIN-001749.

#### B. Record fact

The treatment benefit is patient-reported and clinician-recorded; the PARK II and longer-term treatment implications are the specialist's interpretation.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Agreement that improvement occurred does not make the patient's subjective response and the clinician's genetic/therapeutic interpretation the same type of evidence.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The letter does not identify the mechanism of the foot symptoms or order immediate levodopa.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether any component of the reported motor improvement included the focal painful foot phenomenon and how Healy understood that issue, if at all.

#### F. Decision point

PARK2 testing was recommended; cautious dopamine-replacement strategy remained under consideration.

#### G. Expert question

None beyond the causal/treatment questions already reserved elsewhere.

#### H. Status

Established alignment on reported motor response; interpretation clinician-specific

#### I. Notes

This finding separates a reported response from the meaning assigned to it.

---

### Finding [F-124]: The October 2017 withdrawal episode shows strong alignment on benefit and adverse effects, but not on mechanism

**Date / period:** October 2017

**Reading method(s) used:** Patient–clinician perspective reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Medication-state trace / Perspective separation

#### A. Case fact

During a planned procyclidine interruption the patient contemporaneously reported major prior physical improvement on treatment, then increasing tension, restless feet and a deteriorating walk as treatment was withdrawn, while vision improved. He later told HR that the Parkinson's nurse had stopped the medication for about twelve days because of visual problems, reintroduced it at a lower dose and then advised re-escalation because the lower dose did not control all symptoms.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-001770–CLIN-001801; CLIN-001822–CLIN-001839.

#### B. Record fact

The patient's lived account and the treatment actions are mutually consistent: benefit and visual toxicity were both clinically consequential. The surviving material does not show a contemporaneous clinician statement assigning a specific mechanism to the foot change.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The record supports shared recognition of a treatment trade-off without showing shared causal interpretation of the feet.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Structural foot pathology remained present and was being treated by Podiatry.

#### E. What remains unresolved

What the clinical team understood the restless-foot/foot-symptom change to mean at the time.

#### F. Decision point

Treatment was withdrawn, reduced and then re-escalated around visual toxicity and symptom control.

#### G. Expert question

Whether the reported foot response should have prompted a specific causal reassessment is reserved for expert judgment.

#### H. Status

Supported patient–clinician alignment on treatment effect; causal meaning unrecorded

#### I. Notes

The patient's contemporaneous report is particularly strong evidence of experience and timing, but not by itself of mechanism.

---

### Finding [F-125]: The 2018 Neurology note partly converges with the patient's account by recording rapid return of the outside-left-foot/corn phenomenon during withdrawal

**Date / period:** 24 April 2018, referring back to Sep–Oct 2017

**Reading method(s) used:** Patient–clinician perspective reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Perspective alignment / Retrospective-source qualification

#### A. Case fact

The treating neurologist recorded that when procyclidine had been stopped for two weeks, the first thing noticed to change rapidly was return of the outside-left-foot/corn phenomenon and that the patient became more “shifty” and “unrelaxed” off treatment.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-001887–CLIN-001925, especially CLIN-001914–CLIN-001919.

#### B. Record fact

A later clinician-authored note incorporates a more specific description of the same withdrawal episode than the surviving October patient email. This creates partial perspective convergence on the event while leaving the provenance of the added specificity unclear.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The 2018 note strengthens confidence that the outside-left-foot phenomenon formed part of the medication-state history, but it does not transform the episode into two independent observations or prove the causal mechanism.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The direct October email described restless feet and gait deterioration but did not specify focal pain as the first returning symptom.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether the 2018 wording came from contemporaneous clinical observation, patient report at the 2018 visit, earlier undocumented communication or another source.

#### F. Decision point

Procyclidine continued because of benefit despite visual limitations.

#### G. Expert question

None for source classification; mechanistic significance remains an expert question.

#### H. Status

Partly corroborated perspective convergence

#### I. Notes

The later clinician note can corroborate the patient history without being independent evidence of a second withdrawal experiment.

---

### Finding [F-126]: In 2019 the patient's subjective stiffness and insufficient benefit coexisted with a relatively normal-looking clinic examination

**Date / period:** 2019 Neurology review

**Reading method(s) used:** Patient–clinician perspective reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Observation-window representativeness / Perspective preservation

#### A. Case fact

On Neupro 10 mg the patient said the treatment did not feel sufficient, that he remained aware of excessive tone and did not feel as limber as he should. The neurologist recorded that the patient looked very normal and if anything minimally hyperkinetic during the consultation, while also noting the patient's statement that he would not look as good later in the day.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002111–CLIN-002161, especially CLIN-002116–CLIN-002141.

#### B. Record fact

The patient's subjective state and the clinician's clinic observation differ without necessarily contradicting each other. The clinician explicitly preserved the possibility that the observed time window was better than the patient's later-day state.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

A normal-looking examination cannot automatically invalidate a reported symptomatic deficit occurring outside that observation window; equally, a subjective report does not erase the observed examination.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Higher Neupro doses had produced observable/admitted chorea and behavioural effects, constraining escalation.

#### E. What remains unresolved

How representative that clinic examination was of the patient's full daily treatment cycle.

#### F. Decision point

Medication timing/lifestyle regularity and risk balancing were emphasized rather than simply increasing dopaminergic exposure.

#### G. Expert question

How should a clinician weigh discordance between in-clinic motor appearance and persistent patient-reported OFF-state limitation in treatment decisions?

#### H. Status

Documented perspective/observation-window tension

#### I. Notes

This is not evidence that either account was false; they sampled different aspects of the patient's state.

---

### Finding [F-127]: In 2022 the patient raised a neuropathic interpretation of morning foot soreness while the clinic assessment remained broadly positive

**Date / period:** 2022 Neurology clinic

**Reading method(s) used:** Patient–clinician perspective reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Perspective separation / Causal-proposition control

#### A. Case fact

The patient reported that most mornings the feet took 20–25 minutes to warm up and were sore around the corns/calluses; he wondered whether the pain was “neuralgic.” The neurologist recorded that overall things were going well, with high physical activity, minimal cramping and only subtle excessive movement, while preserving the foot complaint in the narrative.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002375–CLIN-002399.

#### B. Record fact

The patient's “neuralgic” question is a patient hypothesis, not a clinician diagnosis. The clinician did not erase the symptom but did not visibly resolve its cause in that note.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The record should distinguish a patient-raised causal possibility from subsequent clinician use of neuropathic terminology.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Known corns/calluses, later OFF-state relationships and later pregabalin benefit all support adjacent but different propositions.

#### E. What remains unresolved

What weight, if any, the treating clinician gave the neuralgic hypothesis at that stage.

#### F. Decision point

No major regimen change was recorded specifically for the morning foot soreness.

#### G. Expert question

Whether that symptom pattern warranted additional assessment is a separate expert question, not answered by the patient's label.

#### H. Status

Established patient hypothesis; clinician causal position not explicit

#### I. Notes

The absence of an explicit answer in the note is documentary silence, not proof that the issue was not discussed.

---

### Finding [F-128]: March 2023 contains the clearest contemporaneous divergence between the patient's causal belief and clinicians' concern

**Date / period:** 26–28 March 2023

**Reading method(s) used:** Patient–clinician perspective reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Perspective separation / Counterevidence preservation

#### A. Case fact

The patient reported severe bilateral burning foot/lower-leg pain despite high-dose pregabalin and told the Parkinson's nurse that he believed it was not Parkinson-related. He also reported stopping Parkinson medication for ten days without perceiving a Parkinsonian difference. The GP, however, urgently sought Neurology review, recalled an earlier pain episode settling with better Parkinson control and expressed concern about the current situation.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002623–CLIN-002689.

#### B. Record fact

The referral pathway preserved opposing causal perspectives rather than presenting one settled account. The patient's non-Parkinson interpretation and the GP's Parkinson-control concern were both transmitted into the Neurology pathway.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Neither perspective can be treated as objective proof. The divergence itself is a record fact and creates a need to examine what subsequent evidence changed the working model.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Later 2023 records linked pain/shuffling to missed Sinemet/OFF state, while pregabalin also provided substantial benefit.

#### E. What remains unresolved

What the ten-day self-withdrawal actually tested, including adherence, timing, formulation, baseline state and whether the pain changed independently of perceived Parkinsonian motor symptoms.

#### F. Decision point

Urgent Neurology review was requested.

#### G. Expert question

How should the patient's negative self-withdrawal report have been weighed against prior and subsequent medication-state evidence?

#### H. Status

Established contemporaneous perspective divergence

#### I. Notes

The patient's belief is clinically important evidence of his interpretation and experience, even where later evidence points in another direction.

---

### Finding [F-129]: Later 2023 records show partial convergence around an OFF-related component without proving that the patient adopted a single Parkinson-causation model

**Date / period:** Later 2023

**Reading method(s) used:** Patient–clinician perspective reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Perspective evolution / Medication-state trace

#### A. Case fact

The patient later described severe foot pain with shuffling and reduced mobility that could be worse in the morning and later in the day if Sinemet had not been taken. A Parkinson's nurse interpreted the description as undertreatment with OFF-related leg pain/shuffling; after discussion with the consultant, morning Stalevo was started. Other later-2023 material records that Stalevo facilitated activities and that pain was better when levodopa was on board.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002422–CLIN-002495; CLIN-002573–CLIN-002583.

#### B. Record fact

Patient-reported timing/state information and clinician interpretation now point in the same direction for at least part of the symptom complex. The record does not require the stronger conclusion that the patient now believed Parkinson's directly caused all foot pain.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

This is best classified as convergence on **modulation/state dependence**, not necessarily convergence on **pain generator**.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Pregabalin continued to provide substantial pain benefit and the structural foot history remained documented.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The patient's own settled causal view after these later-2023 observations is not clearly stated.

#### F. Decision point

Dopaminergic treatment was changed on an OFF-related working formulation.

#### G. Expert question

What causal inference is justified from this patient-report/clinician-interpretation convergence?

#### H. Status

Supported partial convergence on medication-state modulation

#### I. Notes

Convergence can occur at one proposition level while disagreement remains at another.

---

### Finding [F-130]: In 2024 patient and clinician accounts jointly preserve a dissociation between pain relief and persistent toe curling

**Date / period:** 2024 Neurology clinic

**Reading method(s) used:** Patient–clinician perspective reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Symptom-domain separation / Perspective alignment

#### A. Case fact

The patient reported that pregabalin/Lyrica had removed much of the foot pain, with the right foot much better and some left-sided pain remaining, while toe curling still occurred during activity and evening function remained smaller. He nevertheless felt the overall medication was “spot on.” Neurology separately focused on balancing evening dopaminergic benefit against dyskinesia risk and altered the timing/food relationship of the evening Sinemet rather than simply increasing dose.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002695–CLIN-002710.

#### B. Record fact

Patient and clinician perspectives were broadly aligned that the current regimen had important benefits but residual limitations. The patient supplied a particularly useful phenomenological dissociation: pain improvement did not eliminate toe curling.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The patient's report here functions as evidence against treating pain and motor/postural phenomena as identical, while the clinician's management response addresses a separate dopamine-coverage problem.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Mild dyskinesia remained an important constraint even when the patient felt medication was well balanced overall.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The mechanism of persistent toe curling and remaining left-foot pain.

#### F. Decision point

Evening Sinemet timing/administration was modified.

#### G. Expert question

None beyond the mechanistic questions already reserved.

#### H. Status

Supported perspective alignment with symptom dissociation

#### I. Notes

A patient's positive global assessment does not mean every symptom was resolved.

---

### Finding [F-131]: In 2025 the patient's overnight-coverage hypothesis and the clinician's mixed explanation were close enough to support a formulation trial

**Date / period:** 2025 Neurology clinic

**Reading method(s) used:** Patient–clinician perspective reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Perspective integration / Therapeutic-variable disaggregation

#### A. Case fact

After a major pregabalin reduction, the patient described severe morning foot pain and noted that the feet improved once Parkinson medication reached his system. He raised the problem of the overnight gap after the last evening Sinemet. The clinician considered both loss of pregabalin benefit and Parkinson-related modulation, while remaining concerned about dyskinesia, and proposed bedtime Sinemet CR to alter overnight/early-morning coverage.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002767–CLIN-002800.

#### B. Record fact

The patient's own temporal hypothesis became an input to treatment design, but the clinician did not adopt a single-cause explanation. The resulting plan tested coverage rather than asserting that Parkinsonism generated the underlying pain.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

This is a strong example of perspective integration through a therapeutic test: shared attention to the timing pattern was sufficient for action despite continuing causal uncertainty.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Pregabalin reduction was a major concurrent variable, and the clinician explicitly warned that Sinemet CR might not work and might worsen dyskinesia or dreaming.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The proportion of improvement attributable to motor/postural change, pain modulation, altered loading or another mechanism.

#### F. Decision point

Bedtime Sinemet CR was introduced.

#### G. Expert question

Whether a similar timing/formulation test should have occurred earlier remains an expert counterfactual question.

#### H. Status

Established perspective integration around a testable timing hypothesis

#### I. Notes

The record can show successful shared hypothesis testing without showing definitive causal closure.

---

### Finding [F-132]: The 2025 complaint correspondence crystallizes a difference in explanatory framing rather than resolving it

**Date / period:** 25 March–15 April 2025

**Reading method(s) used:** Patient–clinician perspective reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Complaint-response control / Perspective preservation

#### A. Case fact

The Quality and Patient Safety correspondence records the patient's concern that bias and diagnostic anchoring, particularly emphasis on alcohol excess, affected pain management and that Sinemet CR had become an effective analgesic for the foot pain. In the consultant response, the neurologist accepted the patient's account of his experience and expressly referred to “different perceptions of the person and the provider,” while explaining a model in which Parkinson's can magnify pain from other causes and Sinemet can reduce the experience of pain without proving Parkinson's directly causes all pain.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-003239–CLIN-003284.

#### B. Record fact

The complaint documents two retrospective explanatory frames. The patient's frame concerns prolonged pain and possible anchoring on alcohol/peripheral neuropathy; the clinician's frame emphasizes a broader Parkinson context, pain amplification and long-term dopaminergic risk management.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The existence of disagreement is itself evidence about the later state of the therapeutic relationship and historical interpretation. Neither complaint nor response is a privileged narrator of the earlier record; each backward-looking proposition must still be tested against contemporaneous sources.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The earlier record supports some elements of each account: alcohol/neuropathy were indeed discussed, but Parkinson-related state effects and later OFF-related treatment were also documented.

#### E. What remains unresolved

When each explanatory frame became dominant for each participant, what was communicated between them at intervening points, and whether there were undocumented discussions that would narrow the apparent divergence.

#### F. Decision point

The complaint process produced explicit retrospective clarification but did not itself create new historical evidence about earlier clinical reasoning.

#### G. Expert question

What weight should be given to the documented divergence when evaluating communication, shared understanding and later management decisions?

#### H. Status

Established retrospective perspective divergence

#### I. Notes

An apology and acceptance of the patient's experience do not by themselves amount to acceptance of the patient's causal or governance interpretation.

---

### Finding [F-133]: The 2026 genetic result objectively closes the PRKN disease proposition but does not adjudicate earlier perspective disputes over foot pain

**Date / period:** 3–4 February 2026

**Reading method(s) used:** Patient–clinician perspective reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Scope control / Hindsight control

#### A. Case fact

Genetic testing confirmed two pathogenic Parkin/PRKN variants and the specialist attributed the Parkinsonism and dystonia to compound heterozygous Parkin mutations.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-003220–CLIN-003229.

#### B. Record fact

This new objective evidence resolves a proposition that had previously been a patient/clinician subject of diagnostic discussion and specialist hypothesis. It does not state the mechanism of each foot lesion or pain episode.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Objective closure of the neurological diagnosis should not be used to declare either earlier patient or clinician pain-causation perspective globally correct. The relevant pain propositions remain more granular.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Structural lesions, pregabalin responsiveness and the 2025 amplification explanation remain part of the pain record.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The precise relationship between PRKN-related dystonia and the historical focal/burning foot-pain components.

#### F. Decision point

The genetic-diagnostic proposition changed from suspected to confirmed.

#### G. Expert question

How the confirmed genotype should influence retrospective causal apportionment of the foot syndrome remains expert judgment.

#### H. Status

Established diagnostic closure with limited causal scope

#### I. Notes

Later objective evidence can settle one disputed proposition without settling all adjacent disagreements.



## 3I. Pass 10 — Uncertainty-and-Closure Reading

**Scope of this pass:** This pass tracks each material uncertainty from first appearance through later narrowing, working assumptions, operational decisions and any eventual causal resolution. It distinguishes **operational closure** (enough certainty to act) from **causal closure** (enough evidence to determine why a manifestation occurred). A working label, referral, treatment decision or successful therapeutic trial is not treated as causal closure unless the record expressly supports that step.

### Finding [F-134]: The neurological syndrome moved from open referral question to operational diagnostic closure in July 2017, while exact disease form remained open

**Date / period:** June–July 2017

**Reading method(s) used:** Uncertainty-and-closure reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Operational-versus-causal closure / Prospective-state preservation

#### A. Case fact

The June MAU referral asked whether the progressive abnormal gait, increased tone, movement-initiation difficulty and work impairment represented a Parkinsonian or other underlying diagnosis. In July, specialist examination documented marked rigidity, bradykinesia and a striking dystonic-appearing gait; the DaTscan was markedly abnormal bilaterally.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-000847–CLIN-000884; CLIN-001085–CLIN-001140.

#### B. Record fact

By July 2017 the record had enough convergent clinical and imaging evidence to proceed on a Parkinsonian diagnosis and treatment plan. However, the specialist narrative still retained uncertainty about the exact form, including idiopathic, recessive/genetic and other Parkinsonian possibilities.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The record reached operational diagnostic closure for the Parkinsonian syndrome before it reached etiological/genetic closure.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The presentation was atypical in age, symmetry and phenomenology, and several different disease-form hypotheses remained visible.

#### E. What remains unresolved

At that date: exact subtype, genetic cause and the relationship of the foot-pain/corn/callus syndrome to the neurological disorder.

#### F. Decision point

Parkinson-focused treatment and follow-up proceeded despite remaining etiological uncertainty.

#### G. Expert question

None is required to establish this documentary distinction; the clinical adequacy of the degree of certainty is separate expert territory.

#### H. Status

Operationally closed for syndrome; etiologically open

#### I. Notes

A diagnosis can be sufficiently secure to govern care without resolving every causal proposition attached to the presentation.

---

### Finding [F-135]: The July 2017 foot-causation question was explicitly left open and was not closed by the Parkinsonian diagnosis

**Date / period:** July 2017

**Reading method(s) used:** Uncertainty-and-closure reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Causal-scope control / Operational-versus-causal closure

#### A. Case fact

The specialist documented pes planus and hammertoes and separately stated that the leg/foot symptoms including corns, calluses and burning might be completely separate or might represent a secondary peripheral neuropathy, concluding “Not clear.”

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-001119–CLIN-001149.

#### B. Record fact

The neurological diagnosis and the foot-causation proposition had different closure states in the same document: the former was sufficiently established to drive management, while the latter remained explicitly unresolved.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Later use of a Parkinson's diagnosis field, Podiatry referral, or continued Parkinson treatment cannot by itself be read as closing the separate question of what generated the foot pain or lesions.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Structural foot pathology was objectively present, while dystonic/Parkinsonian gait and later medication-state effects also entered the record.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The relative roles of structural lesions, abnormal loading/posture, neuropathy, neurological medication state and pain amplification.

#### F. Decision point

Parallel neurological and Podiatry management proceeded without causal closure.

#### G. Expert question

How the unresolved components should have been investigated or weighted is for expert assessment.

#### H. Status

Explicitly open

#### I. Notes

This is the principal uncertainty tracked through Pass 10.

---

### Finding [F-136]: The initial treatment plan is an example of operational closure without full etiological closure

**Date / period:** July 2017

**Reading method(s) used:** Uncertainty-and-closure reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Management-plan separation / Operational closure

#### A. Case fact

The plan introduced rasagiline and procyclidine, deferred levodopa/dopamine agonists because of young age and anticipated adverse effects, pursued additional investigations and genetics questions, and requested allied-health/Podiatry input.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-001153–CLIN-001229.

#### B. Record fact

The record did not require complete diagnostic or causal certainty before acting. It used a working Parkinsonian formulation to choose a cautious symptomatic strategy while leaving subtype and foot mechanism open.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

A later reader should not confuse “a plan existed” with “all important uncertainties had been resolved.”

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

None to the documentary point; several unresolved questions are expressly visible within the plan itself.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Genetic form, future levodopa threshold and foot-causation mechanism.

#### F. Decision point

Treatment began under bounded uncertainty.

#### G. Expert question

Whether the chosen degree of closure was clinically sufficient is external judgment.

#### H. Status

Operationally closed for immediate management; multiple causal questions open

#### I. Notes

Operational closure is not evidence of causal closure.

---

### Finding [F-137]: The 2017 procyclidine withdrawal introduced new evidence that narrowed the foot question but did not resolve it

**Date / period:** September–October 2017

**Reading method(s) used:** Uncertainty-and-closure reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Medication-state trace / Treatment-response scope control

#### A. Case fact

During a planned procyclidine interruption for visual adverse effects, the patient contemporaneously described increasing tension, restless feet and deteriorating gait; medication was subsequently reintroduced and increased because the lower dose did not cover all symptoms.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-001770–CLIN-001801; CLIN-001822–CLIN-001839.

#### B. Record fact

This evidence narrowed the previously open proposition by showing that part of the foot symptom complex was neurologically medication-state sensitive.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The July “Not clear” position should no longer be read as if no relevant evidence subsequently appeared. However, state sensitivity did not determine whether the underlying generator was dystonia, altered loading, structural pain, central amplification or another mechanism.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Podiatry continued to document focal structural pathology at the left 5th MTPJ and other mechanical abnormalities.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Mechanism and causal apportionment.

#### F. Decision point

Treatment was reintroduced for symptomatic control; no explicit causal closure of the foot question is visible.

#### G. Expert question

What additional reassessment, if any, should have followed this narrowing evidence?

#### H. Status

Narrowed, not resolved

#### I. Notes

The uncertainty changed state even though it did not disappear.

---

### Finding [F-138]: By April 2018 the treating neurologist had recorded the rapid outside-left-foot change, yet the record still shows operational continuity rather than explicit causal closure

**Date / period:** 24 April 2018

**Reading method(s) used:** Uncertainty-and-closure reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Closure trace / Medication-state trace

#### A. Case fact

The neurologist recorded that during the 2017 procyclidine stop the first rapidly changing feature was the return at the outside-left-foot/corn site, with greater “shifty” and “unrelaxed” symptoms off procyclidine. The same review continued the existing regimen and continued levodopa deferral.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-001914–CLIN-001956.

#### B. Record fact

The medication-foot relationship had entered the treating clinical record, but the cited note does not expressly state that the earlier foot-causation uncertainty was resolved or replaced by a specific mechanism.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Continuation of management is evidence of operational continuity, not proof that causal uncertainty had been adjudicated.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Toe supports remained helpful and structural Podiatry findings persisted.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether the medication-sensitive focal symptom represented painful dystonia, altered mechanical loading, modulation of structural pain or another process.

#### F. Decision point

Existing symptomatic strategy continued.

#### G. Expert question

Whether explicit re-synthesis was clinically indicated is an expert question.

#### H. Status

Operationally bypassed / causally open

#### I. Notes

Silence about closure is recorded as “not visible,” not as proof no reasoning occurred.

---

### Finding [F-139]: The PARK2/PRKN proposition remained deliberately open for years, with testing deferred partly because it was not expected to change current management

**Date / period:** 2017–2018, resolved 2026

**Reading method(s) used:** Uncertainty-and-closure reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Prospective-state preservation / Later-confirmation control

#### A. Case fact

The 2017 record raised recessive/genetic Parkinsonism and Prof Healy thought PARK2 probable and recommended testing. In 2018 the treating neurologist explicitly discussed delaying testing because it would not particularly change current management and because broader sequencing might become more informative later. In 2026 two pathogenic Parkin variants were confirmed.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-001520–CLIN-001560; CLIN-001742–CLIN-001760; CLIN-001955–CLIN-001956; CLIN-003220–CLIN-003229.

#### B. Record fact

Genetic uncertainty was not merely forgotten; at least in 2018 it was expressly retained and its immediate management value questioned. It was causally closed only when confirmatory testing became available in 2026.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

This is a clear example of a proposition remaining open while being treated as non-determinative for immediate symptomatic management.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

None to the later PRKN conclusion; the earlier period still contained alternative genetic and non-genetic possibilities.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether earlier genetic confirmation would have changed management or interpretation of specific foot manifestations.

#### F. Decision point

Testing could be arranged if desired, but was not treated as essential to then-current management; definitive closure came later.

#### G. Expert question

Would earlier genetic confirmation have materially altered care at the relevant dates?

#### H. Status

Open → operationally non-determinative → causally closed in 2026

#### I. Notes

Later correctness does not eliminate the earlier uncertainty.

---

### Finding [F-140]: The 2019 Parkinson-linked Podiatry wording functioned as a working frame but did not demonstrate causal closure of the composite foot syndrome

**Date / period:** March 2019

**Reading method(s) used:** Uncertainty-and-closure reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Referral-scope control / Inherited-framing control

#### A. Case fact

The re-referral stated that previous foot issues related to increased tone and abnormal gait relating to Parkinson's disease and referred to “clawing feet” and pain. The form also contained low-risk ticks for “No deformity” and “No neuropathy.”

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-003083–CLIN-003107.

#### B. Record fact

The wording is more operationally categorical than the 2017 “Not clear” specialist formulation, but the referral itself does not document a new causal investigation resolving the earlier alternatives. Its “No neuropathy” tick also sits in a Podiatry risk-status field rather than a documented specialist neuropathy work-up.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

A stronger working label may represent operational closure for referral purposes without establishing causal closure of the whole syndrome.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Prior and contemporaneous Podiatry records documented deformity/callus, and later records continued to use neuropathic language.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The evidential basis and scope of the 2019 “No neuropathy” entry and the precise causal meaning of the Parkinson-linked referral wording.

#### F. Decision point

Podiatry care was re-opened for recurrent manifestations.

#### G. Expert question

What clinical assessment would be required before this form could support neurological exclusion of neuropathy?

#### H. Status

Operational framing; causal closure not demonstrated

#### I. Notes

Pass 10 treats working assumptions as distinct from explicit resolution.

---

### Finding [F-141]: The peripheral-neuropathy proposition never reaches clear causal closure in the surviving record

**Date / period:** 2017–2025

**Reading method(s) used:** Uncertainty-and-closure reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Proposition-specific closure / Absence rule

#### A. Case fact

Neuropathy first appeared as a possible explanation in 2017. Later records used “neuropathic pain,” treated symptoms with gabapentinoids, discussed alcohol as a possible contributor, and in 2025 still used conditional wording: “If there is a degree of peripheral neuropathy here.”

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-001142–CLIN-001145; CLIN-002574–CLIN-002580; CLIN-002637–CLIN-002683; CLIN-002783–CLIN-002792; CLIN-003240–CLIN-003258.

#### B. Record fact

The record shows repeated use of a neuropathic working frame but, in the material reviewed, no definitive neurophysiological, specialist or other documented causal adjudication establishing peripheral neuropathy as the generator of the foot pain.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

“Neuropathic pain” should not be treated as equivalent to “peripheral neuropathy proven.”

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Structural lesions, medication-state effects, OFF-related interpretation, dystonic/toe-curling phenomena and pregabalin response all coexist.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether a peripheral neuropathy existed, its cause if present, and how much of the pain it explained.

#### F. Decision point

Neuropathic-pain treatment proceeded under working uncertainty.

#### G. Expert question

What evidence would ordinarily be required to establish or exclude peripheral neuropathy in this context?

#### H. Status

Unresolved working proposition

#### I. Notes

The surviving record may be incomplete; non-visible testing is not assumed absent.

---

### Finding [F-142]: The 2021 introduction of levodopa closed one treatment-policy question but did not close the pain-causation question

**Date / period:** September–October 2021

**Reading method(s) used:** Uncertainty-and-closure reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Treatment-policy closure / Causal-scope control

#### A. Case fact

Sinemet was introduced when the patient felt undertreated and as Kemadrin was removed because of ocular concerns. The neurologist then sought to keep levodopa exposure minimal because of early chorea/dyskinesia risk.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002335–CLIN-002353.

#### B. Record fact

The earlier policy question “can levodopa continue to be deferred?” was operationally closed in favour of use. The cited initiation rationale did not simultaneously establish that chronic foot pain was a levodopa-responsive Parkinsonian symptom.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Treatment-policy closure and symptom-causation closure must be tracked separately.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Later records do show pain varying with levodopa state, but that later evidence was not yet part of the 2021 initiation rationale as documented.

#### E. What remains unresolved

When foot-pain modulation by levodopa first became clinically recognized and what it meant mechanistically.

#### F. Decision point

Levodopa became part of the ongoing regimen.

#### G. Expert question

None for the documentary distinction; dosing reasonableness is separate.

#### H. Status

Treatment-policy question closed; pain proposition open

#### I. Notes

One question can close while an adjacent one remains unresolved.

---

### Finding [F-143]: The 2022 morning foot-pain pattern narrowed the temporal problem without yet producing an explicit causal closure

**Date / period:** 2022

**Reading method(s) used:** Uncertainty-and-closure reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Temporal-pattern control / Observation-window control

#### A. Case fact

The patient reported that on most mornings the feet took roughly 20–25 minutes to “warm up,” with soreness around corns and calluses and a question of whether the pain was neuralgic. He was otherwise doing well and remained highly active.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002375–CLIN-002399.

#### B. Record fact

The symptom acquired a reproducible time-of-day pattern, but the record did not then expressly classify that pattern as OFF-related, neuropathic, mechanical or dystonic.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The uncertainty was narrowed in time but not closed in mechanism.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The pain was anatomically described around known corns/calluses, while later medication-state evidence would become stronger.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether the morning “warm-up” reflected loading, medication onset, stiffness/dystonia, neuropathic pain, or interaction among these.

#### F. Decision point

No dedicated overnight-coverage intervention is visible in this entry.

#### G. Expert question

What clinical significance should have been attached to the predictable morning pattern at that date?

#### H. Status

Temporally narrowed; causally open

#### I. Notes

Predictability can increase clinical relevance without itself identifying mechanism.

---

### Finding [F-144]: The March 2023 pain crisis reopened rather than closed the causal question because opposing medication-state signals were documented together

**Date / period:** March 2023

**Reading method(s) used:** Uncertainty-and-closure reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Competing-evidence preservation / Prospective-state preservation

#### A. Case fact

The GP urgently referred severe bilateral burning foot/lower-leg pain, noting a prior pain episode that had settled with better Parkinson control. The nurse separately recorded the patient's view that the current pain was not Parkinson-related and his report of no perceived Parkinsonian difference after ten days off Parkinson medication, despite high-dose pregabalin and debilitating pain.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002623–CLIN-002688.

#### B. Record fact

At this point the surviving record contained simultaneous evidence pointing toward and away from a simple dopaminergic explanation. The appropriate documentary classification is renewed uncertainty requiring reassessment, not closure by either participant's hypothesis.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The severity of the presentation and competing evidence make inherited labels particularly unsafe as substitutes for re-synthesis.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Both sides of the medication-state question are within the same referral episode.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Pain generator, relevance of the ten-day self-withdrawal, and interaction with pregabalin response.

#### F. Decision point

Urgent Neurology review was sought.

#### G. Expert question

How should the negative self-withdrawal report have been weighted and tested against the prior/later medication-state evidence?

#### H. Status

Reopened / broadened uncertainty

#### I. Notes

A crisis can increase urgency while decreasing certainty.

---

### Finding [F-145]: Later 2023 achieved operational closure for an OFF-related component sufficient to change treatment, but not causal closure of all pain

**Date / period:** Later 2023

**Reading method(s) used:** Uncertainty-and-closure reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Operational-versus-causal closure / Medication-state trace

#### A. Case fact

The patient reported severe foot pain with reduced mobility and shuffling that could be worse in the morning and later in the day if Sinemet had not been taken. The nurse interpreted the description as undertreatment with leg pain and shuffling appearing OFF-related, and morning Stalevo was started.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002573–CLIN-002583.

#### B. Record fact

The record now contains an explicit working interpretation linking at least part of the pain/shuffling complex to OFF state and a treatment decision based on that interpretation.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

This is operational closure for a treatment hypothesis: enough confidence to test additional dopaminergic coverage. It is not equivalent to proving the anatomical or pathophysiological generator of all foot pain.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Pregabalin remained beneficial and structural pathology remained historically documented.

#### E. What remains unresolved

How much of the pain was OFF-related, which symptom components responded, and what underlying mechanism produced the pain.

#### F. Decision point

Morning Stalevo was introduced.

#### G. Expert question

What degree of causal inference is justified from the subsequent response?

#### H. Status

Operationally closed for OFF-treatment hypothesis; causally open

#### I. Notes

This is a core example of the operational/causal distinction.

---

### Finding [F-146]: The 2024 dissociation between pain relief and persistent toe curling narrowed the composite syndrome by showing that not all manifestations shared the same treatment response

**Date / period:** 2024

**Reading method(s) used:** Uncertainty-and-closure reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Symptom-disaggregation / Non-monotonic response

#### A. Case fact

Pregabalin substantially reduced the foot pain, particularly on the right, but toe curling persisted during activities such as pumping bicycle tyres. Evening limitation also remained.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002695–CLIN-002710.

#### B. Record fact

The response separated pain intensity from toe-curling/motor-postural phenomena. The record therefore became less compatible with treating the entire foot syndrome as one undifferentiated problem.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

One component could be therapeutically controlled while another remained open, meaning causal closure had to be proposition-specific.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Both symptoms still occurred within the same longitudinal foot syndrome and could interact functionally.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The mechanism of persistent toe curling and the residual left-sided pain.

#### F. Decision point

Evening Sinemet timing/absorption was adjusted rather than simply treating all residual symptoms as failure of pregabalin.

#### G. Expert question

Does this dissociation support a distinct dystonic/postural component and, if so, to what degree?

#### H. Status

Composite uncertainty narrowed into separate components

#### I. Notes

Closure must be symptom-specific.

---

### Finding [F-147]: The 2025 Sinemet CR trial operationally tested the overnight-coverage hypothesis but explicitly preserved uncertainty before treatment

**Date / period:** 2025 Neurology clinic

**Reading method(s) used:** Uncertainty-and-closure reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Therapeutic-variable disaggregation / Prospective-state preservation

#### A. Case fact

The patient described severe morning foot pain, relief once Parkinson medication reached his system and a long overnight medication gap. The neurologist discussed the possibility that increased levodopa could provide some pain relief but risk dyskinesia, and proposed bedtime controlled-release Sinemet while explicitly stating it might not work.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002767–CLIN-002800.

#### B. Record fact

The decision did not presuppose that overnight dopaminergic under-coverage was proven. It converted a bounded uncertainty into a therapeutic test using formulation/coverage rather than simply daytime dose escalation.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The trial represents hypothesis testing under uncertainty, not prior causal closure.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Pregabalin had been substantially reduced and structural/neuropathic possibilities remained active.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Before the result: whether CR would help; after the result: why it helped.

#### F. Decision point

Bedtime Sinemet CR was commenced cautiously.

#### G. Expert question

What can a strong response to altered overnight coverage establish about mechanism and earlier counterfactual treatment options?

#### H. Status

Operational therapeutic test; mechanism open

#### I. Notes

Formulation/coverage is a different question from absolute dose.

---

### Finding [F-148]: The later reported profound Sinemet CR relief closes efficacy for that observed treatment state more than it closes mechanism

**Date / period:** March 2025 complaint response

**Reading method(s) used:** Uncertainty-and-closure reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Treatment-response scope control / Retrospective-claim control

#### A. Case fact

The complaint response states that the recent Sinemet CR escalation provided profound current relief and also states that Parkinson's can magnify pain from other causes rather than directly causing all pain.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-003239–CLIN-003269.

#### B. Record fact

The response supplies strong evidence that the CR intervention was beneficial in the reported period. Its own explanatory language still leaves the underlying generator open, using formulations such as “whatever the underlying cause” and distinguishing pain amplification from direct causation.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The observed efficacy proposition can be substantially closed while the mechanism proposition remains open.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The response is retrospective regarding earlier strategy and does not eliminate structural, neuropathic or dystonic contributors.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The mechanism of relief and whether the same strategy would have produced comparable benefit at earlier dates.

#### F. Decision point

Continued cautious levodopa management was framed around maintaining benefit while limiting dyskinesia risk.

#### G. Expert question

How much mechanistic or counterfactual inference can reasonably be drawn from the CR response?

#### H. Status

Efficacy substantially supported; mechanism unresolved

#### I. Notes

Successful treatment does not automatically close causation.

---

### Finding [F-149]: The 2025 complaint response acknowledges continuing causal non-closure even while offering a broader explanatory model

**Date / period:** 25 March 2025

**Reading method(s) used:** Uncertainty-and-closure reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Complaint-response control / Retrospective reconstruction

#### A. Case fact

The clinician wrote “Whatever the underlying cause for the peripheral neuropathic pain symptomatologies” and explained that Parkinson's may magnify the experience of pain from another cause, while also accepting levodopa-associated pain relief.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-003239–CLIN-003258.

#### B. Record fact

Even the later explanatory document does not claim that the underlying pain generator had been definitively established. It proposes a model of modulation/amplification while retaining uncertainty over the source of the pain.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The complaint response cannot be read as retrospective causal closure of the foot syndrome; at most it offers a later explanatory synthesis and a clearer account of one mechanism of pain modulation.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The patient complaint framed the history differently, and earlier contemporaneous records show several unresolved propositions.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The primary generator(s) of pain and the historical point at which the amplification model became the treating team's working explanation.

#### F. Decision point

No new diagnostic test in the complaint response itself closes the pain mechanism.

#### G. Expert question

How well does the later amplification model fit the full contemporaneous record?

#### H. Status

Retrospective explanatory model with explicit residual uncertainty

#### I. Notes

A later synthesis can coexist with causal non-closure.

---

### Finding [F-150]: The 2026 genetic result closes the PRKN cause of Parkinsonism/dystonia but leaves the specific foot-pain pathway proposition-specific and partly open

**Date / period:** February 2026

**Reading method(s) used:** Uncertainty-and-closure reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Scope control / Later-confirmation control

#### A. Case fact

St Vincent's documented two pathogenic Parkin variants and stated that the Parkinsonism and dystonia were due to compound heterozygous Parkin mutations.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-003220–CLIN-003229.

#### B. Record fact

The genetic etiology of Parkinsonism/dystonia is explicitly closed by this result. The letter does not state that every corn, callus, focal painful lesion, neuropathic-type symptom or medication-sensitive pain episode was directly caused by PRKN.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Later genetic closure narrows earlier causal possibilities but does not automatically close adjacent foot-pain propositions.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Longstanding structural pathology and mixed treatment-response evidence remain part of the record.

#### E. What remains unresolved

How PRKN-related dystonia/posture interacted with structural lesions, mechanical loading, possible neuropathic processes and pain modulation.

#### F. Decision point

Genetic follow-up was planned.

#### G. Expert question

What causal weight should confirmed PRKN dystonia receive in reconstructing the historical foot syndrome?

#### H. Status

Genetic proposition closed; specific foot-pain mechanisms partly open

#### I. Notes

Scope of closure matters as much as certainty of closure.

---

### Finding [F-151]: Across the record, uncertainties followed different trajectories rather than one global transition from “uncertain” to “known”

**Date / period:** 2017–2026

**Reading method(s) used:** Uncertainty-and-closure reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Proposition-specific closure / Temporal reach

#### A. Case fact

The record contains multiple parallel propositions: existence of Parkinsonism, exact genetic cause, structural foot pathology, peripheral neuropathy, dystonia/posture, medication-state modulation, pain amplification, levodopa risk and overnight coverage.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, sources cited in F-134–F-150.

#### B. Record fact

These propositions did not close together. Parkinsonism reached operational closure in 2017; PRKN causation closed in 2026; structural pathology was repeatedly established; medication-state modulation accumulated strong support; peripheral neuropathy remained conditional; and the composite pain generator remained partly unresolved even in the 2025 complaint response.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Any synthesis that says simply “the diagnosis was known,” “the feet were Parkinson's,” or “the pain was neuropathic” risks collapsing different closure states into one retrospective narrative.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Some working assumptions were clinically sufficient to act on even without causal closure.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The final causal apportionment of the foot syndrome and several counterfactual treatment questions.

#### F. Decision point

Future synthesis should state the closure status of each proposition at each material date.

#### G. Expert question

Which unresolved propositions were clinically material enough to require further investigation or alter treatment at particular dates?

#### H. Status

Established record-level finding

#### I. Notes

This is the central Pass 10 finding.


## 3J. Pass 11 — Treatment-Response Reading

**Pass scope:** This pass treats medication introductions, withdrawals, substitutions, dose changes, timing changes and formulation changes as potential natural experiments. It asks what changed immediately before a response, which symptom domains changed or did not change, how contemporaneous the report was, and what confounders remained. The governing control is that **treatment response can establish association, state dependence, dissociation or therapeutic signal without by itself proving mechanism**.

### Finding [F-152]: Treatment response in this record must be read by symptom domain and therapeutic variable, not as a single “worked / did not work” history

**Date / period:** 2017–2025

**Reading method(s) used:** Treatment-response reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Medication-state trace / Therapeutic-variable disaggregation / Non-monotonic response

#### A. Case fact

Across the record, changes in procyclidine, dopamine agonist exposure, levodopa dose and timing, pregabalin exposure and controlled-release levodopa were associated with different changes in movement, gait, pain, toe curling, behaviour, vision and dyskinesia.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, sources cited in F-153–F-169.

#### B. Record fact

The same treatment could improve one domain while worsening another, and a lower or differently timed dose could have a different effect from a higher dose or different formulation.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The treatment history cannot safely be compressed into propositions such as “dopaminergic treatment worked,” “pregabalin worked,” or “the foot pain responded to Parkinson medication” without specifying the drug, dose, timing, formulation, symptom domain and observation period.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Some response descriptions are retrospective, some are confounded by concurrent medications, illness, alcohol/cannabis exposure, meal timing or self-directed dose changes, and several important changes lack a documented outcome in the same note.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The mechanisms producing each observed response and the degree to which responses at different dates represent the same underlying symptom process.

#### F. Decision point

Every later treatment decision should be assessed against the particular response pattern then visible rather than a global efficacy label.

#### G. Expert question

Which of the observed response patterns are clinically informative about mechanism, and which are primarily evidence of therapeutic modulation?

#### H. Status

Established record-level treatment-response finding

#### I. Notes

This is the governing Pass 11 finding.

---

### Finding [F-153]: Early procyclidine treatment produced a large contemporaneously reported improvement across multiple motor and functional domains

**Date / period:** September–October 2017

**Reading method(s) used:** Treatment-response reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Contemporaneous-response priority / Multi-domain response

#### A. Case fact

Prof Healy recorded a dramatic motor improvement on low-dose procyclidine four times daily with rasagiline. During the same period the patient contemporaneously described major improvement in walking, writing, posture, leg looseness, balance and ordinary dexterity after procyclidine titration.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-001742–CLIN-001749; CLIN-001770–CLIN-001801.

#### B. Record fact

The benefit was described contemporaneously and across several functional domains, but procyclidine was not the only neurological treatment present because rasagiline remained part of the regimen.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The record strongly supports a major treatment-responsive neurological state. It does not isolate a single pharmacological mechanism or prove that every symptom improved for the same reason.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Rasagiline was co-administered, and structural foot pathology remained present.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The relative contribution of procyclidine versus background treatment and the extent to which foot pain itself, rather than gait/tension/restlessness, improved at this stage.

#### F. Decision point

The magnitude of response created a reason to preserve benefit while managing toxicity.

#### G. Expert question

What clinical significance should be assigned to such a broad response to low-dose procyclidine in suspected young-onset PRKN disease?

#### H. Status

Strong contemporaneous therapeutic signal

#### I. Notes

Response magnitude is clearer than response mechanism.

---

### Finding [F-154]: Planned procyclidine withdrawal created the clearest early within-patient natural experiment and deterioration included the feet

**Date / period:** October 2017

**Reading method(s) used:** Treatment-response reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Withdrawal experiment / Medication-state trace

#### A. Case fact

During a planned procyclidine break for visual adverse effects, the patient contemporaneously reported increasing tension, restless feet and a walk “falling apart,” while eyesight returned toward normal. The later 2018 Neurology note recorded that during the same withdrawal the outside-left-foot/corn phenomenon returned rapidly.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-001770–CLIN-001801; CLIN-001914–CLIN-001919.

#### B. Record fact

The withdrawal altered neurological symptoms and vision in opposite directions. Rasagiline remained on board, so the principal changed variable was procyclidine exposure.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The record supports medication-state sensitivity of the foot-containing symptom complex and a reproducible treatment-toxicity tradeoff. It does not prove that the structural corn was neurologically generated.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The contemporaneous email describes restless feet rather than explicitly saying the focal corn pain was the first symptom to return; that added specificity appears in later documentation.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The exact foot symptom that changed first and the mechanism linking procyclidine state to the lateral-foot symptom complex.

#### F. Decision point

The medication was reintroduced at lower dose and then re-escalated because symptom control was incomplete.

#### G. Expert question

How much causal or localization information can reasonably be drawn from this withdrawal/rechallenge pattern?

#### H. Status

Strong within-patient association; mechanism unresolved

#### I. Notes

This is not equivalent to a blinded or isolated experimental trial.

---

### Finding [F-155]: Reintroduction at a lower procyclidine dose preserved some benefit but did not control all symptoms, while visual toxicity remained dose-limiting

**Date / period:** October–November 2017

**Reading method(s) used:** Treatment-response reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Dose-response / Adverse-effect tradeoff

#### A. Case fact

After withdrawal, procyclidine was reintroduced at a lower dose. The patient reported that the lower dose did not “tick off all of the symptoms,” and the nurse advised increasing it again while vision was monitored. GP documentation later described symptom control as good on 2.5 mg TDS, with the broader goal of optimizing function with minimum side effects.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-001822–CLIN-001839; CLIN-000754–CLIN-000768.

#### B. Record fact

The sequence contains a practical dose-response signal and an adverse-effect ceiling rather than a binary effective/ineffective result.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Therapeutic benefit and treatment toxicity coexisted, making “procyclidine worked” an incomplete summary.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Exact symptom-by-symptom response at each dose is not fully documented.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Which symptoms required the higher dose and whether focal foot pain followed the same dose-response curve as gait/tension.

#### F. Decision point

A compromise dose was pursued rather than abandoning the drug immediately.

#### G. Expert question

What does this benefit/toxicity balance imply about reasonable next therapeutic options at that time?

#### H. Status

Dose-responsive benefit with dose-limiting toxicity

#### I. Notes

Dose and tolerability must remain linked.

---

### Finding [F-156]: Later variable or missed procyclidine dosing continued to produce state-linked symptoms, but the evidence was less controlled than the 2017 withdrawal

**Date / period:** April 2018

**Reading method(s) used:** Treatment-response reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Medication-state trace / Confounder awareness

#### A. Case fact

The 2018 Neurology note states that the patient varied procyclidine unintentionally and, if he missed it, later felt tighter in the left buttock/piriformis region. The same note preserved the earlier full-withdrawal account involving the outside-left-foot/corn phenomenon.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-001914–CLIN-001925.

#### B. Record fact

This adds repeated real-world state dependence but does not reproduce the cleaner planned withdrawal conditions of October 2017.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

It supports persistence of medication-state sensitivity while carrying less mechanistic weight than the planned interruption.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Timing and adherence were variable and the symptom recorded here was not identical to the foot symptom.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether the later left-sided tightness and earlier lateral-foot response reflected one state-dependent syndrome or separate manifestations.

#### F. Decision point

Procyclidine remained in use with ongoing caution around vision.

#### G. Expert question

How much weight should repeated but uncontrolled missed-dose effects receive relative to the planned 2017 withdrawal?

#### H. Status

Supportive but less controlled state evidence

#### I. Notes

Repeated state dependence is not the same as repeated independent experiments.

---

### Finding [F-157]: Amantadine supplied a negative therapeutic experiment with an adverse mood signal

**Date / period:** 2018

**Reading method(s) used:** Treatment-response reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Negative response / Adverse-effect recording

#### A. Case fact

The record states that amantadine was tried, “didn’t do anything,” and was associated with sad thoughts, making future use unlikely.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002111–CLIN-002113.

#### B. Record fact

This is a treatment-specific negative response, not evidence against the Parkinsonian diagnosis or against responsiveness to other dopaminergic/neurological treatments.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Failure of one symptomatic option should not be generalized to the whole medication class or disease mechanism.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Other treatments produced substantial benefit.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The exact duration and dose of the amantadine trial are not visible in the cited extract.

#### F. Decision point

The treatment pathway moved to Neupro.

#### G. Expert question

None required for the documentary proposition; clinical interpretation of adequacy of the trial would require expertise.

#### H. Status

Negative treatment-specific response

#### I. Notes

A negative trial can be informative without being diagnostically dispositive.

---

### Finding [F-158]: Neupro demonstrated a non-monotonic dose-response with benefit, under-treatment, dyskinesia and behavioural adverse effects

**Date / period:** November 2018–2019

**Reading method(s) used:** Treatment-response reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Non-monotonic response / Dose-response / Adverse effects

#### A. Case fact

Neupro was titrated from low doses through 10 mg and as high as approximately 14–16 mg. The patient preferred 10 mg because higher doses produced chorea/dyskinesia and more problematic behavioural/impulsivity effects, but he also felt 10 mg did not do enough.
