Around 12 mg he once felt excellent, although the following day's deterioration was confounded by a flu-like illness.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002114–CLIN-002140.

#### B. Record fact

Benefit did not increase monotonically with dose. Higher exposure improved some states but crossed an adverse-effect threshold; lower exposure reduced adverse effects but left residual stiffness/cramping.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The record demonstrates a narrow therapeutic window rather than simple drug success or failure, and later caution about dopaminergic escalation had patient-specific empirical support.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

One particularly good 12 mg day was followed by illness, limiting interpretation of that dose point.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The best sustainable balance among movement benefit, dyskinesia and behavioural effects.

#### F. Decision point

The regimen was brought back to 10 mg and emphasis placed on regular timing.

#### G. Expert question

How should this dopamine-agonist response inform, but not automatically determine, later levodopa strategy?

#### H. Status

Established non-monotonic treatment response

#### I. Notes

Adverse response to a dopamine agonist is not automatically identical to response to levodopa.

---

### Finding [F-159]: Neupro also showed a within-day timing pattern, with useful effect peaking and then fading before later-day worsening

**Date / period:** 2019

**Reading method(s) used:** Treatment-response reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Timing / Wearing-off / Observation-window control

#### A. Case fact

The patient described the patch's useful effect peaking around 6 PM and fading thereafter, with an unpleasant “dread” as it wore off. The same note records that he looked close to normal in clinic but believed he would look stiffer later in the afternoon/evening.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002116–CLIN-002141.

#### B. Record fact

Treatment effect varied across the day, and the clinic observation captured only one medication-state window.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

A good examination at one time does not negate later-day treatment failure; treatment-response assessment must incorporate timing and wearing-off.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The patient's subjective account and clinic observation were not simultaneous measurements across the entire day.

#### E. What remains unresolved

How consistently the later-day worsening tracked pharmacological wearing-off versus other daily variables.

#### F. Decision point

Regularization of medication and lifestyle timing was emphasized to improve interpretability.

#### G. Expert question

What monitoring strategy would best distinguish pharmacological wearing-off from day-to-day variability?

#### H. Status

Supported within-day state dependence

#### I. Notes

Timing is a therapeutic variable independent of nominal daily dose.

---

### Finding [F-160]: First levodopa exposure in 2021 produced an early tolerability/benefit tradeoff but does not contemporaneously document a foot-pain response

**Date / period:** September–October 2021

**Reading method(s) used:** Treatment-response reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Symptom-domain specificity / Contemporaneous rationale

#### A. Case fact

Sinemet was started at 62.5 mg twice daily because the patient felt undertreated and was then increased while Kemadrin was removed for ocular-pressure/visual concerns. The patient was tolerating introduction/escalation, but slight intensity/behavioural change and early chorea were noted.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002330–CLIN-002353.

#### B. Record fact

The cited initiation note documents treatment transition and early adverse-effect sensitivity but does not record a specific foot-pain outcome.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The 2021 response evidence supports levodopa sensitivity and a new treatment phase; it should not be backfilled with the later pain-response narrative.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Later records clearly document pain/OFF relationships with levodopa.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether foot pain changed during the 2021 transition but was simply not documented in this note.

#### F. Decision point

Further dose increase was constrained because of chorea risk.

#### G. Expert question

What evidential weight can be assigned to an unrecorded symptom domain during an otherwise documented treatment transition?

#### H. Status

Levodopa sensitivity established; foot-pain response not documented here

#### I. Notes

Silence is not proof of no pain effect.

---

### Finding [F-161]: Stalevo exposure by 2022 supplied further patient-specific evidence of levodopa-related dyskinesia sensitivity

**Date / period:** 2022

**Reading method(s) used:** Treatment-response reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Dose sensitivity / Adverse-effect threshold

#### A. Case fact

The 2022 note records subtle excessive movement on a very low levodopa-containing regimen and recalls that Stalevo 75 had produced clearly obvious chorea/dyskinesia.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002392–CLIN-002400.

#### B. Record fact

This is a repeated levodopa-related adverse-effect signal independent of the earlier dopamine-agonist experience.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Later reluctance to simply increase levodopa dose had a documented patient-specific basis, but dose sensitivity does not answer timing or formulation questions.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The patient remained functional and the lower exposure produced only subtle excess movement.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The exact benefit-to-dyskinesia curve for different formulations and timings.

#### F. Decision point

No change was made at that visit.

#### G. Expert question

How should documented dyskinesia sensitivity affect consideration of lower-dose timing or controlled-release strategies?

#### H. Status

Established adverse-effect sensitivity

#### I. Notes

Dose ≠ timing ≠ formulation.

---

### Finding [F-162]: The 2022 morning “warm-up” pattern and demand-adjusted Sinemet were treatment-state clues rather than a completed causal experiment

**Date / period:** 2022

**Reading method(s) used:** Treatment-response reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Timing clue / Experimental-strength grading

#### A. Case fact

Most mornings the feet took 20–25 minutes to warm up and were sore around corns/calluses; the patient also adjusted Sinemet according to expected demands such as darts.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002375–CLIN-002399.

#### B. Record fact

The note contains temporal and demand-related treatment-state information, but it does not document a formal change immediately followed by a measured foot-pain outcome.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The 2022 entry is an important precursor to later OFF/coverage evidence but should not be over-classified as proof that the morning foot symptoms were levodopa-responsive at that time.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Structural corns/calluses were still explicitly referenced.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether morning symptoms tracked overnight dopaminergic under-coverage, mechanical first-step pain, or both.

#### F. Decision point

No specific overnight formulation experiment is documented in this visit.

#### G. Expert question

At what point does a repeatable temporal pattern justify a targeted timing/formulation trial?

#### H. Status

Treatment-state clue; not a completed experiment

#### I. Notes

Pass 11 grades the strength of natural experiments rather than treating all temporal associations equally.

---

### Finding [F-163]: Pregabalin response in early 2023 was variable across closely spaced accounts and should be classified as non-monotonic rather than simply effective or ineffective

**Date / period:** March 2023

**Reading method(s) used:** Treatment-response reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Non-monotonic response / Source-timepoint separation

#### A. Case fact

The urgent GP referral states pregabalin 25 mg twice daily initially gave some benefit and that after escalation the patient said a much higher dose was only just controlling symptoms. Two days later the Parkinson's nurse recorded 600 mg/day with continuing debilitating pain and no quality of life.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002623–CLIN-002688.

#### B. Record fact

The closely spaced descriptions differ materially in apparent efficacy. They may reflect changing symptoms, dose/timing differences, source wording or genuine fluctuation rather than a single stable response.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Pregabalin should not be summarized from March 2023 alone as either successful or failed. The record instead supports partial/variable response during a severe pain period.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Later 2023 and 2024 notes describe much stronger benefit from Lyrica.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Why efficacy appeared so different across these timepoints and whether the pain phenotype itself was changing.

#### F. Decision point

Urgent Neurology reassessment was sought rather than relying on further dose escalation alone.

#### G. Expert question

How should variable gabapentinoid response be interpreted when pain phenotype, concurrent Parkinson treatment and other exposures are changing?

#### H. Status

Non-monotonic / variable pregabalin response

#### I. Notes

Different source timepoints should not be forced into artificial consistency.

---

### Finding [F-164]: By the 2023 clinic, pregabalin had largely relieved pain while evening walking limitation and clawing persisted

**Date / period:** 2023 Neurology clinic

**Reading method(s) used:** Treatment-response reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Symptom dissociation / Multi-domain response

#### A. Case fact

The patient reported that Lyrica had largely removed the foot pain, while inability to complete a fourth evening walk, clawing and other movement difficulties persisted. The clinician considered an OFF/dystonic component and contemplated evening levodopa despite dyskinesia risk.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002424–CLIN-002495.

#### B. Record fact

Pain and movement/postural impairment did not move together under pregabalin treatment.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

This dissociation argues against treating pregabalin response as proof that every component of the foot syndrome shared one neuropathic mechanism.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Residual lateral-foot discomfort remained and the patient's description of “pain” versus attentional burden varied during the consultation.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Which residual symptoms were dystonic/OFF, mechanical, behavioural/attentional or mixed.

#### F. Decision point

Lyrica was left at 600 mg while additional dopaminergic and diagnostic options were considered.

#### G. Expert question

What diagnostic weight should be assigned to pain relief with persistent clawing and evening hypomobility?

#### H. Status

Supported treatment-response dissociation

#### I. Notes

A treatment can selectively improve one component of a composite syndrome.

---

### Finding [F-165]: Evening Stalevo use in 2023 supplied a pragmatic levodopa-state experiment linking better function and reduced pain experience to dopaminergic coverage

**Date / period:** 2023

**Reading method(s) used:** Treatment-response reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** ON/OFF state / Observation-window limitation

#### A. Case fact

The patient used evening Stalevo on darts nights and described taking it for “pep in the step” and “for pain.” The clinician observed that these evenings allowed him to play darts and interpreted the usual evening immobility as likely OFF-related; a later nurse contact similarly recorded worse morning/later-day shuffling and leg pain when Sinemet had not been taken and commenced morning Stalevo.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002453–CLIN-002474; CLIN-002573–CLIN-002583.

#### B. Record fact

The treatment-state evidence spans function, movement and pain experience, but the darts video itself was acknowledged by the patient as unrepresentative of most evenings and alcohol/extra levodopa were present.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The record supports a dopaminergically modifiable OFF component while retaining important confounders and without proving the generator of the underlying foot pain.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

March 2023 included a ten-day self-withdrawal report without perceived Parkinsonian change; structural and pregabalin-responsive pain remained relevant.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The proportion of evening impairment and pain attributable to OFF state versus other mechanisms.

#### F. Decision point

Stalevo was used/introduced to improve under-treated periods, with dyskinesia risk limiting escalation.

#### G. Expert question

How much weight should be given to pragmatic ON/OFF function and pain improvement when the observation window is confounded?

#### H. Status

Supported levodopa-state therapeutic signal with confounders

#### I. Notes

Function and pain modulation can move together without sharing a single generator.

---

### Finding [F-166]: In 2024 pregabalin response became a clearer dissociation experiment: pain improved substantially while toe curling did not

**Date / period:** 2024

**Reading method(s) used:** Treatment-response reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Dissociation / Symptom-domain specificity

#### A. Case fact

On Lyrica 150 mg four times daily, the patient reported that foot pain had been removed to a large degree, with the right foot much better and some residual left-sided pain, but toe curling during activities such as pumping a bicycle tyre was not relieved.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002695–CLIN-002710.

#### B. Record fact

Two manifestations in the same anatomical region responded differently to the same medication exposure.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The record strongly supports at least partial separation between the pregabalin-responsive pain component and the persistent toe-curling motor/postural component.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The two components may still interact through loading, dystonia or pain amplification.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The exact mechanism of the residual left pain and toe curling.

#### F. Decision point

The treatment focus shifted toward levodopa timing/absorption for evening function rather than further interpreting pregabalin failure globally.

#### G. Expert question

Does this dissociation support a distinct dystonic/postural treatment target?

#### H. Status

Strong symptom-dissociation evidence

#### I. Notes

This is more informative than a unitary “Lyrica helped the feet” statement.

---

### Finding [F-167]: The 2024 evening Sinemet timing change was a proposed natural experiment whose outcome is not documented in the same note

**Date / period:** 2024

**Reading method(s) used:** Treatment-response reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Timing ≠ dose / Outcome-not-assumed

#### A. Case fact

Neurology recommended moving the approximately 5 PM Sinemet dose to before food with a good volume of fluid, with the option to revert if problematic.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002695–CLIN-002710.

#### B. Record fact

This changed timing/absorption conditions without necessarily increasing total dose, but the cited note does not itself record the result of the experiment.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The treatment change shows that timing had become an explicit therapeutic variable. It cannot be scored as successful or unsuccessful without a later outcome source.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Later records may reflect cumulative regimen effects, but they do not necessarily isolate this specific timing change.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether the pre-food/fluid change materially improved evening symptoms.

#### F. Decision point

A low-cost timing modification was preferred before more aggressive dopaminergic escalation.

#### G. Expert question

How should undocumented outcomes of pragmatic timing changes be incorporated into retrospective treatment analysis?

#### H. Status

Natural experiment initiated; outcome not established

#### I. Notes

No response should be inferred from the fact that the change was recommended.

---

### Finding [F-168]: Substantial pregabalin self-reduction before the 2025 clinic was followed by severe pain recurrence, but the recurrence was confounded by the overnight dopaminergic gap

**Date / period:** 2024–2025

**Reading method(s) used:** Treatment-response reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Dechallenge / Multiple-variable confounding

#### A. Case fact

Pregabalin had been reduced from 600 mg/day to 50 mg three times daily. By the 2025 clinic the patient again described severe left-predominant morning foot pain, while also reporting that the feet improved once Parkinson medication reached his system.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002767–CLIN-002792.

#### B. Record fact

The pain recurrence followed a major pregabalin de-escalation, but it also occurred in a predictable overnight dopaminergic trough. Two therapeutic variables were therefore implicated at the same time.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The recurrence supports an ongoing pregabalin-responsive component but does not establish pregabalin withdrawal as the sole explanation.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The patient reported clear relief after Parkinson medication onset and had a long overnight gap without levodopa.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The relative contributions of reduced pregabalin and overnight dopaminergic under-coverage.

#### F. Decision point

The clinician considered increasing Lyrica but also chose to test overnight Sinemet coverage.

#### G. Expert question

How should a two-variable dechallenge/coverage situation be interpreted when both treatments plausibly modify pain experience?

#### H. Status

Confounded dechallenge signal

#### I. Notes

The recurrence cannot be assigned to one medication change alone.

---

### Finding [F-169]: The 2025 Sinemet CR intervention is the strongest later formulation/coverage experiment and its reported profound benefit supports overnight dopaminergic modulation without uniquely identifying mechanism

**Date / period:** 2025

**Reading method(s) used:** Treatment-response reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Formulation/coverage experiment / Treatment-response scope control

#### A. Case fact

Before CR, the patient reported severe morning pain, a long overnight medication gap and relief once Parkinson medication reached his system. Bedtime Sinemet CR was started specifically to extend overnight coverage. The later complaint response reports profound current relief after the CR change.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002777–CLIN-002800; CLIN-003239–CLIN-003269.

#### B. Record fact

This intervention altered formulation and duration of coverage rather than merely increasing a daytime immediate-release dose. The response occurred against a background of reduced pregabalin and established dyskinesia sensitivity.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The response strongly supports the proposition that overnight dopaminergic state materially modulated the morning foot-pain experience. It does not by itself distinguish painful dystonia, altered movement/loading, central pain modulation or another interacting mechanism.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Structural pathology, prior pregabalin responsiveness and the clinician's own “whatever the underlying cause” formulation remain relevant.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Why CR worked so strongly and whether the same coverage strategy would have produced comparable benefit earlier.

#### F. Decision point

The CR regimen was introduced cautiously because of dyskinesia and other adverse-effect concerns.

#### G. Expert question

What mechanistic and counterfactual conclusions can legitimately be drawn from the CR response?

#### H. Status

Strong formulation/coverage therapeutic signal; mechanism unresolved

#### I. Notes

This is the clearest example of why formulation and coverage must be separated from dose.

---

### Finding [F-170]: The longitudinal treatment history is explicitly non-monotonic and contains both concordant and discordant natural experiments

**Date / period:** 2017–2025

**Reading method(s) used:** Treatment-response reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Non-monotonic response / Counterevidence preservation

#### A. Case fact

The record contains strong positive responses, partial responses, negative trials, dose-limiting toxicity, symptom dissociation and one notable neutral self-withdrawal report in March 2023.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, sources cited in F-153–F-169.

#### B. Record fact

Response evidence accumulates but is not perfectly consistent: procyclidine withdrawal was strongly positive for state dependence; amantadine was negative; Neupro had a narrow window; pregabalin varied over time; levodopa produced both benefit and dyskinesia; the March 2023 Parkinson-medication interruption was neutral; later OFF/CR observations were strongly positive.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

A responsible synthesis must preserve the non-monotonic sequence rather than selecting only the responses that fit one mechanism.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Different episodes may involve different symptoms, disease states, doses, formulations and confounders, so apparent inconsistency need not imply unreliable reporting.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Which response discrepancies reflect true biological variability, different symptom generators, treatment timing, confounding or documentation limits.

#### F. Decision point

Later management increasingly shifted from drug-class choice alone toward timing, dose and formulation optimization.

#### G. Expert question

How should the full sequence of positive, negative and discordant responses be weighted in reconstructing mechanism and reasonable treatment options?

#### H. Status

Established non-monotonic longitudinal response pattern

#### I. Notes

Pass 11 preserves both therapeutic signals and counter-signals.



## 3K. Pass 12 — Complaint-Response Reading

**Pass scope:** This pass treats the 25 March 2025 consultant complaint response and the 15 April 2025 Quality and Patient Safety letter as documents produced **within** the evidential history, not as privileged summaries standing outside it. Each backward-looking claim is tested against the contemporaneous record for temporal reach, original rationale, preserved uncertainty, continuity of strategy and the distinction between treatment effect and mechanism. The governing control is that **a retrospective explanation is evidence of the later explanation; its historical reach must be independently shown**.

### Finding [F-171]: The 25 March 2025 complaint response is a participant document with its own date, purpose and evidential limits

**Date / period:** 25 March 2025

**Reading method(s) used:** Complaint-response reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Temporal reach / Participant-document control

#### A. Case fact

The consultant produced a formal response to a Quality and Patient Safety complaint concerning the patient's experience of pain management and historical clinical framing.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-003230–CLIN-003273.

#### B. Record fact

The response contains current 2025 observations, quotations from earlier material and retrospective explanations of what was known, intended and understood over prior years.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Those components have different evidential functions. A contemporaneous 2017 quotation remains evidence of 2017 wording; the connective explanation supplied around it in 2025 remains a 2025 reconstruction unless continuity is independently shown.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Many elements of the response are supported by earlier notes, particularly the long-running concern about dyskinesia and dopaminergic adverse effects.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Which backward-reaching propositions accurately describe the whole earlier period and which accurately describe only later phases of management.

#### F. Decision point

Each historical claim in the response must be audited separately rather than accepting or rejecting the response as a whole.

#### G. Expert question

What weight should a later clinician explanation receive where it is partly supported but temporally broader than the contemporaneous record?

#### H. Status

Established record-level control

#### I. Notes

The response is neither discarded nor privileged.

---

### Finding [F-172]: The response expressly acknowledges a gap between documented pain and the clinician's understanding of the patient's lived experience

**Date / period:** 25 March 2025

**Reading method(s) used:** Complaint-response reading / Patient–clinician perspective reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Perspective distinction

#### A. Case fact

The consultant stated that he had realised the pain and discomfort documented in recent letters but “did not understand that this was how he felt,” and apologised for the patient's experience.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-003239.

#### B. Record fact

The complaint response itself distinguishes **awareness that pain was documented** from **understanding the patient's experience of that pain**.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The document cannot safely be read as saying that the clinician had no knowledge of pain, nor as saying that the patient's experience had been fully understood throughout. It expressly records a communication/perception gap.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Earlier notes contain detailed descriptions of pain and functional limitation, showing that substantial symptom information was recorded.

#### E. What remains unresolved

What aspect of the patient's experience was not understood and when the gap arose.

#### F. Decision point

Historical analysis should distinguish documentation of symptoms from shared understanding of their severity, meaning and treatment implications.

#### G. Expert question

What level of shared understanding and documentation would ordinarily be expected where severe chronic pain is being managed across specialties?

#### H. Status

Established participant-perspective fact

#### I. Notes

The response itself supplies this distinction.

---

### Finding [F-173]: The complaint response preserves rather than closes uncertainty about the underlying pain generator

**Date / period:** 25 March 2025

**Reading method(s) used:** Complaint-response reading / Uncertainty-and-closure reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Causal-scope control

#### A. Case fact

The response refers to “whatever the underlying cause” of the peripheral neuropathic pain symptomatology and later states that Parkinson's does not intrinsically “cause all pain,” but may magnify pain from other causes.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-003240–CLIN-003244.

#### B. Record fact

The response uses a mixed causal model rather than claiming that the pain generator had been definitively established as Parkinsonian or peripheral neuropathy.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The complaint response cannot be used as final causal closure of the structural-versus-dystonic-versus-neuropathic-versus-modulatory question.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The document uses “peripheral neuropathic pain symptomatologies,” which is stronger phenomenological language than the early 2017 “perhaps a secondary peripheral neuropathy” formulation.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether a peripheral neuropathy existed and how much of the pain arose from peripheral, structural, dystonic, mechanical or central-modulatory mechanisms.

#### F. Decision point

The complaint response should be read as preserving a causal distinction between pain source and Parkinson-related pain modulation.

#### G. Expert question

Does the record contain sufficient objective evidence to diagnose peripheral neuropathy, or only a neuropathic pain phenotype/working hypothesis?

#### H. Status

Causal non-closure preserved

#### I. Notes

This is materially inconsistent with treating the response as a definitive causal adjudication.

---

### Finding [F-174]: The 2025 pain-amplification explanation has earlier support but its scope is modulation, not proof of pain origin

**Date / period:** 2018–2025

**Reading method(s) used:** Complaint-response reading / Retrospective reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Causal-proposition separation

#### A. Case fact

In 2025 the consultant explained that Parkinson's may magnify pain from another cause and that pain can worsen when levodopa wears off. Earlier Neurology material had already described a magnified pain experience in Parkinson's, and later 2023 notes explicitly discussed OFF-related worsening of pain.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-001949–CLIN-001951; CLIN-002470–CLIN-002474; CLIN-002573–CLIN-002583; CLIN-003241–CLIN-003252.

#### B. Record fact

The general modulation/amplification model is not invented solely for the complaint response; elements are visible earlier.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

This part of the 2025 explanation has substantial historical support, while still leaving the underlying generator of focal/structural/neuropathic-type pain open.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The March 2023 ten-day medication interruption was reported as producing no perceived Parkinsonian difference, and structural Podiatry pathology remained documented.

#### E. What remains unresolved

How consistently the pain-amplification model was applied specifically to the feet before later 2023.

#### F. Decision point

Modulation should be separated from direct causation when evaluating both earlier care and the complaint response.

#### G. Expert question

What clinical significance should be assigned to an established pain-modulation model where the underlying pain generator remains mixed or uncertain?

#### H. Status

Historically supported explanatory model with limited causal scope

#### I. Notes

Support for modulation is stronger than support for a single generator.

---

### Finding [F-175]: The claim that Sinemet had helped pain “for quite some time” is clearly supported by 2023, but the cited 2021 initiation record does not establish pain as the original levodopa rationale

**Date / period:** 2021–2025

**Reading method(s) used:** Complaint-response reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Contemporaneous rationale versus later-attributed rationale

#### A. Case fact

The 2025 response says the letters show a clear understanding for quite some time that Sinemet helps pain. The October 2021 note records first levodopa treatment for undertreatment and replacement of Kemadrin because of ocular pressure concerns. By later 2023 the record explicitly links Sinemet/Stalevo state to pain and OFF-related leg pain/shuffling.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002330–CLIN-002353; CLIN-002470–CLIN-002474; CLIN-002573–CLIN-002583; CLIN-003247–CLIN-003264.

#### B. Record fact

There is strong contemporaneous support for a recognised pain benefit by 2023. The located 2021 initiation note does not state chronic foot pain as the reason levodopa was begun.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The 2025 claim is supportable with a bounded temporal reach, but should not be used to rewrite the 2021 initiation rationale without additional contemporaneous documentation.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The 2022 morning-foot pattern and demand-adjusted Sinemet are suggestive but do not explicitly state that Sinemet was understood to treat the foot pain.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The earliest date at which the treating team explicitly understood levodopa to materially reduce the foot-pain experience.

#### F. Decision point

Historical treatment rationale should be dated to the earliest explicit support rather than inferred backward from later certainty.

#### G. Expert question

What significance, if any, should be attached to the interval between levodopa introduction and the first clearly documented pain-response formulation?

#### H. Status

Partly supported retrospective claim; temporal reach bounded

#### I. Notes

This refines rather than rejects the complaint-response statement.

---

### Finding [F-176]: The response's explanation that dopaminergic escalation was constrained by dyskinesia and related risks has strong contemporaneous support

**Date / period:** 2017–2025

**Reading method(s) used:** Complaint-response reading / Management-plan reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Historical-rationale testing

#### A. Case fact

The response attributes slow Sinemet escalation to the patient's young age, dyskinesia risk and other dopaminergic complications.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-001166–CLIN-001180; CLIN-002114–CLIN-002161; CLIN-002335–CLIN-002353; CLIN-002392–CLIN-002400; CLIN-003245–CLIN-003268.

#### B. Record fact

Those concerns are repeatedly visible contemporaneously and later became patient-specific through actual chorea/dyskinesia and dopamine-agonist behavioural effects.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

This is one of the complaint response's strongest historically supported retrospective explanations.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The same 2017 record contemplated that levodopa might work very well and might later be regretted as delayed; later management also differentiated timing/formulation from simple dose escalation.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether the degree and duration of caution, and the timing of alternative formulation/coverage trials, were clinically reasonable.

#### F. Decision point

Documentary support for the rationale must remain separate from expert judgment about the appropriateness of each decision.

#### G. Expert question

Was the risk-benefit strategy reasonable at each stage given the evolving evidence and available therapeutic alternatives?

#### H. Status

Strongly supported historical rationale; normative judgment reserved

#### I. Notes

Support for rationale is not proof of optimality.

---

### Finding [F-177]: The response's statement that alcohol was not considered to the exclusion of other causes is partly supported, while the complaint's anchoring concern remains a separate perspective proposition

**Date / period:** 2017–2025

**Reading method(s) used:** Complaint-response reading / Patient–clinician perspective reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Competing-framing preservation

#### A. Case fact

The response states that alcohol was a reasonable possible contributor to neuropathic symptoms but was not blamed to the exclusion of other causes. Earlier records considered alcohol alongside Parkinsonian, structural, neuropathic and other explanations; 2023 also prompted Fabry testing and renewed consideration of a separate foot problem.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-001142–CLIN-001149; CLIN-002458–CLIN-002495; CLIN-002711; CLIN-002783–CLIN-002792; CLIN-003249–CLIN-003258.

#### B. Record fact

The surviving record contains multiple causal possibilities, not alcohol alone. It also contains repeated attention to alcohol as a risk/confounder.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The record can support the narrow proposition that other causes were considered without resolving the broader complaint allegation that alcohol emphasis may have disproportionately shaped interpretation or management.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The patient's complaint specifically concerned bias/diagnostic anchoring and prolonged pain, a question not adjudicated merely by showing that alternative causes appeared somewhere in the notes.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The practical weight alcohol received relative to other hypotheses at each decision point.

#### F. Decision point

Framing frequency, treatment consequences and investigation choices must be assessed separately from the mere presence of alternative diagnoses in the record.

#### G. Expert question

Did alcohol-related reasoning materially displace or delay clinically indicated assessment of other plausible mechanisms?

#### H. Status

Partly supported response claim; anchoring allegation unresolved

#### I. Notes

Presence of alternatives is not itself a quantitative measure of diagnostic weighting.

---

### Finding [F-178]: “The majority of everything” being about Parkinson's is broadly true of the management context but does not establish that the foot-pain cause was understood from the outset

**Date / period:** 2017–2025

**Reading method(s) used:** Complaint-response reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Management context versus causal attribution

#### A. Case fact

The 2025 response says that from the outset the majority of management concerned Parkinson's.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-003253–CLIN-003254.

#### B. Record fact

Neurological investigation and treatment dominated much of the longitudinal care, but the July 2017 specialist note separately stated that the corns/calluses/burning could be separate or neuropathic and were “Not clear”; Podiatry repeatedly treated local structural pathology.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The broad management statement cannot be converted into a narrower historical claim that the foot-pain mechanism was understood as Parkinsonian throughout.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Parkinson-related gait/tone framing did enter Podiatry referrals and later pain management.

#### E. What remains unresolved

When the foot symptoms moved, if at all, from a parallel/uncertain problem into a stable Parkinson-modulated management proposition.

#### F. Decision point

Management focus and causal closure should remain separate in the complaint audit.

#### G. Expert question

At what point should accumulating foot medication-state evidence have altered the management architecture, if at all?

#### H. Status

Broadly supported context; narrower causal implication unsupported

#### I. Notes

This is a scope limitation, not a contradiction of the broad statement.

---

### Finding [F-179]: The early Healy second opinion is accurately invoked as evidence of specialist review, but its scope does not independently resolve the later foot-pain dispute

**Date / period:** September 2017 → March 2025

**Reading method(s) used:** Complaint-response reading / Referral-pathway reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Referral-purpose control

#### A. Case fact

The complaint response cites an early second opinion from Prof Daniel Healy.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-001734–CLIN-001760; CLIN-003255.

#### B. Record fact

Healy addressed probable PARK II, genetic testing and treatment sensitivity/over-use risk. The surviving letter does not show a specific task of resolving the focal foot-pain mechanism.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The existence of an expert second opinion supports neurological diagnostic/treatment review but cannot be treated as independent adjudication of an unasked foot-causation question.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The neurological and foot histories were clinically connected in the broader record.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether additional oral discussion about the feet occurred outside the surviving letter.

#### F. Decision point

Complaint-response reliance on specialist review must be bounded by the documented scope of that review.

#### G. Expert question

Would the information then available ordinarily have warranted a specific foot/dystonia/pain question to the movement-disorders specialist?

#### H. Status

Supported fact with scope limitation

#### I. Notes

A second opinion is only evidence for the questions it demonstrably addressed.

---

### Finding [F-180]: The response's assertion of openness to being wrong has some contemporaneous support but the word “always” remains retrospective

**Date / period:** 2023–2025, reaching back earlier

**Reading method(s) used:** Complaint-response reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Temporal-reach control

#### A. Case fact

The response says the consultant had “always completely accepted” that he might be wrong. In 2023 the clinician documented willingness to investigate the patient's doubts through Fabry testing and a requested repeat DaTscan and stated that diagnostic correctness mattered more than personal correctness.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002475–CLIN-002495; CLIN-003256–CLIN-003257.

#### B. Record fact

There is direct contemporaneous support for diagnostic openness in 2023.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

That supports the proposition for at least that period but does not independently prove an uninterrupted attitude across every earlier decision.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The repeat DaTscan was questioned/declined by Radiology because a prior abnormal scan existed, and the clinician himself retained firm belief in Parkinson's.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether the same degree of openness was operationally present at every earlier stage relevant to the foot-pain question.

#### F. Decision point

The audit should distinguish demonstrated 2023 openness from the broader retrospective “always” formulation.

#### G. Expert question

How should later documented diagnostic openness weigh against claims of earlier anchoring?

#### H. Status

Partly supported retrospective generalisation

#### I. Notes

Attitude claims are especially difficult to prove continuously from records.

---

### Finding [F-181]: “We have known it would be Sinemet or other dopamine agonist responsive” overstates the certainty visible in 2017

**Date / period:** 2017 → 2025

**Reading method(s) used:** Complaint-response reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Prospective-certainty control

#### A. Case fact

The response paraphrases the earlier position as knowledge that the disorder would be Sinemet or dopamine-agonist responsive.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-001601–CLIN-001612; CLIN-001742–CLIN-001749; CLIN-003260–CLIN-003264.

#### B. Record fact

The 2017 source itself says it was **possible** the patient would do very well with levodopa, while Healy's suspected PARK II formulation supported likely benefit from low-dose dopamine replacement.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Later treatment success can validate the expectation retrospectively, but it cannot convert the 2017 probabilistic language into contemporaneous certainty.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

Strong procyclidine response and the later confirmed PRKN diagnosis make the historical expectation increasingly plausible in hindsight.

#### E. What remains unresolved

What degree of levodopa responsiveness could reasonably have been predicted at each earlier date.

#### F. Decision point

The original uncertainty level should govern assessment of earlier decisions.

#### G. Expert question

What would a movement-disorders specialist reasonably have inferred about levodopa responsiveness from the 2017 phenotype and Healy opinion at the time?

#### H. Status

Retrospective overstatement of earlier certainty

#### I. Notes

Accuracy in hindsight is not identical to knowledge in advance.

---

### Finding [F-182]: The pregabalin statement compresses original initiation, later continuation and later levodopa-sparing function into one retrospective rationale

**Date / period:** 2023–2025

**Reading method(s) used:** Complaint-response reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Contemporaneous rationale versus later-attributed rationale

#### A. Case fact

The complaint response says pregabalin “was introduced” for neuropathic pain “in part to try to spare further Sinemet increase.”

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002623–CLIN-002688; CLIN-002788–CLIN-002800; CLIN-003265.

#### B. Record fact

The 2023 material identifies pregabalin as GP-originated treatment for severe burning/neuropathic-type pain, initially at 25 mg twice daily. By 2025 the neurologist explicitly proposes increasing Lyrica as an alternative to increasing Parkinson medication.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

A levodopa-sparing **later management function** is documented, but the surviving initiation record does not establish it as the original prescribing motive.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The complaint response may accurately summarize how pregabalin ultimately functioned in the combined strategy.

#### E. What remains unresolved

When, between initiation and 2025, levodopa-sparing became an explicit reason for continuation or dose choice.

#### F. Decision point

Treatment history must be separated into initiation rationale, continuation rationale, later management function and retrospective description.

#### G. Expert question

Does the distinction materially affect interpretation of whether dopaminergic options were being actively considered for pain during 2023–2024?

#### H. Status

Retrospective compression with later partial support

#### I. Notes

This is the guide's paradigmatic example of rationale drift.

---

### Finding [F-183]: The response accurately links the 2025 CR trial to a clinic plan and reports profound current benefit, while preserving an ambiguity about who initiated the exact March regimen

**Date / period:** March 2025

**Reading method(s) used:** Complaint-response reading / Treatment-response reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Initiator-versus-outcome distinction

#### A. Case fact

The 2025 clinic note proposed bedtime Sinemet CR as an overnight-coverage trial. The complaint response later states that Sinemet treatment had recently been escalated as CR and had provided profound relief, while also recording uncertainty about whether the patient's independent start was separate from the clinician's prescription.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-002792–CLIN-002800; CLIN-003259; CLIN-003266–CLIN-003270.

#### B. Record fact

The treatment concept and prescription are documented before the later report of profound relief; exact initiation logistics are not fully clear in the complaint response.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The response strongly supports current CR efficacy but should not be used to invent precision about who initiated which exact dose on which exact day.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The patient described the start as independent, while the consultant referenced a recent clinic prescription.

#### E. What remains unresolved

The exact dispensing/start chronology and whether any separate prescriber was involved.

#### F. Decision point

Efficacy can be accepted while initiation provenance remains qualified.

#### G. Expert question

What causal inference can be drawn from the CR response independent of the unresolved initiation logistics?

#### H. Status

Efficacy strongly supported; initiation detail partly unresolved

#### I. Notes

Do not let a provenance ambiguity erase a documented therapeutic response.

---

### Finding [F-184]: Saying that Sinemet had been used since 2021 “for the range of symptoms” associated with Parkinson's does not answer whether the specific overnight pain-coverage strategy had been explored earlier

**Date / period:** 2021–2025

**Reading method(s) used:** Complaint-response reading / Therapeutic-variable disaggregation

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Dose ≠ timing ≠ formulation ≠ coverage

#### A. Case fact

In answer to why the later successful possibility was not explored earlier, the response notes that the patient had been on forms of Sinemet since September 2021 and that clinicians had been careful with its use because of age and dyskinesia/ICD risks.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-003267–CLIN-003268; earlier CLIN-002330–CLIN-002353; CLIN-002375–CLIN-002405; CLIN-002695–CLIN-002710; CLIN-002792–CLIN-002800.

#### B. Record fact

Levodopa exposure from 2021 is established. The later question, however, concerned a particular formulation/coverage strategy that was first explicitly documented as an overnight CR trial in 2025.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Prior exposure to the drug class is not by itself an answer to whether timing, formulation or overnight coverage had been separately tested.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The record shows demand-based dosing, later Stalevo use and a 2024 timing adjustment, so levodopa management was not static.

#### E. What remains unresolved

When an overnight-coverage question became clinically indicated, if at all, before 2025.

#### F. Decision point

The complaint response's drug-class answer should be disaggregated into the therapeutic variables actually at issue.

#### G. Expert question

At what point would a reasonable specialist have considered formulation/coverage as distinct from increasing total levodopa dose?

#### H. Status

Factually supported broad answer; incomplete as to formulation/coverage question

#### I. Notes

This is a documentary distinction, not a conclusion that CR should have been used earlier.

---

### Finding [F-185]: The complaint response compresses several distinct management phases into one continuous retrospective strategy

**Date / period:** 2017–2025

**Reading method(s) used:** Complaint-response reading / Management-plan reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Strategy-phase separation

#### A. Case fact

The response presents a continuous narrative of young-patient dopaminergic caution, Sinemet use, pregabalin use and later CR escalation.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-003245–CLIN-003268.

#### B. Record fact

The contemporaneous history contains materially different operative phases: initial levodopa avoidance, procyclidine optimization, dopamine-agonist balancing, first levodopa introduction/replacement of Kemadrin, dose minimization, demand/OFF-state tailoring, timing/absorption adjustments and finally overnight CR coverage.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

One durable concern—dopaminergic adverse effects—can persist across these phases without making them one unchanged treatment strategy.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The response's high-level summary is not necessarily false; it is simply more compressed than the longitudinal management record.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether any important policy transition should have occurred earlier as the evidential state changed.

#### F. Decision point

Historical reasonableness should be assessed phase by phase, not against a retrospective single-strategy narrative.

#### G. Expert question

At which phase transitions, if any, did the existing strategy require formal reconsideration in light of new evidence?

#### H. Status

Retrospective compression of a genuinely evolving strategy

#### I. Notes

Continuity of one risk rationale does not prove continuity of every treatment rationale.

---

### Finding [F-186]: The Quality and Patient Safety letter records the complaint frame and routing decision; it is not an independent clinical adjudication

**Date / period:** 15 April 2025

**Reading method(s) used:** Complaint-response reading / Document-architecture reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Document-purpose control

#### A. Case fact

The Quality and Patient Safety letter summarized the patient's concerns about bias/diagnostic anchoring, noted that Sinemet CR had been effective for foot pain, and explained that a complaint relating solely to clinical judgment was forwarded to the consultant under the applicable pathway.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-003274–CLIN-003287.

#### B. Record fact

The letter reports the complaint allegation and administrative handling. It does not independently investigate or resolve the clinical merits of the historical dispute.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

Its summary of the complaint should not be treated as institutional confirmation that anchoring occurred or that earlier pain was preventable; likewise, the routing decision is not an exoneration of the clinical decisions.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The letter does establish that the complaint was formally received and characterized in those terms.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Any independent institutional or external adjudication of the substantive clinical questions.

#### F. Decision point

The letter's role is evidentially administrative, not clinical-expert determination.

#### G. Expert question

Would independent review require specialist assessment outside the treating clinician's retrospective account?

#### H. Status

Established administrative-document fact

#### I. Notes

Document purpose controls evidential weight.

---

### Finding [F-187]: Quoting the 2017 levodopa-deferral passage inside the 2025 response preserves the original words but not the entire later connective narrative

**Date / period:** 2017 wording reproduced in 2025

**Reading method(s) used:** Complaint-response reading / Document-architecture reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Quotation-versus-reconstruction separation

#### A. Case fact

The response reproduces the 2017 statements that levodopa/dopamine agonists were being delayed “at least for now,” that levodopa might work very well, and that later regret about delay was possible.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-001601–CLIN-001612; CLIN-003260–CLIN-003263.

#### B. Record fact

The quoted wording is independently present in the earlier record. The 2025 sentence that follows—summarising what “in essence” had been known and done—remains a later interpretive bridge.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The quotation has direct historical evidential force; the retrospective synthesis around it must still be tested against later intervening notes.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The quote itself contains both caution and an explicit acknowledgment that earlier levodopa could later be regretted as delayed.

#### E. What remains unresolved

How later evidence altered the original balance and when the “at least for now” policy should be regarded as having changed.

#### F. Decision point

Do not attribute the evidential status of the quoted source to the later connective prose merely because they appear together.

#### G. Expert question

What later findings would ordinarily be expected to trigger reconsideration of the original temporary deferral strategy?

#### H. Status

Original quotation established; retrospective bridge separately auditable

#### I. Notes

This is a document-architecture issue within the complaint response itself.

---

### Finding [F-188]: The complaint-response audit yields a mixed result: several historical explanations are well supported, several are temporally over-broad, and the core causal/counterfactual dispute remains unresolved

**Date / period:** 2017–2025

**Reading method(s) used:** Complaint-response reading

**Cross-cutting control(s):** Claim-by-claim audit / No privileged narrator

#### A. Case fact

The complaint response addresses pain causation, pain modulation, dopaminergic risk, earlier treatment strategy, alcohol, second opinion, diagnostic openness, pregabalin and Sinemet CR.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-003239–CLIN-003270, tested against earlier sources cited in F-172–F-187.

#### B. Record fact

The historical rationale for dopaminergic caution is strongly supported; pain modulation is supported by earlier and later evidence; the earliest temporal reach of Sinemet-as-pain-treatment is less clear; the pregabalin initiation rationale is retrospectively compressed; “known” responsiveness is stronger than 2017 wording; and the response itself preserves uncertainty over the underlying pain cause.

#### C. Interpretive consequence

The complaint response should neither be accepted wholesale nor dismissed wholesale. Its claims have different levels of support and different permissible temporal reach.

#### D. Counterevidence / competing evidence

The contemporaneous record contains genuine evidence supporting both cautious dopaminergic management and the later concern that medication-state clues accumulated while foot causation remained incompletely closed.

#### E. What remains unresolved

Whether any earlier decision fell below an appropriate clinical standard, whether a different pathway would have prevented pain, and what mechanism produced the later CR benefit.

#### F. Decision point

The documentary endpoint is a structured set of expert questions, not a legal or clinical verdict.

#### G. Expert question

How should an independent specialist evaluate the evolving risk-benefit decisions without importing hindsight from the later CR response or 2026 genetics?

#### H. Status

Established complaint-response audit conclusion at documentary level

#### I. Notes

This is the governing Pass 12 finding.


## 3L. Pass 13 — Evidential Persistence / Knowledge-Carry-Forward Audit

**Pass scope:** This pass reads the complete record longitudinally to identify facts that became newly knowable through treatment response, treatment withdrawal/rechallenge, temporal comparison, examination, investigation, or specialist observation, and then tests whether those facts retained visible evidential force when the same or related problem later recurred.

The key control is documentary rather than psychological:

> **A fact can lose visible evidential continuity in the record without proving that any clinician forgot, ignored, misunderstood, or negligently failed to act on it.**

This pass therefore asks what the event made newly knowable, what local management consequence followed, whether it should have triggered re-synthesis of an earlier unresolved proposition, and whether the new knowledge was later explicitly remembered, cited, incorporated, re-tested, or otherwise visible in the working model.

It does not collapse structural pathology into neurological pathology, pain generation into pain modulation, response into mechanism, or absence of later mention into proof of forgetting.

### Finding [F-189]: The 2017 procyclidine withdrawal/rechallenge made neurological medication-state modifiability of the foot symptom complex newly knowable, but that evidence was initially used more clearly for local medication management than for global foot-causation re-synthesis

**Date / period:** July–October 2017, with immediate follow-up into 2018

**Reading method(s) used:** Evidential persistence / treatment-response / uncertainty-and-closure

#### 1. Original unresolved proposition

In July 2017 Neurology had established a Parkinsonian syndrome but explicitly left open whether the corns, calluses and burning symptoms were separate, neuropathic, or otherwise related to the neurological disorder.

**Source location(s):** `complete-record.md`, CLIN-001142–CLIN-001149.

#### 2. Evidence-generating event

A planned interruption of procyclidine/Kemadrin for visual adverse effects created a within-patient treatment-state comparison. During withdrawal the patient contemporaneously reported increasing tension, restless feet and a deteriorating walk; the drug was then reintroduced at a lower dose and later increased because lower exposure did not control all symptoms.

**Source location(s):** CLIN-001770–CLIN-001801; CLIN-001822–CLIN-001839.

#### 3. Newly knowable proposition

After the withdrawal event it was reasonable to say, more strongly than before, that at least part of the symptom complex involving the feet changed with neurological medication state. That was a new patient-specific modifiability finding. It did not establish whether the underlying painful lesion, callus/corn, posture, loading pattern, or pain experience was neurologically generated.

#### 4. Contemporaneous documentation

**Patient report:** the 4 October 2017 email recorded that tension was building, the feet were restless and the walk was falling apart during the medication break.

**Clinician interpretation:** the later treating-neurologist note recorded the medication interruption as clinically meaningful and described the patient as more “shifty” and “unrelaxed” off procyclidine.

**Objective observation:** no contemporaneous objective measurement of the focal foot lesion's change during the withdrawal is located in the cited email.

#### 5. Immediate management consequence

Procyclidine was reintroduced despite the visual problem, first at a lower dose and then increased while vision was monitored. The treatment-state finding therefore had clear local management force.

#### 6. Re-synthesis trigger

Yes. The event materially bore on the earlier unresolved question about whether the foot symptom complex was entirely separate from the neurological disorder. It did not resolve that question, but it altered the evidential state by establishing medication-state sensitivity.

#### 7. Carry-forward test

The finding remained visible into the 2018 Neurology note, but later recurrent foot-pain episodes in 2022 and early 2023 do not expressly cite the 2017 withdrawal experiment. Similar medication-state relevance was later re-established through new levodopa/OFF observations rather than visibly carried forward from the original experiment.

#### 8. Later competing framing

Later records variously described structural corns/calluses, possible neuropathic pain, a separate foot problem, OFF-related leg pain/shuffling, and Parkinson-related amplification of pain.

#### 9. Documentary status

**local updating without global re-synthesis**

#### 10. Interpretive consequence

The record supports that the 2017 treatment-state finding influenced immediate medication management and remained visible at least into 2018. The surviving documentation does not show an equally explicit longitudinal re-synthesis of what that finding meant for the recurring foot-pain proposition before similar evidence was generated again years later.

#### 11. Expert question, if any

What clinical significance should have been attached to a patient-specific withdrawal/rechallenge showing foot involvement, and should it have changed the later investigation, referral design, or treatment strategy when focal/burning foot pain recurred?

---

### Finding [F-190]: The April 2018 clinician note added focal specificity by recording rapid return of the outside-left-foot/corn phenomenon, but that specific state-sensitive finding is not visibly re-used when focal left-foot pain later recurs

**Date / period:** 2018-04-24, tested against 2022–2025 recurrence

**Reading method(s) used:** Evidential persistence / treatment-response / causal-proposition reading

#### 1. Original unresolved proposition

The broader 2017 withdrawal had shown worsening involving the feet, but the exact relation between the neurological medication state and the recurrent focal lateral-left-foot/corn phenomenon remained uncertain.

#### 2. Evidence-generating event

At the April 2018 Neurology review, the treating neurologist recorded that when procyclidine had been stopped for two weeks, “the first thing” changing rapidly was the return on the foot of the outside-left-corn phenomenon.

**Source location(s):** CLIN-001914–CLIN-001919.

#### 3. Newly knowable proposition

The treating record now contained a more anatomically specific statement than the contemporaneous October email: the focal outside-left-foot/corn symptom complex had shown rapid temporal sensitivity to neurological medication withdrawal.

#### 4. Contemporaneous documentation

**Patient report as recorded by clinician:** rapid return of the outside-left-foot/corn phenomenon during the two-week withdrawal.

**Clinician interpretation:** the event was included in the neurological follow-up and was followed by continued procyclidine treatment.

**Objective observation:** the note does not state that the corn itself was objectively measured as enlarging or resolving with medication.

#### 5. Immediate management consequence

Procyclidine remained part of the regimen, with discussion of cautious future increases limited by visual effects.

#### 6. Re-synthesis trigger

Yes. The finding directly intersected with the earlier “Not clear” foot relationship and the parallel structural Podiatry model.

#### 7. Carry-forward test

Later notes again describe corns/calluses, outside-left-foot discomfort and severe foot pain, including 2022 and 2023, but the 2018 focal withdrawal finding is not expressly cited or weighed in those later causal formulations.

#### 8. Later competing framing

Later framing includes structural corn/callus treatment, “neuralgic” or neuropathic-type pain, a patient belief in a separate foot problem, and later OFF-related pain modulation.

#### 9. Documentary status

**loss of evidential continuity**

#### 10. Interpretive consequence

The documentary sequence permits the narrow conclusion that a specifically focal medication-state observation entered the treating Neurology record in 2018 and is not visibly active in later recurrent-foot-pain reasoning. That is not evidence that the fact was forgotten or clinically ignored; it is evidence that its longitudinal evidential use is not visible in the surviving notes.

#### 11. Expert question, if any

Should the 2018 focal withdrawal observation have materially altered the differential, referral question, or later treatment-state testing when the same anatomical region again became a major source of pain?

---

### Finding [F-191]: Repeated Podiatry examinations made persistent structural pathology objectively knowable, and that structural strand was only partly carried into later neurological pain formulations

**Date / period:** 2017–2019, tested against 2023–2025

**Reading method(s) used:** Evidential persistence / examination / causal-proposition reading

#### 1. Original unresolved proposition

It remained open whether the foot syndrome was primarily structural/mechanical, neurological/dystonic, neuropathic, medication-modulated, or mixed.

#### 2. Evidence-generating event

Serial Podiatry examinations documented recurrent left 5th MTPJ callus/HD/corn, clawed toes and other structural/biomechanical findings, with repeated debridement, toe props, insoles and off-loading.

**Source location(s):** CLIN-002864–CLIN-002988; CLIN-003043–CLIN-003081; CLIN-003083–CLIN-003107.

#### 3. Newly knowable proposition

The record could say with increasing confidence that persistent local structural pathology existed independently of any later neurological interpretation. That did not determine whether neurological state altered posture, loading, pain intensity or functional impact at the same site.

#### 4. Contemporaneous documentation

**Patient report:** recurrent local pain and need for foot care.

**Clinician observation:** repeated direct Podiatry findings of callus/HD, clawed toes and pressure/off-loading needs.

**Clinician interpretation:** the service treated a structural/biomechanical manifestation; the 2019 re-referral also linked foot issues to increased tone and abnormal gait in the Parkinson's context.

#### 5. Immediate management consequence

The structural findings drove repeated local treatment, insoles, toe props, debridement and off-loading.

#### 6. Re-synthesis trigger

Yes. Persistent structural pathology was relevant whenever later records considered neuropathic pain, dystonia, OFF symptoms, or Parkinson-related pain amplification.

#### 7. Carry-forward test

Later Neurology did retain some structural awareness: the 2023 note mentions a corn addressed many times and the 2025 model explicitly allows pain from “whatever cause.” However, later pain descriptions often foreground neuropathic terminology or Parkinson modulation rather than explicitly re-integrating the full structural history.

#### 8. Later competing framing

Later records include “neuropathic pain,” possible peripheral neuropathy, OFF-related symptoms, dystonia/clawing and pain amplification.

#### 9. Documentary status

**partial carry-forward**

#### 10. Interpretive consequence

The structural strand was not erased from the record, but its full evidential weight was not consistently visible in later neurological causal summaries. The documentary sequence supports coexistence rather than substitution.

#### 11. Expert question, if any

How should the persistent structural lesion history have been integrated with later evidence of neurological modulation when determining the likely pain generator(s) and management plan?

---

### Finding [F-192]: The 2018–2019 Neupro titration generated patient-specific evidence of a narrow dopaminergic therapeutic window, and that risk knowledge retained strong longitudinal force

**Date / period:** late 2018–2019, carried forward through 2025

**Reading method(s) used:** Evidential persistence / treatment-response / management-plan reading

#### 1. Original unresolved proposition

How far could dopamine replacement be increased to improve stiffness, cramping and function without producing unacceptable adverse effects in a very young patient?

#### 2. Evidence-generating event

Neupro was titrated from low dose through higher exposures, with higher doses producing chorea/dyskinesia and behavioural/impulsivity concerns while the lower 10 mg dose was preferred but felt insufficient.

**Source location(s):** CLIN-002114–CLIN-002161.

#### 3. Newly knowable proposition

Theoretical concern about young-onset dopaminergic adverse effects became patient-specific: this patient could develop dyskinesia and behavioural effects at higher dopamine-agonist exposure while lower exposure left residual under-treatment.

#### 4. Contemporaneous documentation

**Patient report:** higher doses produced unwanted “swinging”/choreiform movement and behavioural changes; 10 mg felt inadequate.

**Clinician observation/interpretation:** the situation was described as an extremely difficult therapeutic “tight rope,” with future levodopa decisions explicitly shaped by the observed adverse-effect pattern.

#### 5. Immediate management consequence

The dose was reduced to 10 mg and the plan emphasized regularity and caution rather than further escalation.

#### 6. Re-synthesis trigger

Yes. The event converted generic risk rationale into an individualized treatment constraint relevant to later levodopa choices.

#### 7. Carry-forward test

The dyskinesia/impulse-control risk remained explicitly active in the 2021 Sinemet introduction, 2022 treatment discussion, 2023 decisions about evening levodopa, the 2025 CR plan and the complaint response.

#### 8. Later competing framing

Later benefit from levodopa and CR created pressure in the opposite direction, but the adverse-effect constraint continued to be documented.

#### 9. Documentary status

**evidential continuity preserved**

#### 10. Interpretive consequence

This is a strong positive-control example showing that some treatment-response knowledge was repeatedly carried forward and used to govern later decisions.

#### 11. Expert question, if any

Given the preserved dyskinesia risk, what alternative timing, formulation or coverage strategies were clinically reasonable before increasing total dopaminergic exposure?

---

### Finding [F-193]: Early chorea on low-dose levodopa and greater dyskinesia on Stalevo 75 produced further patient-specific levodopa-sensitivity evidence that remained visible later

**Date / period:** 2021–2022, carried forward through 2025

**Reading method(s) used:** Evidential persistence / treatment-response

#### 1. Original unresolved proposition

Whether levodopa could provide sufficient benefit after Kemadrin removal while remaining tolerable in a patient already known to be dyskinesia-prone.

#### 2. Evidence-generating event

Sinemet was introduced at 62.5 mg twice daily and increased to four times daily; slight chorea appeared early. By 2022 the record recalled more obvious dyskinesia on Stalevo 75 while subtle excess movement remained even on minimal levodopa exposure.

**Source location(s):** CLIN-002335–CLIN-002353; CLIN-002392–CLIN-002400.

#### 3. Newly knowable proposition

Levodopa sensitivity was not merely theoretical or extrapolated from dopamine agonists: even relatively low levodopa exposure could produce choreiform excess movement in this patient.

#### 4. Contemporaneous documentation

**Patient report:** awareness of chorea while playing darts and clear recollection of being dyskinetic on Stalevo 75.

**Clinician observation:** subtle excess movement was visible at clinic on minimal levodopa.

**Clinician interpretation:** dosing should remain minimal and future increases would likely increase dyskinesia.

#### 5. Immediate management consequence

The dose was constrained; no major escalation was made in the 2022 review.

#### 6. Re-synthesis trigger

Yes. This strengthened the individualized risk side of every later pain-versus-dopaminergic-benefit decision.

#### 7. Carry-forward test

The risk is repeatedly cited in 2023, 2025 and the complaint response when evening levodopa, pain relief and CR treatment are discussed.

#### 8. Later competing framing

Increasing evidence that levodopa improved pain and function created a competing therapeutic benefit, but did not displace the dyskinesia constraint.

#### 9. Documentary status

**evidential continuity preserved**

#### 10. Interpretive consequence

The longitudinal record shows strong continuity of adverse-effect evidence and provides an important comparator when assessing the less consistent carry-forward of foot-modulation evidence.

#### 11. Expert question, if any

How should confirmed levodopa sensitivity alter the choice between dose escalation, timing changes, controlled-release formulations and non-dopaminergic pain treatment?

---

### Finding [F-194]: The 2022 “20–25 minute warm-up” of the feet created a reproducible temporal clue that later became more salient when the 2025 overnight-coverage experiment succeeded

**Date / period:** 2022, tested against 2025

**Reading method(s) used:** Evidential persistence / temporal-association / retrospective control

#### 1. Original unresolved proposition

The mechanism of recurrent morning foot soreness around corns/calluses was not established.

#### 2. Evidence-generating event

The patient reported that on most mornings the feet took approximately 20–25 minutes to “warm up,” with soreness around the corns and calluses, while he was also adjusting Sinemet according to expected demands.

**Source location(s):** CLIN-002375–CLIN-002386.

#### 3. Newly knowable proposition

A reproducible morning temporal phenotype existed. The note did not establish whether the improvement reflected medication onset, movement/activity, footwear, mechanical warming, natural fluctuation, or a mixed process.

#### 4. Contemporaneous documentation

**Patient report:** most mornings required 20–25 minutes for the feet to warm up.

**Clinician interpretation:** no explicit OFF or overnight-coverage interpretation is recorded in that 2022 passage.

**Objective observation:** no before/after morning examination is documented there.

#### 5. Immediate management consequence

No specific foot-directed timing/formulation change was made at that visit.

#### 6. Re-synthesis trigger

Potentially. The pattern bore on the temporal behavior of the recurring foot syndrome but did not yet isolate the therapeutic variable responsible.

#### 7. Carry-forward test

The 2025 note again documents severe first-step morning pain, a long overnight medication gap and relief when Parkinson medication reached the system, leading to Sinemet CR. The 2022 “warm-up” clue is not explicitly cited as antecedent evidence.

#### 8. Later competing framing

Structural corns/calluses remained plausible; later pain was also framed as neuropathic and Parkinson-modulated.

#### 9. Documentary status

**partial carry-forward**

#### 10. Interpretive consequence

The same broad temporal pattern reappears and becomes much more informative in 2025, but the record does not show that the 2022 observation itself retained explicit evidential force in the intervening model.

#### 11. Expert question, if any

Should a predictable morning “warm-up” pattern in a levodopa-treated young-onset Parkinson patient have prompted earlier structured testing of medication timing, overnight coverage or other state variables?

---

### Finding [F-195]: The March 2023 ten-day Parkinson-medication interruption generated discordant dechallenge evidence that remained visible at the perspective level but was not explicitly reconciled with the later OFF-related model

**Date / period:** March 2023, tested against later 2023

**Reading method(s) used:** Evidential persistence / treatment-withdrawal / patient–clinician perspective

#### 1. Original unresolved proposition

Whether the severe bilateral burning foot/lower-leg pain was Parkinson-related, neuropathic, structurally generated, psychologically mediated, or otherwise separate.

#### 2. Evidence-generating event

The patient reported that he had stopped Parkinson medication for ten days and felt no difference “from a Parkinson's perspective” while the severe pain episode was ongoing.

**Source location(s):** CLIN-002675–CLIN-002688.

#### 3. Newly knowable proposition

The patient's own uncontrolled dechallenge did not reveal an obvious perceived Parkinsonian deterioration over that interval. This was discordant with a simple model in which all relevant symptoms would necessarily worsen off medication. The source does **not** clearly state that the foot pain itself was unchanged, so it cannot be treated as a direct negative pain-response test.

#### 4. Contemporaneous documentation

**Patient report:** no perceived difference from a Parkinson's perspective after ten days off medication.

**Clinician transmission:** the Parkinson's nurse relayed the observation to the consultant and asked for advice.

**Objective observation:** no formal OFF examination or standardized pain comparison is documented.

#### 5. Immediate management consequence

The finding contributed to urgent review rather than immediate closure; the patient remained on a pathway for Neurology reassessment.

#### 6. Re-synthesis trigger

Yes. It was patient-specific competing evidence relevant to how strongly medication-state dependence should be inferred.

#### 7. Carry-forward test

Later 2023 documentation preserves the patient's broader belief that a separate foot problem existed and explicitly records a “misalignment” of perspectives. However, the exact ten-day withdrawal observation is not visibly re-tested or directly reconciled when later leg pain/shuffling is classified as OFF-related.

#### 8. Later competing framing

Later observations show worse mobility/pain when Sinemet is missed, improved function with Stalevo on board, and clinician interpretation of typical OFF symptoms.

#### 9. Documentary status

**partial carry-forward**

#### 10. Interpretive consequence
