The competing proposition remained alive at the perspective level, but the specific negative/neutral dechallenge was not visibly integrated into the later positive ON/OFF model. Because the dechallenge was uncontrolled and not pain-specific, the documentary sequence cannot determine what weight it deserved.

#### 11. Expert question, if any

What clinical weight should be given to this self-directed ten-day withdrawal when later, more symptom-specific ON/OFF observations pointed in the opposite direction?

---

### Finding [F-196]: Later 2023 ON/OFF and Stalevo observations converted medication-state relevance of leg pain/shuffling into an explicit working proposition that was visibly carried forward

**Date / period:** later 2023 through 2025

**Reading method(s) used:** Evidential persistence / treatment-state comparison

#### 1. Original unresolved proposition

After the March 2023 conflict, it remained uncertain whether the pain and walking difficulty were materially related to dopaminergic state.

#### 2. Evidence-generating event

Later records documented worse mobility and shuffling in the morning or later in the day when Sinemet had not been taken, improvement on evenings with Stalevo on board, and a nurse/consultant conclusion that the leg pain and shuffling appeared OFF-related. Stalevo was then introduced in the morning.

**Source location(s):** CLIN-002453–CLIN-002474; CLIN-002573–CLIN-002583.

#### 3. Newly knowable proposition

By this stage the record could support an explicit patient-specific working proposition that at least part of the leg pain/shuffling/walking difficulty was dopaminergically state-sensitive and could emerge in an OFF state.

#### 4. Contemporaneous documentation

**Patient report:** worse mobility at medication gaps; Stalevo described as giving “pep in the step” and helping pain.

**Clinician observation:** evening video showed some movement abnormalities but was acknowledged as not representative of the patient's usual worst state.

**Clinician interpretation:** the described symptoms sounded like typical OFF symptoms; the nurse explicitly recorded undertreatment and OFF-related leg pain/shuffling.

#### 5. Immediate management consequence

Morning Stalevo was commenced, while further evening levodopa was considered but constrained by chorea risk.

#### 6. Re-synthesis trigger

Yes. This was a major re-synthesis point because it provided new symptom-state information after the March uncertainty.

#### 7. Carry-forward test

The medication-state proposition remains visible in the 2024 timing discussion, the 2025 observation that the feet improve when Sinemet reaches the system, the CR trial, and the 2025 complaint response.

#### 8. Later competing framing

Pregabalin benefit, persistent structural pathology and the clinician's distinction between pain generation and Parkinson-related amplification remained alongside the OFF model.

#### 9. Documentary status

**evidential continuity preserved**

#### 10. Interpretive consequence

This is a strong example of new evidence changing the working model and then retaining visible force in subsequent management.

#### 11. Expert question, if any

At what point did the accumulated OFF-related evidence justify structured testing of evening/overnight timing or formulation, given the competing dyskinesia risk?

---

### Finding [F-197]: The 2023 pregabalin course made non-dopaminergic pain modifiability newly knowable, and that information retained visible force despite a variable response

**Date / period:** 2023–2025

**Reading method(s) used:** Evidential persistence / treatment-response / therapeutic-variable disaggregation

#### 1. Original unresolved proposition

Whether the severe burning pain represented a neuropathic process, another pain generator, Parkinson-related amplification, or a mixed syndrome.

#### 2. Evidence-generating event

Pregabalin was started by the GP at 25 mg twice daily and escalated. Early sources report some benefit but also severe/debilitating pain despite high-dose treatment; later in 2023 the patient reported that the pain was largely gone, and in 2024 Neurology recorded that Lyrica had removed most foot pain.

**Source location(s):** CLIN-002623–CLIN-002688; CLIN-002424–CLIN-002495; CLIN-002695–CLIN-002710.

#### 3. Newly knowable proposition

The pain experience was materially modifiable by pregabalin, but the response was incomplete and non-monotonic. That supported a pain-processing/neuropathic-treatment signal without proving a peripheral neuropathy as the generator.

#### 4. Contemporaneous documentation

**Patient report:** some early benefit, severe pain despite escalation at one stage, later substantial relief.

**Clinician interpretation:** Lyrica was continued because of benefit; alternative neuropathic-pain agents were considered if needed.

**Objective observation:** no treatment-specific objective neuropathy marker is documented in these passages.

#### 5. Immediate management consequence

Pregabalin was escalated and maintained at high dose; additional neuropathic-pain treatment was deferred when pain was reported as controlled.

#### 6. Re-synthesis trigger

Yes. It showed that the pain could respond to a non-dopaminergic intervention and therefore should not be reduced to a single dopaminergic mechanism.

#### 7. Carry-forward test

The benefit remained explicit in 2024, and when the patient later self-reduced the dose the 2025 clinician specifically considered that he was “missing the Lyrica.” The complaint response also cites the reduction and subsequent deterioration.

#### 8. Later competing framing

Levodopa/OFF and CR responses independently supported dopaminergic modulation, while toe curling persisted despite analgesic benefit.

#### 9. Documentary status

**evidential continuity preserved**

#### 10. Interpretive consequence

The longitudinal model retained the idea that pregabalin materially influenced pain even while the record also accumulated evidence for dopaminergic modulation. The two treatment responses need not identify the same mechanism.

#### 11. Expert question, if any

How should a substantial but incomplete pregabalin response be weighted alongside dopaminergic state sensitivity when distinguishing neuropathic pain, pain amplification and motor/postural contributors?

---

### Finding [F-198]: The 2024 dissociation between substantial pain relief and persistent toe curling made the pain and motor/postural components more clearly separable, but that distinction was not fully developed into a longitudinal causal model

**Date / period:** 2024, tested against 2025

**Reading method(s) used:** Evidential persistence / symptom-dissociation / causal-proposition reading

#### 1. Original unresolved proposition

Whether foot pain, toe curling/clawing and activity-related walking limitation were manifestations of one mechanism or interacting but distinct phenomena.

#### 2. Evidence-generating event

On pregabalin 150 mg four times daily, the patient reported that the foot pain was largely removed, particularly on the right, while toe curling still occurred during sustained activity such as pumping a bicycle tyre and remained a limiting symptom.

**Source location(s):** CLIN-002695–CLIN-002710.

#### 3. Newly knowable proposition

Pain and toe curling were at least partly dissociable under the same treatment state. A treatment could improve pain without eliminating the motor/postural phenomenon.

#### 4. Contemporaneous documentation

**Patient report:** Lyrica removed the pain substantially but did not relieve the toe curling.

**Clinician observation/interpretation:** the note preserved both findings separately and adjusted evening Sinemet timing rather than treating the whole syndrome as a single pain variable.

#### 5. Immediate management consequence

Pregabalin was continued and an evening Sinemet timing/absorption adjustment was proposed.

#### 6. Re-synthesis trigger

Yes. The dissociation bore directly on whether “foot pain,” clawing/dystonia and neurological medication state should be analyzed as separate propositions.

#### 7. Carry-forward test

The 2025 note continues to distinguish pain from dyskinesia and medication-state effects, but the specific 2024 finding that pain relief left toe curling intact is not prominently re-used in the later complaint-centered pain narrative.

#### 8. Later competing framing

The 2025 focus shifts to severe morning pain, reduced Lyrica and overnight dopaminergic coverage; the complaint response emphasizes pain modulation more than the earlier pain-versus-toe-curling dissociation.

#### 9. Documentary status

**local updating without global re-synthesis**

#### 10. Interpretive consequence

The record contains a clinically informative dissociation that argues against treating every foot manifestation as one treatment-responsive entity. Its full causal significance is not visibly developed later.

#### 11. Expert question, if any

Should persistent toe curling despite substantial analgesic response have prompted a more explicit dystonia/posture/mechanical-loading analysis separate from the pain-treatment pathway?

---

### Finding [F-199]: The 2024–2025 pregabalin self-reduction created a dechallenge signal that materially strengthened the proposition that pregabalin had been contributing to pain control, and that knowledge was explicitly carried forward

**Date / period:** approximately late 2024 to 2025

**Reading method(s) used:** Evidential persistence / treatment dechallenge

#### 1. Original unresolved proposition

How much of the prior improvement in foot pain was attributable to pregabalin, as opposed to other changes in neurological state, activity, mood or natural fluctuation?

#### 2. Evidence-generating event

The patient reduced Lyrica from 600 mg/day to 50 mg three times daily. By the 2025 review he again had severe left-greater-than-right morning pain, while the clinician compared the new state with the prior maximum-dose period.

**Source location(s):** CLIN-002767–CLIN-002792.

#### 3. Newly knowable proposition

The temporal deterioration after a major dose reduction strengthened, though did not prove, the proposition that pregabalin had been materially suppressing part of the pain syndrome.

#### 4. Contemporaneous documentation

**Patient report:** severe recurrent morning pain after the reduction.

**Clinician interpretation:** the clinician explicitly suspected that the patient was “missing the Lyrica” from the perspective of the feet.

**Objective observation:** no standardized before/after pain measurement is recorded.

#### 5. Immediate management consequence

Increasing Lyrica was considered as an alternative to increasing Parkinson medication, while a separate overnight dopaminergic-coverage trial was also initiated.

#### 6. Re-synthesis trigger

Yes. The dechallenge created simultaneous evidence for at least two modifiable dimensions: pregabalin-responsive pain and dopaminergic overnight coverage.

#### 7. Carry-forward test

The complaint response explicitly states that Lyrica had been self-reduced and that pain deteriorated, demonstrating direct carry-forward of the dechallenge interpretation.

#### 8. Later competing framing

The same 2025 episode also contained strong Sinemet-onset and CR response evidence, so the worsening cannot be uniquely assigned to pregabalin withdrawal.

#### 9. Documentary status

**evidential continuity preserved**

#### 10. Interpretive consequence

The record preserved the pregabalin dechallenge as an active explanatory variable rather than allowing the later CR response to erase it.

#### 11. Expert question, if any

How should the simultaneous pregabalin reduction and overnight dopaminergic gap be disentangled when estimating the contribution of each treatment variable to the 2025 morning pain?

---

### Finding [F-200]: The 2025 medication-onset observation and Sinemet CR response made overnight dopaminergic coverage of morning foot pain directly testable and then strongly supported, with immediate evidential continuity thereafter

**Date / period:** 2025

**Reading method(s) used:** Evidential persistence / formulation-and-coverage natural experiment

#### 1. Original unresolved proposition

Whether severe predictable morning pain reflected an overnight dopaminergic coverage gap, and whether modifying formulation/duration rather than simply increasing daytime dose could improve it.

#### 2. Evidence-generating event

The patient reported that once Parkinson medication “hit his system” the feet improved. The record documented an evening last dose around 8 PM, sleep until approximately 08:45, and then introduced bedtime Sinemet CR specifically to test overnight/early-morning coverage. The later complaint response reports profound relief.

**Source location(s):** CLIN-002777–CLIN-002800; CLIN-003239–CLIN-003269.

#### 3. Newly knowable proposition

The 2025 sequence strongly supported that the morning pain experience was modifiable by dopaminergic **timing/formulation/duration of coverage**, not merely by total daily dose. This was more specific knowledge than a general statement that levodopa sometimes reduces pain.

#### 4. Contemporaneous documentation

**Patient report:** feet improved when medication reached the system; later profound relief with CR.

**Clinician interpretation:** the overnight gap was explicitly identified as a plausible therapeutic target, with CR selected as a cautious trial.

**Objective observation:** no blinded or standardized analgesic outcome is documented.

#### 5. Immediate management consequence

Sinemet CR was initiated with a slow planned escalation and explicit warnings about dyskinesia and dreaming.

#### 6. Re-synthesis trigger

Yes. This was a major re-synthesis point because it separated dose from formulation/coverage and materially altered what could be said about morning pain modifiability.

#### 7. Carry-forward test

The benefit was immediately carried into the March 2025 complaint response and April Quality and Patient Safety correspondence.

#### 8. Later competing framing

The response retrospectively extends the finding into a broader claim that Sinemet pain responsiveness had been known “for quite some time.” That broader temporal claim must be tested separately from the well-supported 2025 CR result.

#### 9. Documentary status

**evidential continuity preserved**

#### 10. Interpretive consequence

The 2025 CR experiment is a strong positive example of a newly generated fact rapidly entering the explicit clinical and complaint model. It does not establish the original pain generator or prove that the same formulation would necessarily have worked at an earlier date.

#### 11. Expert question, if any

Given the prior temporal and medication-state clues, when would a timing/formulation/overnight-coverage trial have become clinically indicated, if at all?

---

### Finding [F-201]: The PARK2/PRKN hypothesis shows preserved proposition-level continuity from specialist suspicion to later genetic confirmation, although confirmation itself arrived much later

**Date / period:** 2017–2026

**Reading method(s) used:** Evidential persistence / investigation / specialist observation

#### 1. Original unresolved proposition

What specific disease form accounted for the unusually young, symmetric Parkinsonism/dystonia.

#### 2. Evidence-generating event

The 2017 specialist record raised autosomal-recessive Parkinsonism and specifically PRKN/PARK2; Prof Healy recorded that the patient “probably has Park II” and recommended testing. The 2018 Neurology note continued to identify PARK2/PRKN as a suspected cause. In February 2026 genetic testing confirmed two pathogenic Parkin variants causing the Parkinsonism and dystonia.

**Source location(s):** CLIN-001520–CLIN-001525; CLIN-001742–CLIN-001749; CLIN-001955–CLIN-001956; CLIN-003220–CLIN-003229.

#### 3. Newly knowable proposition

In 2017 the proposition became specifically testable and specialist-supported; in 2026 it became confirmed. The later test changed the evidential status from hypothesis to established genetic causation.

#### 4. Contemporaneous documentation

**Specialist interpretation:** PRKN/PARK2 was repeatedly identified as a plausible/likely monogenic explanation.

**Investigation:** the 2026 consultant letter directly states that two pathogenic Parkin variants were present and that Parkinsonism and dystonia were due to compound heterozygous Parkin mutations.

#### 5. Immediate management consequence

Earlier notes treated genetic confirmation as potentially informative but not necessarily management-changing; the 2026 letter planned follow-up after confirmation.

#### 6. Re-synthesis trigger

Yes, at the etiological level. The confirmation resolved the disease-form proposition but did not itself resolve the generator of every historical foot lesion or pain episode.

#### 7. Carry-forward test

The PRKN hypothesis remained visible across the early specialist record and was ultimately tested and confirmed rather than disappearing from the longitudinal record.

#### 8. Later competing framing

Other etiologies and rare overlaps were explored while confirmation remained absent, including later Fabry testing and broader diagnostic discussion.

#### 9. Documentary status

**evidential continuity preserved**

#### 10. Interpretive consequence

This is another positive-control example: a specific hypothesis retained enough longitudinal identity to be recognized and later confirmed, while the record appropriately preserved uncertainty before the result.

#### 11. Expert question, if any

Would confirmation of PRKN at an earlier stage have materially changed symptomatic treatment, counselling, or the interpretation of dystonic/foot manifestations, given the knowledge available at the time?

---

### Pass 13 end-point table — strongest examples of preserved vs reduced evidential continuity

| Earlier evidence | What became newly knowable | Later continuity | Classification | Documentary consequence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2017 procyclidine withdrawal with restless feet/gait deterioration | The foot symptom complex was neurologically medication-state modifiable | Visible locally and into 2018; not expressly invoked when severe foot pain recurred years later | **local updating without global re-synthesis** | Strong early modifiability evidence existed, but later similar reasoning appears to have been re-derived from new observations |
| 2018 rapid return of outside-left-foot/corn phenomenon off procyclidine | A focal lateral-foot phenomenon was specifically state-sensitive | Later focal left-foot pain recurs without explicit citation of this finding | **loss of evidential continuity** | The exact earlier focal observation loses visible force in later causal documentation; this does not prove forgetting |
| Serial Podiatry structural findings | Persistent structural pathology objectively coexisted with neurological disease | Later notes acknowledge corn/other causes but do not always re-state the full structural history | **partial carry-forward** | Structural and neurological/pain-modulation strands remain coexistent rather than mutually exclusive |
| 2018–2019 Neupro dyskinesia/behavioural effects | Patient-specific dopaminergic adverse-effect susceptibility | Repeatedly cited in later levodopa decisions | **evidential continuity preserved** | Risk evidence remained a durable governing constraint |
| 2021–2022 chorea on low levodopa / Stalevo 75 | Patient-specific levodopa sensitivity | Explicit in 2023–2025 decisions and complaint response | **evidential continuity preserved** | The adverse-effect side of the risk-benefit model shows strong continuity |
| 2022 morning 20–25 minute foot “warm-up” | A reproducible morning temporal phenotype existed | Similar morning pattern becomes clinically actionable in 2025, but the 2022 clue is not expressly cited | **partial carry-forward** | A prior clue becomes retrospectively more salient without becoming proof that an earlier CR trial was indicated |
| March 2023 ten-day medication interruption | Patient perceived no clear Parkinsonian change during an uncontrolled dechallenge | Broader separate-problem belief remains visible; exact dechallenge is not explicitly reconciled with later OFF model | **partial carry-forward** | Discordant evidence survives at perspective level but not as an explicit later treatment-state comparison |
| Later 2023 OFF-related pain/shuffling and Stalevo response | At least part of pain/shuffling was dopaminergically state-sensitive | Re-used in 2024 timing, 2025 onset/CR reasoning, and complaint response | **evidential continuity preserved** | New medication-state evidence materially changed and then persisted in the working model |
| 2023–2024 pregabalin benefit | Foot pain was materially modifiable by pregabalin | Explicitly retained through 2025 reduction/worsening analysis | **evidential continuity preserved** | Non-dopaminergic pain-modulation evidence remained active alongside levodopa evidence |
| 2024 pain relief with persistent toe curling | Pain and toe-curling were partly dissociable | Some separation persists, but the specific dissociation is not central in the later complaint narrative | **local updating without global re-synthesis** | The record contains evidence for multiple symptom domains that are not fully collapsed into one mechanism |
| 2024–2025 Lyrica reduction with pain recurrence | Pregabalin likely contributed materially to prior pain control | Explicitly cited in 2025 management and complaint response | **evidential continuity preserved** | The dechallenge remained an active explanatory variable |
| 2025 Sinemet-onset + CR response | Overnight dopaminergic timing/formulation materially modulated morning pain | Immediately incorporated into clinical and complaint documentation | **evidential continuity preserved** | Strong coverage-specific evidence entered the model rapidly; retrospective reach remains bounded |
| 2017 PRKN/PARK2 suspicion → 2026 confirmation | A specific monogenic hypothesis became testable, then confirmed | Hypothesis remained identifiable and was ultimately confirmed | **evidential continuity preserved** | Shows that some unresolved propositions retained clear longitudinal identity until closure |

### Re-synthesis points identified in Pass 13

1. **July → October 2017:** the foot relationship moved from “Not clear” to a new patient-specific finding that the feet participated in a neurological medication-state change during procyclidine withdrawal.
2. **April 2018:** the withdrawal evidence became more anatomically specific when the treating neurologist recorded rapid return of the outside-left-foot/corn phenomenon.
3. **2018–2022:** adverse-effect observations converted generic caution about dopamine replacement into a patient-specific constraint through Neupro dyskinesia/behavioural effects and later low-dose levodopa/Stalevo chorea.
4. **March → later 2023:** an unresolved and internally discordant severe-pain presentation was re-synthesized after new ON/OFF observations into an explicit working proposition that part of the leg pain/shuffling was OFF-related.
5. **2023–2024:** substantial pregabalin benefit alongside persistent clawing/toe curling separated pain modulation from motor/postural phenomena more clearly.
6. **2025:** severe morning pain, a long overnight medication gap and relief when Sinemet reached the system made timing/formulation a distinct therapeutic variable; the subsequent CR response strongly strengthened the overnight-modulation proposition.
7. **2026:** genetic testing changed PRKN/PARK2 from a long-standing hypothesis into confirmed causation of Parkinsonism and dystonia without automatically closing the separate historical foot-pain generator question.

### Pass 13 synthesis — recurring pattern of local facts losing force in the longitudinal model

The strongest continuity in the record concerns **treatment-risk facts**: dyskinesia, impulse-control/behavioural effects, visual toxicity and later levodopa sensitivity are repeatedly carried forward and visibly govern subsequent prescribing. The record also preserves later **pregabalin** and **levodopa/OFF** response evidence once those propositions become explicit in 2023–2025.

The less consistent continuity concerns the earlier **foot-specific modifiability evidence**. The 2017 withdrawal and 2018 focal outside-left-foot observation entered local neurological management and were documented with increasing specificity, but they are not visibly re-used when severe foot pain re-emerges years later. Similar medication-state relevance is later reconstructed from new 2023 and 2025 observations. The documentary pattern is therefore not that the earlier facts disappeared from the record, but that some facts appear to have **local treatment force without equally visible longitudinal causal force**.

That pattern must remain documentary. It does not establish forgetting, diagnostic error, negligence, or that a different treatment should have been used earlier. It does identify concrete re-synthesis questions for independent experts: when patient-specific treatment-state evidence should alter a working causal model, how discordant natural experiments should be weighted, and when dose, timing, formulation and duration-of-coverage should be tested as separate therapeutic variables.

| RF-069 | 2024–2025 | Pain relief with persistent toe curling, then severe morning pain relieved after medication onset and tested with CR, added dissociating and coverage-specific observations. | The evidential state became more granular; pain, motor phenomenon and coverage cannot be treated as one undifferentiated variable. | F-115, F-116 | A + B | CLIN-002695–CLIN-002710; CLIN-002767–CLIN-002800 |
| RF-070 | Mar 2025 | The complaint response adds a new 2025 explanatory account but does not itself supply new contemporaneous evidence of earlier intentions or knowledge. | Historical claims must be tested against earlier notes rather than counted as fresh corroboration of the past. | F-117 | D — Retrospective reconstruction | CLIN-003239–CLIN-003269 |
| RF-071 | Feb 2026 | Biallelic pathogenic PRKN results are genuinely new confirmatory evidence, not stronger repetition of earlier PARK2 suspicion. | Moves the genetic proposition from hypothesis to established from 2026 while preserving earlier uncertainty. | F-118 | A — New definitive test result | CLIN-003220–CLIN-003229 |
| RF-072 | 2017–2026 | Evidential strength across the record depends on what each later entry adds, not on how often a label appears. | Separates true accumulation of evidence from accumulation of terminology. | F-119 | Supported record-level proposition | F-103–F-118 source set |
| RF-073 | Apr 2017 | The patient's initial sciatica/habit explanation of gait is recorded separately from the GP's observed structural foot pathology and later neurological concern. | Preserves patient belief, clinician observation and later diagnosis as different evidential layers. | F-120 | Established perspective distinction | CLIN-000018–CLIN-000028 |
| RF-074 | Jun 2017 | Patient-reported movement-initiation/dexterity impairment and clinician-observed tone/reflex/gait abnormalities converged sufficiently to trigger urgent neurological assessment while diagnosis remained open. | Shows convergence in concern without retroactively imposing diagnostic agreement. | F-121 | Supported convergence | CLIN-000064–CLIN-000111; CLIN-000190–CLIN-000235; CLIN-000847–CLIN-000884 |
| RF-075 | Jul 2017 | The patient phenomenologically separated leg tension from foot pain/corns, while the neurologist independently recorded the causal relation as “Not clear.” | Patient experience and clinician uncertainty align on non-equivalence without proving separate mechanisms. | F-122 | Established perspective alignment with causal non-closure | CLIN-001142–CLIN-001149 |
| RF-076 | Sep–Oct 2017 | Patient and clinicians were aligned that procyclidine produced major benefit but visual adverse effects constrained use; no contemporaneous shared foot-mechanism explanation is visible. | Demonstrates treatment-effect agreement without causal agreement. | F-123, F-124 | Supported perspective alignment | CLIN-001742–CLIN-001749; CLIN-001770–CLIN-001801; CLIN-001822–CLIN-001839 |
| RF-077 | Apr 2018 | The treating neurologist later recorded rapid return of the outside-left-foot/corn phenomenon during withdrawal, partly converging with the patient's account of the episode. | Corroborates event content while preserving uncertainty about source of the added specificity. | F-125 | Partly corroborated perspective convergence | CLIN-001914–CLIN-001919 |
| RF-078 | 2019 | The patient reported persistent stiffness/insufficient benefit even when the neurologist thought he looked close to normal in clinic; the note itself preserved that he might look worse later. | Prevents subjective and observed states from being treated as mutually exclusive. | F-126 | Observation-window/perspective tension | CLIN-002116–CLIN-002141 |
| RF-079 | 2022 | “Neuralgic” was raised by the patient as a question about morning foot soreness; the note does not visibly convert that question into a clinician-established neuropathy diagnosis. | Prevents patient terminology from being silently reclassified as objective diagnostic evidence. | F-127 | Established perspective distinction | CLIN-002375–CLIN-002399 |
| RF-080 | Mar 2023 | The patient's belief that the severe pain was not Parkinson-related and his ten-day self-withdrawal report coexisted with the GP's concern that an earlier pain episode had improved with better Parkinson control. | The receiving pathway contained genuine causal disagreement before Neurology reassessment. | F-128 | Established perspective divergence | CLIN-002623–CLIN-002689 |
| RF-081 | Later 2023 | Patient-reported timing/state information and clinician OFF interpretation converged sufficiently to change treatment, but not to establish that the patient adopted a single Parkinson-causation model. | Distinguishes convergence on modulation from convergence on generator. | F-129 | Supported partial convergence | CLIN-002422–CLIN-002495; CLIN-002573–CLIN-002583 |
| RF-082 | 2024 | The patient's report that pregabalin relieved pain while toe curling persisted supplied a clinically useful dissociation that the treatment plan preserved. | Patient phenomenology can narrow propositions even without a definitive diagnosis. | F-130 | Supported perspective alignment | CLIN-002695–CLIN-002710 |
| RF-083 | 2025 | The patient's observation of morning relief after Parkinson medication and concern about the overnight gap became an input to the clinician's mixed model and CR trial. | Shows explicit integration of patient temporal experience into a testable management hypothesis without causal overreach. | F-131 | Established perspective integration | CLIN-002767–CLIN-002800 |
| RF-084 | Mar–Apr 2025 | Complaint correspondence expressly records different patient/provider explanatory frames; the clinician acknowledges different perceptions while disputing a simple direct-causation model of pain. | The disagreement is evidence of perspective and communication, not itself proof that either historical causal account is correct. | F-132 | Established retrospective perspective divergence | CLIN-003239–CLIN-003284 |
| RF-085 | Feb 2026 | PRKN confirmation objectively resolves the genetic Parkinsonism/dystonia proposition but does not resolve the historical patient–clinician disagreement about the source of every foot-pain component. | Objective closure at one proposition level cannot be expanded to adjacent perspective disputes. | F-133 | Established diagnostic closure with limited scope | CLIN-003220–CLIN-003229 |

| RF-086 | Jun–Jul 2017 | The record moved from an open “?parkinsonian or other” referral question to a Parkinsonian syndrome sufficiently established to govern treatment, while exact subtype remained open. | Demonstrates operational diagnostic closure without etiological closure. | F-134 | Operational closure | CLIN-000847–CLIN-000884; CLIN-001128–CLIN-001180 |
| RF-087 | Jul 2017 | The same specialist document that operationally establishes Parkinsonism expressly states that the relationship of corns/calluses/burning is “Not clear.” | Closure status must be proposition-specific. | F-135 | Explicit unresolved proposition | CLIN-001119–CLIN-001149 |
| RF-088 | Jul 2017 | A treatment plan was implemented while genetic form, foot mechanism and future levodopa threshold remained unresolved. | Management action does not imply global causal closure. | F-136 | Operational closure / residual uncertainty | CLIN-001153–CLIN-001229 |

| RF-093 | 2017–2025 | Peripheral neuropathy remains conditional or working language in the surviving record; 2025 still says “If there is a degree of peripheral neuropathy here.” | Prevents “neuropathic pain” from silently becoming a proven neuropathy diagnosis. | F-141 | Unresolved proposition | CLIN-001142–CLIN-001145; CLIN-002783–CLIN-002792; CLIN-003240–CLIN-003258 |

| RF-095 | 2022 | A repeatable morning “warm-up” pattern appears before the record explicitly labels later pain/shuffling as OFF-related. | Temporal narrowing precedes later causal/therapeutic interpretation. | F-143 | Narrowed uncertainty | CLIN-002375–CLIN-002399 |

| RF-098 | 2024 | Pregabalin substantially reduced pain while toe curling persisted. | The composite “foot problem” separated into differently responding propositions. | F-146 | Proposition narrowing | CLIN-002695–CLIN-002710 |
| RF-099 | 2025 | Sinemet CR was introduced as a bounded test of overnight coverage with explicit acknowledgement that it might fail; later profound relief supports efficacy more than mechanism. | Therapeutic success and causal closure are separate. | F-147, F-148 | Efficacy supported / mechanism open | CLIN-002767–CLIN-002800; CLIN-003239–CLIN-003269 |
| RF-100 | 2025–2026 | The complaint response still leaves the underlying pain cause open, while the 2026 genetic result definitively closes PRKN causation of Parkinsonism/dystonia only. | Even late in the record, different causal propositions have different closure states. | F-149–F-151 | Mixed closure states | CLIN-003239–CLIN-003269; CLIN-003227–CLIN-003229 |

| RF-101 | 2017–2025 | Treatment responses in the record vary by symptom domain, dose, timing, formulation and adverse-effect burden rather than forming a single binary efficacy history. | Prevents broad labels such as “worked” or “failed” from erasing multi-domain and non-monotonic response. | F-152, F-170 | Established record-level proposition | F-153–F-169 source set |
| RF-102 | Sep–Oct 2017 | Procyclidine initiation and planned withdrawal produced contemporaneous multi-domain improvement followed by deterioration in tension, gait and feet while vision improved off treatment. | Supplies strong within-patient state evidence with an opposite-direction toxicity signal. | F-153, F-154 | Strong natural-experiment evidence | CLIN-001742–CLIN-001749; CLIN-001770–CLIN-001801 |

| RF-106 | 2018–2019 | Neupro produced a non-monotonic dose-response: higher doses caused dyskinesia/behavioural effects, while 10 mg was preferred but felt insufficient. | Gives patient-specific evidence of a narrow dopaminergic therapeutic window. | F-158 | Established non-monotonic response | CLIN-002114–CLIN-002140 |

| RF-109 | 2022 | Low levodopa exposure still produced subtle excess movement and prior Stalevo 75 had produced obvious dyskinesia. | Confirms patient-specific levodopa sensitivity while leaving timing/formulation questions open. | F-161 | Established adverse-effect sensitivity | CLIN-002392–CLIN-002400 |
| RF-110 | 2022 | Morning foot “warm-up” and demand-adjusted Sinemet are temporal treatment-state clues, not a completed measured foot-pain experiment. | Grades evidential strength and avoids over-reading association. | F-162 | Treatment-state clue | CLIN-002375–CLIN-002399 |

| RF-116 | Mar 2025 | The consultant complaint response is a 2025 participant document containing current observations, earlier quotations and backward-looking explanations with different evidential functions. | Prevents the response from being treated as a privileged narrator of 2017–2023. | F-171 | Established record-level fact | CLIN-003230–CLIN-003273 |

| RF-127 | 2023–2025 | Pregabalin was GP-initiated for severe burning/neuropathic-type pain; levodopa-sparing is clearly visible later as a management function. | Separates original initiation rationale from later function. | F-182 | Retrospective compression | CLIN-002623–CLIN-002688; CLIN-002792–CLIN-002800; CLIN-003265 |

| RF-130 | 2017–2025 | The complaint response compresses several operative management phases into one high-level continuous strategy. | Durable risk rationale does not prove an unchanged treatment policy. | F-185 | Retrospective compression | CLIN-003245–CLIN-003268; longitudinal treatment record |
| RF-131 | Apr 2025 | The Quality and Patient Safety letter records the complaint frame and administrative routing but does not independently adjudicate the clinical merits. | Prevents administrative correspondence from being treated as clinical expert determination. | F-186 | Established document-purpose fact | CLIN-003274–CLIN-003287 |
| RF-132 | 2017 wording reproduced 2025 | The 2017 levodopa-deferral quotation is independently established, but the 2025 “in essence” connective interpretation remains retrospective. | Quotation and later synthesis carry different evidential weight despite physical proximity. | F-187 | Mixed evidential layers | CLIN-001601–CLIN-001612; CLIN-003260–CLIN-003264 |
| RF-133 | 2017–2025 | Claim-by-claim audit produces mixed classifications rather than a single verdict on the complaint response. | Preserves supported rationale, over-broad temporal claims, residual uncertainty and expert boundaries simultaneously. | F-188 | Established audit-level finding | F-171–F-187 source set |
| RF-134 | 2017–2018 → later recurrence | Procyclidine withdrawal/rechallenge created new patient-specific evidence that the feet participated in neurological medication-state change; the finding had immediate treatment consequences and remained visible into 2018. | Distinguishes local therapeutic updating from broader longitudinal causal re-synthesis. | F-189 | local updating without global re-synthesis | CLIN-001142–CLIN-001149; CLIN-001770–CLIN-001801; CLIN-001914–CLIN-001919 |
| RF-135 | Apr 2018 → 2022–2025 | The treating neurologist specifically documented rapid return of the outside-left-foot/corn phenomenon during withdrawal, but later recurrent focal foot-pain notes do not expressly cite or weigh that exact finding. | Establishes a documentary gap in visible evidential continuity without proving forgetting. | F-190 | loss of evidential continuity | CLIN-001914–CLIN-001919; later CLIN-002382; CLIN-002442; CLIN-002777–CLIN-002800 |
| RF-136 | 2017–2019 → 2023–2025 | Serial Podiatry examinations repeatedly established persistent structural foot pathology; later Neurology partly retains this strand but often foregrounds neuropathic or Parkinson-modulation language. | Preserves structural pathology as an independent evidential strand. | F-191 | partial carry-forward | CLIN-002864–CLIN-002988; CLIN-003043–CLIN-003107; CLIN-002491; CLIN-003240–CLIN-003244 |
| RF-137 | 2018–2019 → 2025 | Neupro titration converted generic young-onset dopaminergic risk into patient-specific dyskinesia and behavioural/impulsivity evidence that was repeatedly reused later. | Positive control showing strong longitudinal retention of adverse-effect knowledge. | F-192 | evidential continuity preserved | CLIN-002114–CLIN-002161; later CLIN-002342–CLIN-002353; CLIN-003245–CLIN-003268 |
| RF-138 | 2021–2022 → 2025 | Early chorea on low-dose Sinemet and greater dyskinesia on Stalevo 75 created patient-specific levodopa-sensitivity evidence that remained active in later dose/formulation decisions. | Shows continuity of an individualized treatment constraint. | F-193 | evidential continuity preserved | CLIN-002335–CLIN-002353; CLIN-002392–CLIN-002400; CLIN-002789–CLIN-002795 |
| RF-139 | 2022 → 2025 | A reproducible 20–25 minute morning “warm-up” of the feet was documented before the later 2025 overnight-gap/CR formulation model, but the earlier clue is not explicitly cited when that model is developed. | Prior temporal evidence becomes retrospectively more salient without proving an earlier indication. | F-194 | partial carry-forward | CLIN-002382–CLIN-002386; CLIN-002777–CLIN-002800 |
| RF-140 | Mar 2023 → later 2023 | The patient-reported ten-day Parkinson-medication interruption produced discordant dechallenge evidence; the broader separate-problem belief persisted, but the exact withdrawal observation was not explicitly reconciled with the later OFF-related model. | Requires graded weighting of conflicting natural experiments. | F-195 | partial carry-forward | CLIN-002682–CLIN-002684; CLIN-002475–CLIN-002481; CLIN-002573–CLIN-002581 |

# PART III — DECISION-POINT REGISTER

## 5. Decision Points

| ID | Date | What was known at that point | What remained unresolved | New evidence since prior review | Management response | Explicit re-synthesis documented? | Documentary concern raised | Expert question |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| DP-001 | 2017-06-12 | Abnormal gait plus observed movement-initiation difficulty; old records not yet reviewed. | Cause/diagnosis. | Occupational Health doctor independently echoed gait concern. | Neurology referral proceeded before old notes arrived. | Yes — referral threshold changed. | None beyond diagnostic uncertainty. | None needed for the documentary sequence. |
| DP-002 | 2017-06-29 | Increased tone, brisk reflexes, worsening dexterity and work failure; routine Neurology referral pending. | Neurological diagnosis and cause. | Functional deterioration. | Urgent MAU assessment arranged. | Yes — escalation documented. | Diagnostic openness preserved in “?parkinsonian or other”. | Was urgency/breadth of assessment appropriate? |
| DP-003 | Jul 2017 | Young-onset Parkinsonian syndrome strongly suspected/established; structural foot pathology also present; foot relation “Not clear.” | Exact disease form and foot-pain mechanism. | Specialist examination, imaging and DaTscan. | Rasagiline/procyclidine strategy; levodopa/dopamine agonists deferred. | Partial — syndrome synthesized, foot causation remained open. | Operational closure without causal closure. | Was treatment sequencing reasonable on the evidence then available? |
| DP-004 | 2017-09-12 | Parkinsonism plus good response to initial regimen. | Genetic form; later treatment course. | Healy suspected PARK2 and recommended testing; documented dramatic motor response. | Existing low-dose strategy continued; genetic testing question opened. | Partial/unclear in later record. | Follow-through on PARK2 testing not established by this source. | Would confirmation then have altered management? |
| DP-005 | Sep–Oct 2017 | Procyclidine beneficial but causing visual problems. | Meaning of foot changes and best tolerable regimen. | During withdrawal: gait/tension/feet worsened while vision improved. | Reintroduction at lower dose, later increase with monitoring. | Partial — treatment effect acted on; causal foot formulation not expressly closed in cited sources. | New medication-foot evidence entered record. | How should this natural experiment have informed the foot formulation? |
| DP-006 | 2018-04-24 | Treating neurologist now recorded rapid return of outside-left-foot/corn off procyclidine; structural foot issues persisted. | Mechanism of foot symptoms; whether levodopa should be introduced. | Retrospective clinical recording of withdrawal effect plus continuing symptoms. | Continued procyclidine/rasagiline approach and levodopa deferral. | Unclear for the foot-causation proposition. | Earlier “Not clear” foot question not expressly re-closed in cited note. | Did the new evidence warrant different investigation or treatment testing? |
| DP-007 | Late 2018–2019 | Residual symptoms at Neupro 10 mg; higher doses caused dyskinesia/behavioural effects; later-day state reportedly worse. | Best balance of stimulation vs adverse effects. | Dose-dependent adverse effects on dopamine agonist. | Lower dose maintained; levodopa still deferred. | Yes for treatment-risk balance; no clear foot re-synthesis. | Observation representativeness and narrow therapeutic window. | What alternative dopaminergic strategies were reasonable? |
| DP-008 | Sep–Oct 2021 | Patient felt undertreated; ocular pressure/visual concerns made Kemadrin removal desirable. | Tolerable levodopa exposure and later role in pain. | Need to substitute for procyclidine. | First Sinemet introduced and escalated; further increase limited by early chorea. | Yes for treatment strategy. | Later pain rationale not yet visible as initiation rationale. | What dose strategy was reasonable given early dyskinesia? |
| DP-009 | Mar 2023 | Severe burning pain despite high-dose pregabalin; patient reported no difference after 10 days off Parkinson meds; GP recalled prior pain improvement with better Parkinson control. | Pain mechanism and optimal treatment. | Severe recurrence and conflicting state evidence. | Urgent Neurology review requested. | No single synthesis yet; conflict preserved. | Competing evidence required reassessment. | How should the self-withdrawal report and prior response be weighted? |
| DP-010 | Later 2023 | Ongoing pain/shuffling worsened when Sinemet not taken; pregabalin also helping pain; dyskinesia risk known. | Degree of OFF contribution and best timing/dose. | Explicit clinician interpretation of leg pain/shuffling as OFF-related. | Morning Stalevo started; evening levodopa considered. | Yes — OFF-related working proposition documented. | Pain generator still not resolved. | What timing/formulation strategy balanced benefit and dyskinesia risk? |
| DP-011 | 2024 | Pregabalin improved pain but toe curling/evening limitation persisted; dyskinesia remained a concern. | Whether timing/absorption/coverage could improve evenings. | Symptom dissociation. | Evening Sinemet moved before food with fluid. | Partial. | Dose was not the only therapeutic variable. | What is the evidential meaning of response to timing changes? |

# PART V — TREATMENT-RATIONALE TRACE

## 7. Contemporaneous Rationale vs Later-Attributed Rationale

| Treatment | First documented initiator | Contemporaneous reason for initiation | Later reason for continuation/change | Later-attributed historical rationale | Earliest support for later rationale | Tension / consequence |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Procyclidine / Kemadrin | Neurology, Jul 2017 | Gradual symptomatic alternative while delaying levodopa/dopamine agonists; introduced cautiously because of adverse-effect concerns. | Continued because of marked physical benefit; interrupted/reduced because of visual effects; later replaced by Sinemet as ocular concerns increased. | Later records emphasise how strongly it improved movement and how withdrawal brought back foot/gait symptoms. | 2017 contemporaneous emails and 2018 clinician note. | Later evidence strengthens the observed treatment effect but does not change why the drug was originally selected. |
| Levodopa / Sinemet | Parkinson's nurse / Neurology, Sep 2021 | Patient felt undertreated; Kemadrin was being removed because of ocular-pressure/visual concerns and Sinemet was used as replacement dopaminergic therapy. | Dose kept low because of early chorea/dyskinesia; later used for OFF-related symptoms, pain modulation and eventually overnight coverage with CR. | 2025 complaint response says responsiveness was known and that Sinemet had helped pain “for quite some time.” | Specific pain relationship is clearly documented by 2023; the 2021 initiation note does not state pain as the reason for starting. | Later pain role is supported; its reach back to the original 2021 initiation is not established. |
| Pregabalin / Lyrica | GP, early 2023 | Severe burning/neuropathic-type foot/leg pain; started at 25 mg BD and escalated. | Continued/escalated because of reported pain benefit; by 2025 increasing Lyrica was considered as an alternative to further Parkinson-medication dose increase. | 2025 complaint response says it was introduced for neuropathic pain “in part to try to spare further Sinemet increase.” | Levodopa-sparing is explicit as a later management function in the 2025 clinic, not in the located initiation record. | A later management function must not be substituted for the original prescribing rationale. |

# PART VIII — EVIDENTIAL DEVELOPMENT REGISTER

## 10. Proposition Development

Use one row for each important proposition and trace how it changes over time.

| Proposition | First appearance | Initial status | New evidence | Later wording | New evidence or inherited framing? | Was uncertainty expressly closed? | Final documentary status |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Structural/local pathology contributed to foot symptoms | Apr 2017 | Established local findings; causal weight unclear | Repeated callus/HD, clawed toes, pronation, pressure/offloading treatment 2017–2019 | Later records continue focal outside-left-foot pain and structural references | New observation repeatedly added | No, not as sole explanation | Established local pathology; degree of causal contribution unresolved |
| Parkinsonian/dystonic motor state affected the feet | Jul 2017 | Suspected/observed dystonic-looking gait and rigidity | Procyclidine withdrawal effects; clawing/toe curling; later OFF-related pain/shuffling; PRKN dystonia confirmed | Later records increasingly use dystonia/OFF language | New evidence plus some inherited framing | Partial for motor/dystonia component | Strongly supported; exact relation to pain generator unresolved |
| Peripheral neuropathy explained the severe burning pain | Jul 2017 | Possible, explicitly “Not clear” | Burning phenotype; pregabalin response; alcohol-related concern; later normal Fabry-related tests; no definitive neuropathy result located here | “Neuropathic pain” used as working language | Mixed: new phenotype evidence plus working labels | No | Unresolved proposition |
| Neurological medication state modulated the foot symptom complex | Sep–Oct 2017 | Supported by withdrawal deterioration | 2018 clinician confirmation; later OFF-related pain/shuffling; medication-onset relief; CR response | By 2025 Sinemet benefit is described as longstanding | Substantial new evidence over time | Yes for modulation, not mechanism | Established/supportable modulation; mechanism unresolved |
| Pregabalin-responsive pain and toe-curling motor phenomenon were the same process | 2023–2024 | Unclear | In 2024 pain substantially improved while toe curling persisted | Later notes continue both pain and dyskinetic/dystonic motor concerns | New dissociating evidence | No | Evidence supports at least partial dissociation |
| Parkinson's directly generated all foot pain | Jul 2017 | Not established; specialist explicitly kept separate/neuropathic possibilities open | Later OFF-related effects and CR benefit; 2025 complaint response expressly says Parkinson's does not directly cause all pain | Later explanation favors amplification/modulation of pain from whatever cause | New evidence and retrospective interpretation | No | Not established |
| Parkinson's amplified/modulated pain arising from another cause | 2025 complaint response; earlier state-sensitive clues | Explicitly articulated late | Earlier and later medication-state effects are compatible with the model | 2025 response gives this as explanatory account | Retrospective interpretation supported by treatment-response evidence | No mechanism-specific closure | Supported explanatory proposition; historical reach requires caution |
| PRKN caused the Parkinsonism and dystonia | 2017 PARK2 suspicion | Hypothesis | 2026 biallelic pathogenic PRKN confirmation | “due to compound heterozygous mutations in the Parkin gene” | New definitive evidence | Yes from 2026 | Established from 2026 onward; does not automatically resolve all foot-pain mechanisms |

**Pass 8 inheritance control:** A repeated label is counted as new evidential support only where the later entry contains an independent observation, test, treatment-state effect, or a new interpretation grounded in newly observed evidence. Where wording merely becomes more categorical, its evidential status does not automatically increase.

| ID | Evidence-generating event | Newly knowable proposition | Carry-forward result | Documentary classification | Key source |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| EP-001 | 2017 procyclidine withdrawal/rechallenge | Feet/gait participated in neurological medication-state change | Local management + 2018 retention; not explicitly cited at later recurrence | local updating without global re-synthesis | CLIN-001770–CLIN-001801; CLIN-001914–CLIN-001919 |

| EP-004 | Neupro titration and adverse effects | Individual dyskinesia/behavioural susceptibility at higher dopamine-agonist exposure | Repeatedly active later | evidential continuity preserved | CLIN-002114–CLIN-002161 |
| EP-005 | Low-dose Sinemet/Stalevo dyskinesia | Individual levodopa sensitivity | Repeatedly active later | evidential continuity preserved | CLIN-002335–CLIN-002353; CLIN-002392–CLIN-002400 |

| EP-007 | March 2023 ten-day self-withdrawal | Patient perceived no obvious Parkinsonian change during uncontrolled dechallenge | Broader separate-problem view persisted; exact test not reconciled | partial carry-forward | CLIN-002682–CLIN-002684 |
| EP-008 | Later 2023 OFF/Stalevo comparisons | Part of pain/shuffling was dopaminergically state-sensitive | Explicitly reused 2024–2025 | evidential continuity preserved | CLIN-002453–CLIN-002474; CLIN-002573–CLIN-002583 |
| EP-009 | Pregabalin escalation/benefit | Pain materially but incompletely modifiable by pregabalin | Explicitly retained through 2025 | evidential continuity preserved | CLIN-002623–CLIN-002688; CLIN-002447–CLIN-002491 |
| EP-010 | 2024 pain relief with persistent toe curling | Pain and motor/postural phenomena partly dissociable | Locally recognized; not fully developed later | local updating without global re-synthesis | CLIN-002695–CLIN-002710 |

| EP-013 | PARK2/PRKN specialist hypothesis → genetic test | Specific monogenic hypothesis became confirmed etiology | Hypothesis remained identifiable until closure | evidential continuity preserved | CLIN-001520–CLIN-001525; CLIN-001742–CLIN-001749; CLIN-003227–CLIN-003229 |

**Pass 13 continuity control:** A later note need not repeat every earlier fact. “Loss of evidential continuity” here means only that the earlier fact is no longer visibly active in the surviving working model when the same or closely related proposition recurs. It does not establish mental forgetting, clinical ignorance, negligence, or that a different action was required.

| 2017-09-12 | Treatment response / PARK2 | Reported dramatic motor improvement on procyclidine plus rasagiline. | Healy recorded the response and interpreted the syndrome as probably PARK II, with low-dose dopamine-replacement benefit and dyskinesia risk. | Agreed major motor benefit had occurred. | Genetic/therapeutic meaning came from clinician interpretation, not patient report. | PRKN was genetically confirmed in 2026. | CLIN-001742–CLIN-001749 |

| 2018-04-24 | Meaning of 2017 withdrawal | Patient history underlying the note included worsening off procyclidine. | Treating neurologist recorded the first rapidly changing feature as return of the outside-left-foot/corn phenomenon and greater shiftiness/unrelaxed state. | Partial convergence on the event and anatomical site. | Exact source of the added specificity is not visible; mechanism still not stated. | Later levodopa/OFF evidence strengthened medication-state modulation. | CLIN-001914–CLIN-001925 |

# PART X — RETROSPECTIVE-CLAIM AUDIT

## 12. Later Claims About Earlier Knowledge or Strategy

| Later date | Later claim | Period the claim reaches back to | Earliest contemporaneous support located | Contrary / qualifying contemporaneous evidence | Classification | Consequence |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2025-03-25 | “We have had a clear understanding for quite some time that Sinemet does help his pain.” | Potentially reaches back to Sinemet introduction in 2021. | By 2023 the record expressly links Stalevo/Sinemet state with pain and OFF-related leg pain/shuffling. | The 2021 first-Sinemet note states undertreatment and replacement of Kemadrin for ocular reasons; it does not state foot pain as the initiation rationale. | Partly supported / Retrospective reconstruction | Safe temporal reach is clear by 2023; earlier reach is not established on the cited record. |
| 2025-03-25 | Pregabalin “was introduced … in part to try to spare further Sinemet increase.” | Reaches back to early 2023 initiation. | In 2025 the clinician explicitly suggests increasing Lyrica to avoid increasing Parkinson medication. | The 2023 initiation record says the GP started Lyrica at 25 mg BD for severe burning/neuropathic-type pain. | Retrospective reconstruction | May accurately describe a later management function, but does not prove the original initiation rationale. |
| 2025-03-25 | “We have known it would be Sinemet or other dopamine agonist responsive.” | Reaches back to 2017. | 2017 record predicts possible strong levodopa response; Healy suspected PARK2 and described expected low-dose dopamine-replacement responsiveness; later actual responses support the prediction. | Contemporaneous 2017 wording is probabilistic (“possible”), not a direct demonstrated response to levodopa. | Partly supported / Retrospective reconstruction | Later outcome strengthens the earlier expectation but should not upgrade its original certainty. |
| 2025-03-25 | Slow/cautious Sinemet escalation reflected concern about dyskinesia and other dopaminergic risks in a young patient. | Reaches back to 2017 and subsequent dopaminergic decisions. | Explicit 2017 deferral rationale; 2019 dopamine-agonist dyskinesia/behavioural effects; 2021 early chorea on Sinemet. | Same 2017 record also contemplated that levodopa might work very well and might later be regretted as delayed. | Supported | Rationale is historically documented; whether the duration/intensity of caution was clinically reasonable remains an expert question. |
| 2025-03-25 | “From the very outset the majority of everything that has been done has been about [the] Parkinson's.” | Reaches back to 2017. | Neurological investigation, diagnosis and treatment dominated much of the management from Jul 2017 onward. | The same 2017 specialist record left the relationship of corns/calluses/burning to the neurological syndrome “Not clear,” and Podiatry treated structural pathology separately. | Partly supported | Broad management focus cannot be converted into proof that the foot-pain cause was understood as Parkinsonian from the outset. |
| 2026-02-04 | PRKN/Parkin result confirmed the genetic form “as predicted.” | Reaches back to 2017 PARK2 suspicion. | Healy in Sep 2017: “probably has Park II”; Galway record also raised PRKN/PARK2. | Other genetic and Parkinson-plus possibilities remained open in 2017; testing was not then confirmatory. | Supported | Confirms accuracy of the earlier hypothesis without back-projecting confirmation. |
| Present retrospective account | Outside-left-foot focal pain was the first pain to return during procyclidine withdrawal and persisted until 2025. | Reaches back to Sep–Oct 2017. | Apr 2018 Neurology note records the “first thing” changing rapidly was return of the outside-left-foot/corn phenomenon. | Direct Oct 2017 email records restless feet and gait deterioration but does not itself specify focal pain as the first symptom or prove uninterrupted persistence to 2025. | Partly supported / Retrospective reconstruction | Exact sequence has corroboration but remains more specific than the surviving contemporaneous patient email. |


| 2025-03-25 | “Whatever the underlying cause” / Parkinson's does not directly “cause all pain” but can magnify pain. | Reaches back across the pain history. | Earlier 2018 note already describes magnified pain experience in Parkinson's; later 2023 OFF-related pain formulation provides direct foot/leg support. | Structural pathology and unresolved peripheral-neuropathy question remain; March 2023 self-withdrawal report is discordant. | Supported modulation model / causal non-closure | The response itself does not close the underlying pain generator. |
| 2025-03-25 | “We are not blaming the alcohol to the exclusion of the consideration of any other cause.” | Reaches back to periods where alcohol/neuropathy were discussed. | Earlier records consider Parkinsonian, structural, neuropathic and later Fabry/other possibilities. | The complaint alleges disproportionate alcohol framing; mere presence of alternatives does not establish their practical weighting. | Partly supported / Weighting unresolved | Can support non-exclusivity, not resolve anchoring or management-weight questions. |
| 2025-03-25 | “I have always completely accepted that I may be wrong.” | Potentially reaches across the entire treating relationship. | 2023 record documents diagnostic openness, Fabry testing, and attempt to repeat DaTscan despite clinician belief in Parkinson's. | Continuous attitude across every earlier decision cannot be independently demonstrated from the surviving record. | Partly supported / Retrospective generalisation | Safe direct support is strongest for 2023 onward; “always” remains broader than shown. |
| 2025-03-25 | Early Healy second opinion demonstrates specialist review. | Reaches back to Sep 2017. | Healy letter directly confirms second opinion, probable PARK II and treatment/genetic advice. | Surviving letter does not show that a specific foot-pain causal question was referred or resolved. | Supported fact / Scope-limited inference | Specialist review cannot be expanded beyond its documented task. |
| 2025-03-25 | Recent Sinemet CR escalation “has provided profound relief currently.” | Current March 2025 response to treatment. | Pre-response clinic note documents bedtime CR plan for overnight coverage. | Response records uncertainty whether the patient's independent start was the same as the consultant's prescription or another initiation. | Strongly supported current outcome / provenance qualified | Therapeutic efficacy can be accepted without inventing exact initiation chronology. |
| 2025-03-25 | Prior Sinemet use since 2021 answers why the successful possibility was not explored earlier. | Reaches back to Sep 2021. | Sinemet exposure is documented from 2021, with later demand-based and OFF-related use. | Controlled-release overnight coverage is a distinct variable first explicitly documented in 2025; drug exposure does not equal prior formulation/coverage trial. | Factually supported but incomplete response to the narrower question | Requires therapeutic-variable disaggregation and expert judgment on when coverage became indicated. |

| 2025-04-15 | QPS summary states the patient believes anchoring/alcohol emphasis led to prolonged preventable pain and notes Sinemet CR effectiveness. | Complaint history through 2025. | It accurately records the complaint frame and current reported CR benefit. | It is an administrative complaint letter, not an independent specialist determination of causation, preventability or standard of care. | Administrative summary, not clinical adjudication | Use as evidence of complaint framing and routing only. |

# PART XI — CONTRADICTIONS AND DOCUMENTARY TENSIONS

## 13. Tension Register

A tension should be preserved when the record does not justify choosing one account over another.

| ID | Proposition A | Proposition B | Can both be true? | Possible explanation | Does the record resolve it? | Significance |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| T-001 | July 2017 structured field: “Primary Diagnosis: Parkinsons Disease.” | Same specialist episode retains atypical differential language and says the relation of corns/calluses/burning to the neurological syndrome is “Not clear.” | Yes | Operational diagnostic coding can coexist with unresolved subtype/causal questions. | Partial | A categorical field must not be made to carry more certainty than the surrounding narrative. |
| T-002 | Podiatry form is stamped received 10 Apr 2017. | Same physical page contains “PARKINSONS” and neurological/gait handwriting that may have been added later. | Yes | Layered annotations on a reused paper form. | No exact annotation date | Same-page placement cannot prove same-date knowledge. |
| T-003 | Podiatry appointment letters bear a 14 Aug 2026 letter date. | The appointments listed are in 2017–2019 and contemporaneous treatment notes independently document those visits. | Yes | Later system regeneration/export of historical appointment letters. | Partially | Event dates are supported; original contemporaneous correspondence is not established by the regenerated pages alone. |
| T-004 | The 2025 complaint response presents 2017 quotations within a continuous retrospective strategy. | Intervening notes document evolving rationales, treatment functions and symptom interpretations. | Possibly | A later summary can accurately capture one durable theme while compressing distinct management phases. | Partial | Continuity must be demonstrated from intervening contemporaneous records, not inferred from quotation layout. |
| T-005 | Repeated later use of Parkinson-linked or neuropathic terminology can make a proposition look increasingly corroborated. | Some later entries add no clearly visible new causal examination/test, while other entries do add genuinely new treatment-state or symptom-pattern evidence. | Yes | The same label can be carried forward across changing evidential states. | Partial | Frequency of wording must be separated from evidential independence. |
| T-006 | The 2017–2018 procyclidine episode is described in contemporaneous patient material and again in later clinician material. | The later clinician account is more specific about the outside-left-foot/corn sequence. | Yes | Later documentation may add specificity to one earlier event without creating a second independent natural experiment. | No exact source of the added specificity is shown | Corroboration should be graded without double-counting the event. |
| T-007 | In 2019 the patient reported persistent stiffness and insufficient benefit on Neupro 10 mg. | The neurologist observed him looking near-normal/minimally hyperkinetic during the clinic visit. | Yes | Medication state and time of day can make a clinic sample unrepresentative; the note itself records that he would look worse later. | Partial | Subjective impairment and observed examination should both be preserved rather than using one to negate the other. |
| T-008 | In March 2023 the patient believed the severe foot pain was not Parkinson-related and reported no perceived Parkinsonian difference during ten days off medication. | Later 2023 and 2025 records describe pain/shuffling varying with Sinemet/OFF state and improvement once Parkinson medication reached the system. | Yes | The self-withdrawal may have sampled a different symptom dimension, been confounded, or reflected an evolving/non-monotonic state relationship. | No unique explanation | The patient's earlier belief remains a valid perspective fact even though later evidence strengthened medication-state modulation. |
| T-009 | The 2025 complaint frame emphasizes possible diagnostic anchoring on alcohol/peripheral neuropathy and prolonged pain. | The consultant's response says management was framed in a broader Parkinson context and that Parkinson's magnifies pain from other causes. | Possibly | Different participants can retrospectively organize the same record around different salient features and experiences. | No | Complaint and response must each be tested against contemporaneous evidence; neither resolves the history by assertion. |

| T-010 | 2019 Podiatry form records “No neuropathy.” | 2017 specialist considered possible secondary peripheral neuropathy; 2023–2025 records repeatedly use neuropathic language and 2025 still says “If there is a degree of peripheral neuropathy here.” | Yes | The 2019 tick may be a scope-limited Podiatry risk-status field rather than causal neurological closure. | No | The record does not show that neuropathy was definitively established or excluded by that form. |
| T-011 | 2025 complaint response refers to “idiopathic Parkinson's disease.” | 2017 records repeatedly raised recessive/PRKN disease and 2026 confirmed biallelic pathogenic Parkin mutations causing Parkinsonism/dystonia. | Yes, retrospectively | The 2025 label may reflect inherited clinical shorthand rather than definitive etiological closure. | Yes for genetic etiology in 2026 | The later genetic result changes the etiological classification but does not by itself resolve foot-pain mechanism. |
| T-012 | Management repeatedly proceeded as if sufficient working explanations existed. | Several key causal propositions remained expressly or conditionally open, including foot-pain generator and peripheral neuropathy. | Apparent rather than necessary contradiction | Operational closure and causal closure are different states. | Yes as a documentary explanation | Acting under uncertainty is compatible with causal non-closure; whether the remaining uncertainty was adequately managed is an expert question. |

| T-013 | March 2023 pregabalin was described in one referral as only just controlling symptoms at high dose. | Two days later the nurse recorded debilitating pain despite 600 mg/day. | Yes | Symptom intensity, dose timing and source timepoint may have changed rapidly; the response may genuinely have been unstable. | No | Classify as variable/non-monotonic response rather than forcing one account to cancel the other. |
| T-014 | Higher dopaminergic exposure repeatedly improved movement or functional capacity. | The same or higher exposure also produced dyskinesia/chorea and, with Neupro, behavioural/impulsivity effects. | Yes, therapeutically | Benefit and harm can rise together across a narrow therapeutic window. | Yes as treatment tradeoff, not as optimal-dose conclusion | “Responsive” does not mean “more is better.” |
| T-015 | Pregabalin substantially relieved pain in 2023–2024. | Clawing/toe curling and evening movement limitation persisted. | Yes | Pain and motor/postural manifestations are at least partly dissociable even within the same anatomical syndrome. | Partial | Response to one drug cannot be used to assign one mechanism to all foot manifestations. |
| T-016 | Complaint response says Sinemet pain benefit had been clearly understood “for quite some time.” | The 2021 first-Sinemet note does not identify foot pain as the reason for initiation; explicit pain/OFF linkage is clear by 2023. | Yes | Understanding may have developed after initiation. | Partially | Temporal reach must be bounded rather than treated as all-or-nothing. |
| T-017 | Complaint response says pregabalin was introduced partly to spare Sinemet increase. | 2023 initiation record identifies GP-originated pregabalin for severe burning/neuropathic-type pain. | Yes | A later management function can differ from original initiation rationale. | Yes as documentary explanation | Do not merge initiation, continuation and later function. |
| T-018 | Complaint response presents dopaminergic caution as a continuous strategy. | Contemporaneous record shows multiple different operative phases and later timing/formulation changes. | Yes | One enduring risk constraint can persist through changing strategies. | Yes | Historical reasonableness must be assessed phase by phase. |
